is a zero-mono. By assumption, there is precisely one zero-monic
Y\to I
, whence
e^{\dagger}
must equal
\top_{K^{\perp},I}
.
∎

Proposition 3.81
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with dagger kernels such that every object of
\mathbf{R}
has precisely one zero-monic effect. Let
X
be an object of
\mathbf{R}
. Then
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
and
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
are ortho-isomorphic complete orthomodular lattices if the latter is equipped with an orthocomplementation
r\mapsto\neg r
with

\neg r=\bigvee\{s\in R(X,I):r\perp s\},

where the orthogonality relation
\perp
on
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
is given by
r\perp s
if and only if
r\circ s^{\dagger}=0_{I}
. The ortho-isomorphism

\mathbf{R}(X,I)\to\mathrm{KSub}(X)
is given by
r\mapsto[\ker(r)_{\perp}]
.

Proof.

Recall Proposition
2.59
which states that
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
is an orthomodular lattice.
We claim that the map
\varphi:\mathbf{R}(X,I)\to\mathrm{KSub}(X)
,
r\mapsto[\ker(r)_{\perp}]
is an order isomorphism such that
\varphi(\neg r)=\neg\varphi(r)
for each
r\in\mathbf{R}(X,I)
. Since
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
is an orthomodular lattice, it then follows that
r\mapsto\neg r
defines an orthocomplementation on
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
such that
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
is an orthomodular lattice. Completeness of
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
follows since
\mathbf{R}
is a quantaloid. Note that once the ortho-isomorphism between
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
and
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
is established, completeness of the former implies completeness of the latter.

In order to show that
\varphi
is an ortho-isomorphism, we first check that
\varphi
is monotone. So let
r,s:X\to I
, and let
K_{r}
and
K_{s}
be the domains of
\ker(r)
and
\ker(s)
, respectively. Assume that
r\leq s
, then
r\circ\ker(s)\leq s\circ\ker(s)=0_{K_{s},I}
, which by Lemma
2.43
forces
r\circ\ker(s)=0
. It follows from the universal property of dagger kernels that there must be some
a:K_{s}\to K_{r}
such that
\ker(s)=\ker(r)\circ a
, hence
[\ker(s)]\leq[\ker(r)]
in
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
, implying
\varphi(r)=[\ker(r)_{\perp}]\leq[\ker(s)_{\perp}]=\varphi(s)
. Next, we show that
\varphi
is an order embedding. So assume that
\varphi(r)\leq\varphi(s)
, i.e.,
[\ker(r)_{\perp}]\leq[\ker(s)_{\perp}]
. In other words,
\ker(r)_{\perp}=\ker(s)_{\perp}\circ a
for some morphism
a:K_{r}^{\perp}\to K_{s}^{\perp}
, which is necessarily a dagger mono, see the discussion below Definition
2.55
. Using Lemma
3.80
, we obtain
r=\top_{K_{r}^{\dagger},I}\circ\ker(r)_{\perp}^{\dagger}=\top_{K_{r}^{\dagger},I}\circ a^{\dagger}\circ\ker(s)_{\perp}^{\dagger}\leq\top_{K_{s}^{\perp},I}\circ\ker(s)_{\perp}^{\dagger}=s
, so
\varphi
is indeed an order embedding. In order to show that it is an order isomorphism, we only have to show it is surjective. So let
k:K\to I
in
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
, and let
r=T_{K,I}\circ k^{\dagger}
. Now,
\top_{K,I}
is a zero-mono by Lemma
3.79
. Moreover, we have
\ker(m\circ f)=\ker(f)
for each morphism
f
and each zero-mono
m
by
[
5
, Lemma 4.2]
. Hence,
\ker(r)=\ker(T_{K,I}\circ k^{\dagger})=\ker(k^{\dagger})=k_{\perp}
, which implies
\varphi(r)=[\ker(r)_{\perp}]=[k_{\perp\perp}]=[k]
.

Finally, for arbitrary
r:X\to I
, we have
\neg r=\top_{K,I}\circ(\ker r)^{\dagger}
by Lemma
3.78
. Again using that
\top_{K,I}
is a zero-mono, we obtain
\varphi(\neg r)=[\ker(\neg r)_{\perp}]=\neg[\ker(\neg r)]=\neg[\ker(\top_{K,I}\circ(\ker r)^{\dagger})]=\neg[\ker(\ker r)^{\dagger}]=\neg[(\ker r)_{\perp}]=\neg\varphi(r)
. Finally, it follows from (a) of Lemma
3.78
that
\perp
is the associated orthogonality relation of the orthocomplementation
\neg
on
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
.
∎

We note that we never assumed our categories to be well powered, so a priori, there is no guaranty that
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
in
\mathbf{R}
is a set. However, since
\mathbf{R}
is a quantaloid, it is locally small, and the theorem above establishes a bijection between
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
and
\mathrm{KSub}(X)
, which assures that the latter is indeed a set.

Corollary 3.82
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with dagger kernels such that every object
\mathbf{R}
has precisely one zero-monic effect. Then the set
\mathbf{R}(I,X)
of states on any object
X
is a complete orthomodular lattice.

3.3.2
Homsets

Since
\mathrm{Tr}(s)=s
for any scalar
s
in a dagger compact category (cf. Proposition
2.13
), it follows that the definition of
\perp
and
\neg
in the next theorem generalizes the definition of
\perp
and
\neg
on sets of effects in Proposition
3.81
.

Theorem 3.83
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a nondegenerate dagger compact quantaloid with dagger kernels such that every object has precisely one zero-monic effect. Then for any two objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathbf{R}
, the homset
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)
is a complete orthomodular lattice with orthocomplementation
r\mapsto\neg r
given by
\neg r=\bigvee\{s\in\mathbf{R}(X,Y):r\perp s\}
, where the orthogonality relation
\perp
on
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)
is given by
r\perp s
if and only if
\mathrm{Tr}(r\circ s^{\dagger})=0_{I}
. Moreover, the map
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)\to\mathbf{R}(X\otimes Y^{*},I)
,
r\mapsto\llcorner r\lrcorner
is an ortho-isomorphism.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
3.81
that
\mathbf{R}(X\otimes Y^{*},I)
is a complete orthomodular lattice. We consider the order isomorphism
\llcorner-\lrcorner:\mathbf{R}(X,Y)\to\mathbf{R}(X\otimes Y^{*},I)
,
r\mapsto\llcorner r\lrcorner=\epsilon_{Y}\circ(r\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})
from Lemma
3.67
. Then for
r,s:X\to Y
, we find

\mathrm{Tr}(r\circ s^{\dagger})=\epsilon_{Y}\circ((r\circ s^{\dagger})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ\epsilon_{Y}^{\dagger}=\epsilon_{Y}\circ(r\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(s^{\dagger}\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ\epsilon_{Y}^{\dagger}=\epsilon_{Y}\circ(r\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(\epsilon_{Y}\circ(s\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}))^{\dagger}=\llcorner r\lrcorner\circ\llcorner s\lrcorner^{\dagger}
. It follows that
r\perp s
if and only if
\mathrm{Tr}(r\circ s^{\dagger})=0_{I}
if and only if
\llcorner r\lrcorner\circ\llcorner s\lrcorner^{\dagger}=0_{I}
.
Hence, using that
\llcorner-\lrcorner
is an order isomorphism, we obtain

\displaystyle\llcorner\neg r\lrcorner

\displaystyle=\raisebox{-3.00003pt}{$\llcorner$}\bigvee\{s\in\mathbf{R}(X,Y):r\perp s\}\raisebox{-3.00003pt}{$\lrcorner$}=\bigvee\{\llcorner s\lrcorner:s\in\mathbf{R}(X,Y),r\perp s\}

\displaystyle=\bigvee\{\llcorner s\lrcorner:s\in\mathbf{R}(X,Y),\llcorner r\lrcorner\circ\llcorner s\lrcorner^{\dagger}=0_{I}\}=\bigvee\{t\in\mathbf{R}(X\otimes Y^{*},I):\llcorner r\lrcorner\circ t^{\dagger}=0_{I}\}

\displaystyle=\bigvee\{t\in R(X\otimes Y^{*},\llcorner r\lrcorner\perp t\}=\neg\phantom{.}{\llcorner r\lrcorner}.

So,
\llcorner-\lrcorner
preserves the orthocomplementation, hence it is an ortho-isomorphism. It follows that
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)
inherits the structure of a complete orthomodular lattice from
\mathbf{R}(X\otimes Y^{*},I)
. It remains to be shown that
\perp
is the associated orthogonality relation of the orthocomplementation
\neg
on
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)
, but this follows directly from the result that
r\mapsto\llcorner r\lrcorner
is an ortho-isomorphism with respect to
\neg
, and our previous calculation that
r\perp s
if and only if
\llcorner r\lrcorner\circ\llcorner s\lrcorner^{\dagger}=0_{I}
, which is equivalent to
\llcorner r\lrcorner\perp\llcorner s\lrcorner
.
∎

Corollary 3.84
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a dagger compact category with dagger kernels, all small dagger biproducts, with precisely two scalars, and such that every object has precisely one zero-monic effect. Then
\mathbf{R}
is a dagger compact quantaloid such that every homset
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)
is a complete orthomodular lattice with orthogonality relation
r\perp s
if and only if
\mathrm{Tr}(r\circ s^{\dagger})=0_{I}
and orthocomplementation
\neg r=\bigvee\{s\in\mathbf{R}(X,Y):r\perp s\}
.

Proof.

Combine Theorems
3.74
and
3.83
.
∎

3.4
The embedding of
\mathbf{Rel}

In this section, we show that
\mathbf{Rel}
can be embedded into categories
\mathbf{R}
with small biproducts. For
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{qRel}
, this embedding is of importance, since it allows us to embed the standard universe of discourse of ordinary mathematics into our proposed universe of discourse of (discrete) quantum mathematics.

Definition 3.85
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a symmetric monoidal category with small biproducts such that
I\ncong 0
. For each set
A
define
`A:=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}I
, and for each
\alpha\in A
, we denote by
i_{\alpha}
and
p_{\alpha}
the canonical injection of
I
into the
\alpha
-th factor of
`A
and the projection of
`A
on the
\alpha
-th factor, respectively. Furthermore, for each binary relation
r:A\to B
let
`r:`A\to`B
be the morphism whose matrix element
(`r)_{\alpha,\beta}
is given by

(`r)_{\alpha,\beta}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}_{I},&(\alpha,\beta)\in r,\\
0_{I},&(\alpha,\beta)\notin r,\end{cases}

for each
\alpha\in A
and each
\beta\in B
.

Lemma 3.86
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a symmetric monoidal category with small biproducts such that
\mathrm{id}_{I}\neq 0_{I}
.
Then the assignment
A\mapsto`A
on sets and
r\mapsto`r
on binary relations defines a faithful functor
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
that is full if
\mathbf{R}
has precisely two scalars.

Proof.

For each set
A
, and each
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in A
, we have

(`\mathrm{id}_{A})_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}_{I},&\alpha=\alpha^{\prime},\\
0_{I},&\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime},\end{cases},

so
(`\mathrm{id}_{A})_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}=\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
, which, via Lemma
2.28
we recognize as the
(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
-matrix entry of the identity on
`A
.
Let
r:A\to B
and
s:B\to C
be binary relations between sets. Let
\alpha\in A
and
\gamma\in C
. Then
(`(s\circ r))_{\alpha,\gamma}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if
(\alpha,\gamma)\in(s\circ r)
if and only if there is some
\beta\in B
such that
(\alpha,\beta)\in r
and
(\beta,\gamma)\in s
if and only if there is some
\beta\in B
such that
(`r)_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
and
(`s)_{\beta,\gamma}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
.

On the other hand, using Lemma
2.27
, we have
(`s\circ`r)_{\alpha,\gamma}=\sum_{\beta\in B}s_{\beta,\gamma}\circ r_{\alpha,\beta}
, hence
(`s\circ`r)_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if there is some
\beta\in B
such that
s_{\beta,\gamma}=\mathrm{id}_{I}=r_{\alpha,\beta}
.
Hence,
(`s\circ`r)_{\alpha,\gamma}=(`(s\circ r))_{\alpha,\gamma}
for each
\alpha\in A
and each
\gamma\in C
, whence
`s\circ`r=`(s\circ r)
by Lemma
2.25
. We conclude that
`(-)
is functorial. If
r,s:A\to B
are two binary relations such that
`r=`s
, then
(`r)_{\alpha,\beta}=(`s)_{\alpha,\beta}
for each
\alpha\in A
and each
\beta\in B
, hence
(\alpha,\beta)\in r
if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)\in s
for each
\alpha\in A
and each
\beta\in B
, whence
r=s
. So
`(-)
is faithful. Finally if
\mathbf{C}(I,I)=\{\mathrm{id}_{I},0_{I}\}
, and
f:`A\to`B
is a morphism in
\mathbf{C}
for sets
A
and
B
, then Lemma
2.25
assures that
f=(f_{\alpha,\beta})_{\alpha\in A,\beta\in B}
for some
f_{\alpha,\beta}:I\to I
. We define the binary relation
r:A\to B
by
(\alpha,\beta)\in r
if and only if
f_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
. It now clearly follows that
(`r)_{\alpha,\beta}=f_{\alpha,\beta}
for each
\alpha\in A
and each
\beta\in B
, which implies
`r=f
by Lemma
2.25
.
∎

Lemma 3.87
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with small dagger biproducts. Then, for each two sets
A
and
B
, we have
`\top_{A,B}=\top_{`A,`B}
.

Proof.

Since
(\alpha,\beta)\in\top_{A,B}
for each
\alpha\in A
and each
\beta\in B
, we have
(`\top_{A,B})_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}=\top_{I}
, where the last equality follows because
\mathbf{R}
is assumed to be affine. It follows now from Proposition
2.49
that
`\top_{A,B}=\top_{`A,`B}
.
∎

Proposition 3.88
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with small dagger biproducts. Then
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
a homomorphism of dagger quantaloids that preserves all dagger biproducts.

Proof.

We first show that
`(r^{\dagger})=(`r)^{\dagger}
for a binary relation
r:A\to B
.
Let
\alpha\in A
and
\beta\in B
. Since
`(-)
is faithful by Lemma
3.86
, we have
(`r)_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)\in r
, and
(`(r^{\dagger}))_{\beta,\alpha}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if
(\beta,\alpha)\in r^{\dagger}
.

Using Proposition
2.29
, we obtain
((`r)^{\dagger})_{\beta,\alpha}=((`r)_{\alpha,\beta})^{\dagger}
.
Hence,
((`r)^{\dagger})_{\beta,\alpha}\in\{\mathrm{id}_{I},0_{I}\}
and
((`r)^{\dagger})_{\beta,\alpha}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if
((`r)_{\alpha,\beta})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if
(`r)_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)\in r
if and only if
(\beta,\alpha)\in r^{\dagger}
if and only if
(`(r^{\dagger}))_{\beta,\alpha}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
. Thus
(`(r^{\dagger}))_{\beta,\alpha}=((`r)^{\dagger})_{\beta,\alpha}
, whence, using Lemma
2.25
, we obtain
`(r^{\dagger})=(`r)^{\dagger}
.

We proceed by showing that
`(-)

is a homomorphism of quantaloids, where we will use that
`(-)
is faithful (cf. Lemma
3.86
. Let
A
and
B
be sets, and let
(r_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in C}
be a set-indexed family of binary relations
A\to B
. Fix
\alpha\in A
and
\beta\in B
. Then
\left(`\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}r_{\gamma}\right)_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)\in\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}r_{\gamma}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in C}r_{\gamma}
if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)\in r_{\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in C
if and only if
(`r_{\gamma})_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
for some
\gamma\in C
. Now, since
(`r_{\gamma})_{\alpha,\beta}\in\{0_{I},\mathrm{id}_{I}\}
and
0_{I}<\mathrm{id}_{I}
by Lemma
2.43
and by assumption that
\mathbf{R}
has at least two scalars, we have
(`r_{\gamma})_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
for some
\gamma\in C
if and only if
\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}(`r_{\gamma})_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{I}
, which is equivalent to
\left(\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}`r_{\gamma}\right)_{\alpha,\beta}
by Proposition
2.49
. Thus
\left(`\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}r_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha,\beta}=\left(\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}`r_{\gamma}\right)_{\alpha,\beta}
, and since
\alpha\in A
and
\beta\in B
are arbitrary, it follows from Lemma
2.25
that
`\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}r_{\alpha}=\bigvee_{\gamma\in C}`r_{\gamma}
. Thus
`(-)
is a homomorphism of quantaloids.
It now immediately follows from Proposition
2.50
that
`(-)
preserves dagger biproducts.
∎

Proposition 3.89
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with all small dagger biproducts. Then the functor
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is dagger strong symmetric monoidal (cf. Definition
2.10
). Here, the coherence morphisms
\varphi:`1\to I
and
\varphi_{A,B}:`A\otimes`B\to`(A\times B)
for each two sets
A
and
B
are given by the identity
\mathrm{id}_{I}
on
I
, and
\varphi_{A,B}=\langle\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\alpha}\otimes p_{\beta})\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B}
, where
p_{\alpha}:`A\to I
and
p_{\beta}:`B\to I
are the canonical projections on the
\alpha
-th and
\beta
-th factor of
`A
and
`B
, respectively.

Proof.

We denote the monoidal unit of
\mathbf{Rel}
by
1
, which is a singleton, say
1=\{*\}
. Hence, we have
`1=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\{*\}}I=I
, so indeed
\varphi
can be taken to be the identity on
I
, which is clearly a dagger isomorphisms.
We have
`A\otimes`B=\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}I\right)\otimes\left(\bigoplus_{\beta\in B}I\right)
. Write
i_{\alpha}:=p_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
and
i_{\beta}:=p_{\beta}^{\dagger}
for
\alpha\in A
and
\beta\in B.

Using Corollaries
2.32
and
2.23
, one easily finds
\varphi_{A,B}^{\dagger}\circ\varphi_{A,B}=\mathrm{id}_{`A\otimes`B}
, whereas it is straightforward to find
(\varphi_{A,B}\circ\varphi_{A,B}^{\dagger})_{((\alpha,\beta),(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}=\delta_{(\alpha,\beta),(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}
.
Also straightforward but tedious is the naturality of
\varphi_{A,B}
in
A
and
B
.
To show that the coherence morphisms satisfy the coherence conditions for a monoidal functor, we need to show that

\displaystyle\varphi_{B,C\times D}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{`B}\otimes\varphi_{C,D})\circ\alpha_{`B,`C,`D}

\displaystyle=`\alpha_{B,C,D}\circ\varphi_{B\times C,D}\circ(\varphi_{B,C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{`D})

(4)

\displaystyle`\lambda_{C}\circ\varphi_{1,C}\circ(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{`C})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{`C}

(5)

\displaystyle`\rho_{B}\circ\varphi_{B,1}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{`B}\otimes\varphi)

\displaystyle=\rho_{`B}

(6)

for sets
B,C,D
.
We start with the latter two equalities.
Since
\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{I}
, equations (
5
) and (
6
) translate to

\displaystyle`\lambda_{C}\circ\varphi_{1,C}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{`C}

(7)

\displaystyle`\rho_{B}\circ\varphi_{B,1}

\displaystyle=\rho_{`B}

(8)

Let
\gamma\in C
. We denote the single element in the singleton set
1
by
*
. Then using Lemma
2.26
, we obtain

\displaystyle p_{\gamma}\circ`\lambda_{C}\circ\varphi_{1,C}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(*,\gamma^{\prime})\in 1\times C}(`\lambda_{C})_{(*,\gamma^{\prime}),\gamma}p_{(*,\gamma^{\prime})}\circ\varphi_{1,C}=p_{(*,\gamma)}\circ\varphi_{1,C}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{*}\otimes p_{\gamma})=\lambda_{I}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{I}\otimes p_{\gamma})=p_{\gamma}\circ\lambda_{`C},

where we used Lemma
2.26
in the first equality, Lemma
3.86
in the second equality, and naturality of
\lambda
in the last equality. Since
\gamma\in C
is arbitrary, we conclude that (
7
) holds. Let
\beta\in B
. Then

\displaystyle p_{\beta}\circ`\rho_{B}\circ\varphi_{B,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\beta^{\prime},*)\in B\times 1}(`\rho_{B})_{(\beta^{\prime},*),\beta}p_{(\beta^{\prime},*)}\circ\varphi_{B,1}=p_{(\beta,*)}\circ\varphi_{B,1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\beta}\otimes p_{*})=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\beta}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I})=\rho_{I}\circ(p_{\beta}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I})=p_{\beta}\circ\rho_{`B},

where we used Lemma
2.26
in the first equality, Lemma
3.86
in the second equality, and naturality of
\rho
in the last equality. Since
\beta\in B
is arbitrary, we conclude that (
8
) holds.

Finally, let
\beta\in B
,
\gamma\in C
and
\delta\in D
. Then

\displaystyle p_{(\beta,(\gamma,\delta))}\circ\varphi_{B,C\times D}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{`B}\otimes\varphi_{C,D})\circ\alpha_{`B,`C,`D}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\beta}\otimes p_{(\gamma,\delta)})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{`B}\otimes\varphi_{C,D})\circ\alpha_{`B,`C,`D}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\beta}\otimes(\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\gamma}\otimes p_{\delta})))\circ\alpha_{`B,`C,`D}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{I}\otimes\lambda_{I})\circ(p_{\beta}\otimes(p_{\gamma}\otimes p_{\delta}))\circ\alpha_{`B,`C,`D}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{I}\otimes\lambda_{I})\circ\alpha_{I,I,I}\circ((p_{\beta}\otimes p_{\gamma})\otimes p_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(\lambda_{I}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I})\circ((p_{\beta}\otimes p_{\gamma})\otimes p_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ((\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\beta}\otimes p_{\gamma}))\otimes p_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{(\beta,\gamma)}\otimes p_{\delta})\circ(\varphi_{B,C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{`D})

\displaystyle=p_{((\beta,\gamma),\delta)}\circ\varphi_{B\times C,D}\circ(\varphi_{B,C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{`D})

\displaystyle=\sum_{((\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}),\delta^{\prime})\in B\times(C\times D)}(`\alpha_{B,C,D})_{((\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}),\delta^{\prime}),(\beta,(\gamma,\delta))}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\circ p_{((\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}),\delta^{\prime})}\circ\varphi_{B\times C,D}\circ(\varphi_{B,C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{`D})

\displaystyle=p_{(\beta,(\gamma,\delta))}\circ`\alpha_{B,C,D}\circ\varphi_{B\times C,D}\circ(\varphi_{B,C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{`D}),

where we used naturality of
\alpha
in the fourth equality, coherence for a monoidal category in the fifth equality, Lemma
3.86
in the penultimate equality, and Lemma
2.26
in the last equality. Since the resulting equality holds for all
\beta\in B
,
\gamma\in C
and
\delta\in D
, we obtain (
4
).

We conclude with showing that
`(-)
is a symmetric monoidal functor, i.e.,
`\sigma_{A,B}\circ\varphi_{A,B}=\varphi_{B,A}\circ\sigma_{`A,`B}
. We first note that in a symmetric monoidal category, we always have
\lambda_{X}=\rho_{X}\circ\sigma_{I,X}
and
\lambda_{I}=\rho_{I}
, hence we have
\lambda_{I}=\lambda_{I}\circ\sigma_{I,I}
.
Let
\gamma\in A
and
\delta\in B
. Then using Lemma
2.26
yields

\displaystyle p_{(\delta,\gamma)}\circ`\sigma_{A,B}\circ\varphi_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B}(`\sigma_{A,B})_{(\alpha,\beta),(\delta,\gamma)}\circ p_{(\alpha,\beta)}\circ\varphi_{A,B}=p_{(\gamma,\delta)}\circ\varphi_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\gamma}\otimes p_{\delta})=\lambda_{I}\circ\sigma_{I,I}\circ(p_{\gamma}\otimes p_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\delta}\otimes p_{\gamma})\circ\sigma_{`A,`B}=p_{(\delta,\gamma)}\circ\varphi_{B,A}\circ\sigma_{`A,`B},

where we used the naturality of
\sigma
in the penultimate equality. We conclude that
`(-)
is indeed a symmetric monoidal functor.
∎

Finally, we collect our results:

Theorem 3.90
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with all small dagger biproducts. Then the functor
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}

•

is a homomorphism of dagger quantaloids;

•

is faithful, and even full if
\mathbf{R}
has precisely two scalars;

•

preserves top elements of homsets if
\mathbf{R}
is affine;

•

preserves all dagger biproducts;

•

is dagger strong monoidal with coherence maps
\varphi:I\to`1
and
\varphi_{A,B}:`A\otimes`B\to`(A\times B)
for sets
A
and
B
that are dagger isomorphisms, and that are given by
\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{I}
and
\varphi_{A,B}=\langle\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\alpha}\otimes p_{\beta})\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B}
, where
p_{\alpha}:`A\to I
and
p_{\beta}:`B\to I
are the canonical projections on the
\alpha
-th and
\beta
-th factor of
`A
and
`B
, respectively.

4
Internal maps

From this section on, we will focus on internalizing structures in dagger quantaloids. We will regard morphisms in dagger quantaloids as generalizations of relations, and will also refer to them as ‘relations’. Then we can generalize properties of ordinary endorelations as follows:

Definition 4.91
.

Let
X
be an object of a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
and let
r:X\to X
be an endorelation on
X
. Then we call
r
:

•

reflexive
if
\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq r
;

•

transitive
if
r\circ r\leq r
;

•

idempotent
if
r\circ r=r
;

•

symmetric
if
r^{\dagger}=r
;

•

anti-symmetric
if
r\wedge r^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{X}
;

•

a
preorder
if
r
is reflexive and transitive;

•

an
order
if
r
is an antisymmetric preorder;

•

a
partial equivalence relation (PER)
if
r
is symmetric and transitive;

•

an
equivalence relation
if
r
is a reflexive PER, or equivalently, if
r
is a symmetric preorder;

•

a
projection
if it is a symmetric idempotent.

If, in addition,
\mathbf{R}
can be equipped with a dagger-compact monoidal structure, we say that
r
is:

•

irreflexive
if
\mathrm{Tr}(r)=0_{I}
.

4.1
Definition and properties of internal maps

We proceed with introducing internal maps in dagger quantaloids, whose definition is similar to the definition of an internal map in an allegory.

Definition 4.92
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. We call a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{R}
a
map
if
f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}
.

Lemma 4.93
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be objects of a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Then:

(1)

for any two maps
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
in
\mathbf{R}
, also
g\circ f:X\to Z
is a map;

(2)

\mathrm{id}_{X}
is a map.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be maps, so
f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}
, and
g^{\dagger}\circ g\geq\mathrm{id}_{Y}
and
g\circ g^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Z}
. Then
(g\circ f)^{\dagger}\circ(g\circ f)=f^{\dagger}\circ g^{\dagger}\circ g\circ f\geq f^{\dagger}\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ f=f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
, and
(g\circ f)\circ(g\circ f)^{\dagger}=g\circ f\circ f^{\dagger}\circ g^{\dagger}\leq g\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ g^{\dagger}=g\circ g^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Z}
, so
g\circ f
is indeed a map. Finally, we have
\mathrm{id}_{X}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, hence
\mathrm{id}_{X}^{\dagger}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}^{\dagger}
, showing that
\mathrm{id}_{X}
is a map.
∎

The previous lemma assures that the following category is well defined.

Definition 4.94
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. Then by
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
we denote the wide subcategory of
\mathbf{R}
of maps.

Note that
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{Rel})=\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{qRel})=\mathbf{qSet}
.

Definition 4.95
.

A map
f:X\to Y
in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
is called

•

injective
if
f^{\dagger}\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
;

•

surjective
if
f\circ f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
;

•

bijective
if it is both injective and surjective.

We note that given the dagger biproduct
X
of a set-indexed family
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of objects in a dagger quantaloid with small biproducts, the canonical injection
i_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\to X
is indeed an injection in the above sense, since
i_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ i_{\alpha}=p_{\alpha}\circ i_{\alpha}=\mathrm{id}_{X_{\alpha}}
, whereas
i_{\alpha}\circ i_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\leq\bigvee_{\beta\in A}i_{\beta}\circ i_{\beta}^{\dagger}=\bigvee_{\beta\in A}i_{\beta}\circ p_{\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
(cf. Proposition
2.47
).

Lemma 4.96
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f
is a bijective map;

(b)

f
is a dagger isomorphism in
\mathbf{R}
;

(c)

f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
.

Proof.

The equivalence between (a) and (b) is trivial. Let
f
be a bijective map, so
f^{\dagger}\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
. It follows immediately that
f^{\dagger}
is also a map that is the inverse of
f
, hence
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
. Conversely, assume that
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, so there is a map
g:Y\to X
such that
g\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ g=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
.
Using that
f
is a map, it follows that

g^{\dagger}=g^{\dagger}\circ g\circ f\geq f
and
g^{\dagger}=f\circ g\circ g^{\dagger}\leq f
. Thus
g^{\dagger}=f
, hence
f^{\dagger}=g
. It follows that
f^{\dagger}\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
, so
f
is a bijective map.
∎

Lemma 4.97
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid, and let
f,g:X\to Y
be parallel maps in
\mathbf{R}
. If
f\leq g
in
\mathbf{R}
, then
f=g
.

Proof.

Since
f\leq g
, we have
f^{\dagger}\leq g^{\dagger}
.
We have
g=g\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq g\circ f^{\dagger}\circ f\leq g\circ g^{\dagger}\circ f\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ f=f
, which yields equality between
f
and
g
.
∎

Lemma 4.98
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid. Then
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
is a monoidal subcategory of
\mathbf{R}
.

Proof.

In order to show that
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
is a monoidal subcategory of
\mathbf{R}
, we only have to verify that the associator, unitors and symmetry are maps, but this follows immediately because in the definition of a dagger symmetric monoidal category, these morphisms are required to be dagger isomorphisms.
∎

A symmetric monoidal category is called
semicartesian
if its monoidal unit is terminal. The next lemma states some mild conditions that assure that the internal maps in a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid form a semicartesian category. Note that
\mathbf{qRel}
satisfies the conditions of the lemma (cf. Propositions
D.194
and
D.198
).

Lemma 4.99
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with dagger kernels. Assume that for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
:

(1)

there is a zero-monic effect
e:X\to I
;

(2)

any zero-monic PER on
X
is an equivalence relation on
X
.

Then:

(a)

I
is terminal in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
;

(b)

for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
, the morphism
\top_{X,I}:X\to I
is the unique effect that is zero-monic.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to I
be a zero-monic effect. Then
f\circ f^{\dagger}
is a scalar, and since
\mathbf{R}
is affine, it follows that
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{I}
. Consider
p=f^{\dagger}\circ f
. Then
p^{\dagger}=p
and
p\circ p=f^{\dagger}\circ f\circ f^{\dagger}\circ f\leq f^{\dagger}\circ\mathrm{id}_{I}\circ f=p
, so
p
is a PER. Since
f
is a zero-mono, it follows from Lemma
2.58
that
p
is also a zero-mono. Hence, by assumption, we have that
p
is an equivalence relation, so
p\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
. It follows that
f
is a map. Let
g:X\to I
be another map. Since
\mathbf{R}
is affine, we have
f\circ g^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{I}
, hence
f\leq f\circ g^{\dagger}\circ g\leq\mathrm{id}_{I}\circ g=g
, hence it follows from Lemma
4.97
that
f=g
. By assumption, there is a zero-monic effect
e:X\to I
. By Lemma
3.79
it follows that
\top_{X,I}
is a zero-monic effect, which is therefore a map, and any other map
X:\to I
must be equal to
\top_{X,I}
, proving that
I
is terminal in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
. For (b), if
e:X\to I
is another zero-monic effect, it follows that
e
is a map which necessarily equals
\top_{X,I}
, so
\top_{X,I}:X\to I
is the unique effect on
X
that is zero-monic.
∎

Proposition 4.100
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with all small dagger biproducts. Then the embedding
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{R}
creates all coproducts, i.e., if
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is a collection of objects in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, then their dagger biproduct in
\mathbf{R}
is their coproduct in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, and the canonical injections in
\mathbf{R}
are maps.

Proof.

Let
X
be the dagger biproduct in
\mathbf{R}
of a collection
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of objects in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
with canonical injections
i_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\to X
for each
\alpha\in A
. Fix
\alpha\in A
. Since
X
is a dagger biproduct, the canonical projection
p_{\alpha}:X\to X_{\alpha}
satisfies
p_{\alpha}=i_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
. By definition of a biproduct, we have
i_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ i_{\alpha}=p_{\alpha}\circ i_{\alpha}=\mathrm{id}_{X_{\alpha}}.
It follows from Corollary
2.23
that
i_{\alpha}\circ i_{\alpha}^{\dagger}=i_{\alpha}\circ p_{\alpha}\leq\bigvee_{\beta\in A}i_{\beta}\circ p_{\beta}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, so
i_{\alpha}
is indeed a map.

Now, let
Y
be another object of
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, and for each
\alpha\in A
, let
f_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\to Y
be a map. To show that
X
is the coproduct of
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, we have to show that
f:=[f_{\alpha}]_{\alpha\in A}:X\to Y
is a map. Using Proposition
2.31
, we find

f\circ f^{\dagger}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y},

for
f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}
because
f_{\alpha}
is a map. By the same proposition, we obtain
(f^{\dagger}\circ f)_{\alpha,\beta}=f_{\beta}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}
.
Let
\alpha,\beta\in A
. First assume that
\alpha\neq\beta
. By Lemma
2.28
, we have
(\mathrm{id}_{X})_{\alpha,\beta}=0_{X_{\alpha},X_{\beta}}
for
\alpha\neq\beta
, so
(f^{\dagger}\circ f)_{\alpha,\beta}\geq(\mathrm{id}_{X})_{\alpha,\beta}
. Now, let
\alpha=\beta
. Since
f_{\alpha}
is a map, it follows that
(f^{\dagger}\circ f)_{\alpha,\beta}=f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\geq\mathrm{id}_{X_{\alpha}}=(\mathrm{id}_{X})_{\alpha,\beta}
, where the last identity also follows from Lemma
2.28
. So
(f^{\dagger}\circ f)_{\alpha,\beta}\geq(\mathrm{id}_{X})_{\alpha,\beta}
for each
\alpha,\beta\in A
. It now follows from Proposition
2.49
that
f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
, so
f
is a map.∎

4.2
The embedding of
\mathbf{Set}

Theorem 4.101
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with all small dagger biproducts. Then the embedding
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
restricts and corestricts to a faithful strong symmetric monoidal functor
`(-):\mathbf{Set}\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, which is full if
\mathbf{R}
has precisely two scalars. The coherence maps
\varphi:I\to`1
and
\varphi_{A,B}:`A\otimes`B\to`(A\times B)
for sets
A
and
B
are given by
\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{I}
and
\varphi_{A,B}=\langle\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\alpha}\otimes p_{\beta})\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B}
, where
p_{\alpha}:`A\to I
and
p_{\beta}:`B\to I
are the canonical projections on the
\alpha
-th and
\beta
-th factor of
`A
and
`B
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
f:A\to B
be a function between ordinary sets. Then
f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{A}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{B}
. By Theorem
3.90
,
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is a homomorphism of dagger quantaloids, whence

(`f)^{\dagger}\circ(`f)=`(f^{\dagger}\circ f)\geq`\mathrm{id}_{A}=\mathrm{id}_{`A}
and
(`f)\circ(`f)^{\dagger}=`(f\circ f^{\dagger})\leq`\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{`B}
. We conclude that
`f
is a map, hence
`(-)
restricts and corestricts to a functor
\mathbf{Set}\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
.
By Theorem
3.90
,
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is dagger strong symmetric monoidal, which implies that the morphisms
\varphi:I\to`1
and
\varphi_{A,B}:`A\otimes`B\to`(A\times B)
are dagger isomorphisms, so isomorphisms in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
by Lemma
4.96
. Naturality of
\varphi
and the commutativity of the diagrams for a symmetric monoidal functor are inherited from
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
being a symmetric monoidal functor. We conclude that
`(-):\mathbf{Set}\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
is strong symmetric monoidal. We proceed with showing that
`(-):\mathbf{Set}\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
is faithful. Let
f,g:A\to B
be functions between sets such that
`f=`g
in
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
. Then
`f=`g
in
\mathbf{R}
, and since Theorem
3.90
assures that
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is faithful, it follows that
f=g
. Thus
`(-)
is faithful. Now assume that
\mathbf{R}
has precisely two scalars. Let
g:`A\to`B
be a map. Since
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is fully faithful by Theorem
3.90
, it follows from Lemma
2.40
that
\mathbf{Rel}(A,B)\to\mathbf{R}(`A,`B)
,
r\mapsto`r
is an order isomorphism. Hence, there is some binary relation
f:A\to B
such that
`f=g
. Since
g
is a map, we have
(`f)^{\dagger}\circ(`f)\geq\mathrm{id}_{`A}
and
(`f)\circ(`f)^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{`B}
. Since
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
preserves daggers, we obtain
`(f^{\dagger}\circ f)\geq`\mathrm{id}_{A}
and
`(f\circ f^{\dagger})\leq`\mathrm{id}_{B}
. Since
\mathbf{Rel}(A,B)\to\mathbf{R}(`A,`B)
,
r\mapsto`r
is an order isomorphism, we obtain
f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{A}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{B}
. So
f
is indeed a function.
∎

Proposition 4.102
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with all small dagger biproducts. Let
\mathbf{S}=\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
. Then the embedding
`(-):\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{S}
has a right adjoint given by
\mathbf{S}(I,-)
.

Proof.

Given a set
A
, the
A
-component
\eta_{A}:A\to\mathbf{S}(I,`A)
of the unit
\eta
of the adjunction is defined by
\eta_{A}(\alpha)=i_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in A
, where
i_{\alpha}:I\to`A
is the canonical injection of
I
into the
\alpha
-th factor of
`A=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}I_{\alpha}
. We note that
i_{\alpha}
is a map in
\mathbf{R}
by Proposition
4.100
, hence
\eta_{A}
is well defined. Now, let
X
be an object of
\mathbf{S}
, and let
f:A\to\mathbf{S}(I,X)
be a function. We define
g:`A\to X
as the morphism
[f(\alpha)]_{\alpha\in A}
. Since
f(\alpha):I\to X
is a map in
\mathbf{R}
for each
\alpha\in A
, it follows from Proposition
4.100
that
g
is a map in
\mathbf{R}
, so a morphism in
\mathbf{S}
. Then for each
\beta\in A
, we have
\mathbf{S}(I,g)\circ\eta_{A}(\beta)=g\circ\eta_{A}(\beta)=g\circ i_{\beta}=[f(\alpha)]_{\alpha\in A}\circ i_{\beta}=f(\beta)
, so
\mathbf{S}(I,g)\circ\eta_{A}=f
.
Given any other map
h:`A\to X
such that
\mathbf{S}(I,h)\circ\eta_{A}=f
, we have
f(\alpha)=S(I,h)\circ\eta_{A}(\alpha)=h\circ\eta_{A}(\alpha)=h\circ i_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in A
, which shows that
h=[f(\alpha)]_{\alpha\in A}=g
.
∎

4.3
Maps to sets

Lemma 4.103
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with small dagger biproducts. Let
X
be an object of
\mathbf{R}
, let
A
be a set, and let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
be a set-indexed family in
\mathbf{R}(X,I)
. Write
f=\langle f_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha\in A}:X\to`{A}
. Then:

(a)

f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{`A}
if and only if
f_{\alpha}\perp f_{\beta}
for every district
\alpha,\beta\in A
;

(b)

f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
if and only if
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
.

Proof.

We denote the embedding of
I
onto the
\alpha
-th factor of
`A
by
i_{\alpha}
. Its corresponding projection is denoted by
p_{\alpha}
, which satisfies
p_{\alpha}=i_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
. Proposition
2.31
yields
(f\circ f^{\dagger})_{\alpha,\beta}=f_{\beta}\circ f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
for each
\alpha,\beta\in A
.
By Lemma
2.28
, we have
(\mathrm{id}_{`A})_{\alpha,\beta}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
for each
\alpha,\beta\in A
.
It now follows from Proposition
2.49
that
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{`A}
if and only if

f_{\alpha}\circ f^{\dagger}_{\alpha}\leq\mathrm{id}_{I}
for each
\alpha\in A
and
f_{\alpha}\circ f^{\dagger}_{\beta}\leq 0_{I}
for each
\alpha\neq\beta
. Since by assumption,
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
is affine,
\mathrm{id}_{I}
is the largest scalar, hence the former condition always holds, whereas the second condition translates to
f_{\alpha}\perp f_{\beta}
for
\alpha\neq\beta
.

For (b), let
g_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}:I\to X
, and let
g=[g_{\alpha}]_{\alpha\in A}:`A\to X
. Then
f=g^{\dagger}
by Proposition
2.31
, which also yields
f^{\dagger}\circ f=g\circ g^{\dagger}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}g_{\alpha}\circ g_{\alpha}^{\dagger}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}
,
from which the statement follows.
∎

Proposition 4.104
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger compact quantaloid with dagger kernels such that every object has exactly one zero-mono effect. Then an effect
r:X\to I
is a zero-mono if and only if
r^{\dagger}\circ r\geq\mathrm{id}_{I}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.83
, the homsets of
\mathbf{R}
are orthomodular lattices under the orthogonality relation
\perp
given by
f\perp g
if and only if
\mathrm{Tr}(f\circ g^{\dagger})=0_{I}
.
Assume
r^{\dagger}\circ r\geq\mathrm{id}_{I}
. Let
s:Z\to X
be a morphism such that
r^{\dagger}\circ r\circ s=0_{Z,X}
. Then
0_{Z,X}=r^{\dagger}\circ r\circ s\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ s=s
, forcing
s=0_{Z,X}
. Hence,
r^{\dagger}\circ r
a zero-mono, hence also
r
is a zero-mono by Lemma
2.58
. Conversely, assume
r
is a zero-mono. Let
p=r^{\dagger}\circ r
. Then
p^{\dagger}=p
.
Let
f:X\to X
such that
p\perp f
. Then
0_{I}=\mathrm{Tr}(f\circ p^{\dagger})=\mathrm{Tr}(f\circ p)=\mathrm{Tr}(f\circ r^{\dagger}\circ r)=\mathrm{Tr}(r\circ f\circ r^{\dagger})=r\circ f\circ r^{\dagger}
, since
r\circ f\circ r^{\dagger}
is a scalar. Since
r
is a zero-mono, it follows that
f\circ r^{\dagger}=0_{I,X}
, hence
r\circ f^{\dagger}=0_{X,I}
. Again, since
r
is a zero-mono, it follows
f^{\dagger}=0_{X}
, so also
f=0_{X}
. Thus we have shown that
f\perp p
implies
f=0_{X}
. Since
\mathbf{R}
is a dagger compact quantaloid, it follows from Lemma
3.68
that the trace preserves arbitrary suprema. Hence,
\mathrm{Tr}(\neg p\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}^{\dagger})=\mathrm{Tr}(\neg p)=\bigvee\{\mathrm{Tr}(f):f\perp p\}=\mathrm{Tr}(0_{X})=0_{I}
. We conclude that
\neg p\perp\mathrm{id}_{X}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq\neg\neg p=p
.
∎

Theorem 4.105
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger compact quantaloid with small dagger biproducts and dagger kernels such that for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
:

(1)

\top_{X,I}
is a zero-monic effect;

(2)

every zero-monic PER on
X
is a equivalence relation on
X
.

Then for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
and each set
A
, any morphism
f=\langle f_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha\in A}:X\to`A
is a map if and only if
f_{\alpha}\perp f_{\beta}
for each distinct
\alpha,\beta\in A
and
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}=\top_{X,I}
.

Proof.

For any object
X
it follows from Lemma
4.99
that
\top_{X,I}
is the unique zero-monic effect on
X
.
Now, if
f:X\to`A
is a map, it follows from Lemma
4.103
that
f_{\alpha}\perp f_{\beta}
for distinct
\alpha,\beta\in A
and that
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f^{\dagger}_{\alpha}\circ f_{\alpha}\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Then, using that
\mathbf{R}
is a dagger quantaloid, we obtain

\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}\right)^{\dagger}\circ\left(\bigvee_{\beta\in A}f_{\beta}\right)=\bigvee_{\alpha,\beta\in A}f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\beta}\geq\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\geq\mathrm{id}_{X},

hence
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}
is a zero-monic effect on
X
by Proposition
4.104
. Since
\top_{X,I}
is the unique zero-monic effect on
X
, we conclude that
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}=\top_{X,I}
.

Conversely, assume that
f:X\to`A
satisfies
f_{\alpha}\perp f_{\beta}
for distinct
\alpha,\beta\in A
and that
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}=\top_{X,I}
. It follows from Lemma
4.103
that
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{`A}
. Moreover, since
\top_{X,I}
is a zero-mono, it follows that
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}
is a zero-mono.

For each
\alpha\in A
, let
s_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}
. Let
s=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}s_{\alpha}
. We are done if we can show that
s\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Firstly, we clearly have
s_{\alpha}^{\dagger}=s_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in A
, whence
s^{\dagger}=s
for
\mathbf{R}
is a dagger quantaloid. For each
\alpha\in A
, since
f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
is a scalar and
\mathbf{R}
is affine, we have
f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{I}
, hence
s_{\alpha}\circ s_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\leq f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ\mathrm{id}_{I}\circ f_{\alpha}=s_{\alpha}
.
Now assume that
\alpha
and
\beta
in
A
are distinct. By assumption,
f_{\alpha}\perp f_{\beta}
, so
f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\beta}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Tr}(f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\beta}^{\dagger})=0_{I}
, using Proposition
2.13
in the first equality. Hence,
s_{\alpha}\circ s_{\beta}=f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\beta}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\beta}=0_{X}
. Then
s\circ s=\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}s_{\alpha}\right)\circ\left(\bigvee_{\beta\in A}s_{\beta}\right)=\bigvee_{\alpha,\beta\in A}s_{\alpha}\circ s_{\beta}\leq\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}s_{\alpha}=s
, so
s
is symmetric and transitive, hence a PER.

We claim that
s
is a zero-mono. So let
r:Y\to X
be a morphism such that
s\circ r=0_{Y,X}
. Since
\mathbf{R}
is a quantaloid, this implies
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}s_{\alpha}\circ r=0_{Y,X}
, which is only possible if
s_{\alpha}\circ r=0_{Y,X}
for each
\alpha\in A
. Then
r^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\circ r=r^{\dagger}\circ s_{\alpha}\circ r=0_{Y}
for each
\alpha\in A
, which implies
f_{\alpha}\circ r=0_{Y,I}
for each
\alpha\in A
by Lemma
2.57
. Hence,
0_{Y,I}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}\circ r=\top_{X,I}\circ r
, which implies
r=0
for
\top_{X,I}
is a zero-mono. So
s
is indeed a zero-mono. It now follows from assumption (2) that
s\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
, i.e.,
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}f_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\circ f_{\alpha}\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
.
∎

Corollary 4.106
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger compact quantaloid with small dagger biproducts and dagger kernels such that for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
:

(1)

\top_{X,I}
is a zero-monic effect;

(2)

every zero-monic PER on
X
is a equivalence relation on
X
.

Let
\Omega=I\oplus I
, and denote the projection
\Omega\to I
on the first factor by
p_{0}
, and the projection on the second factor by
p_{1}
. Then for each object
X
, we have a bijection

\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})(X,\Omega)\to\mathbf{R}(X,I),\qquad f\mapsto p_{1}\circ f

whose inverse is given by
r\mapsto\langle\neg r,r\rangle
, where

\neg r=\bigvee\{s\in\mathbf{R}(X,I):r\perp s\}
with
r\perp s
if and only if
\mathrm{Tr}(r\circ s^{\dagger})=0_{I}
for each
r,s:X\to I
.

Proof.

For any object
X
it follows from Lemma
4.99
that
\top_{X,I}
is the unique zero-monic effect on
X
. Hence, we can apply Theorem
3.83
to conclude that homsets in
\mathbf{R}
are orthomodular lattices with respect to the orthocomplementation
\neg
.
Let
\varphi
be the map
\mathbf{R}(X,I)\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})(X,\Omega)
,
r\mapsto\langle\neg r,r\rangle
. It follows directly from Theorem
4.105
that
\varphi
is well defined. Denote the map
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})(X,\Omega)\to\mathbf{R}(X,I)
,
f\mapsto p_{1}\circ f
by
\psi
. Let
r\in\mathbf{R}(X,I)
. Then
\psi\circ\varphi(r)=\psi(\langle\neg r,r\rangle)=p_{1}\circ\langle\neg r,r\rangle=r
. Let
f:X\to\Omega
be a map. Let
f_{1}=p_{1}\circ f
and
f_{0}=p_{0}\circ f
, so
f=\langle f_{0},f_{1}\rangle
as morphism in
\mathbf{R}
. By Theorem
4.105
, we have
f_{0}\perp f_{1}
and
f_{0}\vee f_{1}=\top_{X,I}
, so
f_{0}=\neg f_{1}
.
As a consequence,
\varphi\circ\psi(f)=\varphi(p_{1}\circ f)=\varphi(f_{1})=\langle\neg f_{1},f_{1}\rangle=\langle f_{0},f_{1}\rangle=f
.
We conclude that
\varphi
and
\psi
are each other’s inverses, which proves the statement.
∎

5
Internal preorders

5.1
Preordered objects

In this section, we investigate internal preorders in dagger quantaloids (cf. Definition
4.91
).

Lemma 5.107
.

Let
r:X\to X
be an endorelation on an object
X
of a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Then:

(a)

r^{\dagger}
is reflexive if
r
is reflexive;

(b)

r^{\dagger}
is transitive if
r
is transitive;

(c)

r^{\dagger}
is symmetric if
r
is symmetric;

(d)

r^{\dagger}
is anti-symmetric if
r
is anti-symmetric;

(e)

r^{\dagger}
is irreflexive if
r
is irreflexive (under the additional assumptions that
\mathbf{R}
is a dagger compact quantaloid with a zero object).

Proof.

For (a), (b) and (d), we will we use that
(-)^{\dagger}
is a functor whose action on homsets is an order isomorphism:

(a)

We have
\mathrm{id}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}^{\dagger}\leq r^{\dagger}
;

(b)

We have
r^{\dagger}\circ r^{\dagger}=(r\circ r)^{\dagger}\leq r^{\dagger}
;

(c)

We have
(r^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=r^{\dagger}
for
r^{\dagger}=r
;

(d)

We have
r^{\dagger}\wedge(r^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=r^{\dagger}\wedge r=r\wedge r^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{X}
;

(e)

We have
\mathrm{Tr}(r^{\dagger})=\mathrm{Tr}(r)
by
[
15
, Lemma 3.63(f)]
, from which the statement follows.

∎

Lemma 5.108
.

Let
r:X\to X
be an endorelation on an object
X
of a dagger compact quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Then
r^{*}
on
X^{*}
satisfies the following properties:

(a)

r^{*}
is reflexive if
r
is reflexive;

(b)

r^{*}
is transitive if
r
is transitive;

(c)

r^{*}
is symmetric if
r
is symmetric;

(d)

r^{*}
is anti-symmetric if
r
is anti-symmetric;

(e)

r^{*}
is irreflexive if
r
is irreflexive (under the additional assumptions that
\mathbf{R}
has a zero object).

Proof.

For (a), (b), and (d), we will use that
(-)^{*}
is a functor whose action on homsets is an order isomorphism (cf. Lemma
3.67
):

(a)

We have
\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}=\mathrm{id}_{X}^{*}\leq r^{*}
;

(b)

We have
r^{*}\circ r^{*}=(r\circ r)^{*}\leq r*
;

(c)

Using Lemma
2.11
, we find
(r^{*})^{\dagger}=(r^{\dagger})^{*}=r^{*}
for
r^{\dagger}=r
;

(d)

We have
r^{*}\wedge(r^{*})^{\dagger}=r^{*}\wedge(r^{\dagger})^{*}=(r\wedge r^{\dagger})^{*}\leq(\mathrm{id}_{X})^{*}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
, where we used Lemma
2.11
in the first equality;

(e)

We have
\mathrm{Tr}_{X^{*}}(r^{*})=\mathrm{Tr}_{X}(r)
by
[
15
, Exercise 3.12(c)]
, from which the statement follows.

∎

Example 5.109
.

Let
X
be an object in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Then the identity morphism
\mathrm{id}_{X}
on
X
is reflexive, transitive, symmetric, and anti-symmetric. We call
\mathrm{id}_{X}
the
trivial
or
flat
order on
X
.

Definition 5.110
.

An
preorder
on an object
X
of a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
is a reflexive and transitive endomorphism
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}:X\to X
. We call the pair
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
a
preordered
object.
If, in addition,
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
is anti-symmetric, we call
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
a
partial order
and
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
a
partially ordered object
, or with a slight abuse of terminology a
poset
. Sometimes, we will say that
X
is a preordered object or poset without mentioning the (pre)order
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
explicitly.

We will often formulate inequalities between morphisms in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
involving preorders
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
on objects
X
of
\mathbf{R}
. In order to increase the readability of those expressions, we will sometimes write
({\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
instead of
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
.

Given a preorder
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
on an object
X
in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
, it follows from Lemma
5.107
that the dagger
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}^{\dagger}
of
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
is again a preorder. Similarly, if
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
is a dagger compact quantaloid, it follows from Lemma
5.108
that the dual
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}^{*}
of
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
is a preorder (on
X^{*}
). In both cases, the resulting preorders are even orders when
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
is an order. This leads to the the following definition:

Definition 5.111
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
be a preordered object in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
.

•

We call the preorder
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}:={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}^{\dagger}
the
opposite
preorder, and the pair
(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})
the
opposite
preordered objects, also denoted by
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})^{\mathrm{op}}
, or simply
X^{\mathrm{op}}
if it is clear that
X
is preordered by
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
.

•

If
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
is a dagger compact quantaloid, we call the preorder
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}^{*}
the
dual
preorder, and the pair
(X^{*},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}^{*})
the preordered object
dual
to
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
, also denoted by
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})^{*}
, or simply
X^{*}
if it is clear that
X
is preordered by
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
.

If
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
is an order, we call
X^{\mathrm{op}}
and
X^{*}
the
opposite
poset and the
dual
poset of
X
, respectively.

For
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{Rel}
, the opposite preorder on an object coincides with the dual preorder. However, for
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{qRel}
both concepts differ, since objects are not naturally isomorphic to their dual in this category, let alone equal as in the case of
\mathbf{Rel}
.

For the next definition, recall that a
map
from an object
X
to an object
Y
in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
is a morphism
f:X\to Y
such that
f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}
.

Definition 5.112
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
and
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be preordered objects of a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Then a map
f:X\to Y
is called:

•

monotone
if it satisfies satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions (hence all):

(1)

f\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f
;

(2)

f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
;

(3)

({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\leq f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f
.

•

an order embedding
if
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}=f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f
;

•

an order isomorphism
if it is a monotone map that has an inverse which is also monotone.

We verify that the conditions in the definition are indeed equivalent. Assume that (1) holds. We show that (2) holds:

f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y}=({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}).

Now assume that (2) holds. We show that (3) holds:

({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{X}}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq f^{\dagger}\circ f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}\circ f\leq f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f.

Finally we show that (3) implies (1).

f\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\leq f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}\circ f\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f.

It follows directly from the definitions that an order embedding is monotone.

Lemma 5.113
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
, and let
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
be a preorder on
Y
. Then
f:(X,\mathrm{id}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
is monotone.

Proof.

By a direct calculation:
f\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ f\leq{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}\circ f
.
∎

Lemma 5.114
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
,
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
and
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
be preordered objects in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
and let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be monotone maps. Then
g\circ f
is a monotone map.

Proof.

By monotonicity of
f
and
g
, we have
f\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f
and
g\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})\circ g
, hence
g\circ f\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\leq g\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})\circ g\circ f
.
∎

It follows from the previous lemma that the following categories are well defined.

Definition 5.115
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. Then:

•

\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is defined as the category of preordered objects and monotone maps. The identity morphism on an object
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
of
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is the identity
\mathrm{id}_{X}
on
X
.

•

\mathbf{Pos}(\mathbf{R})
is defined as the full subcategory of
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
of partially ordered objects.

If
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{Rel}
, we have
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})=\mathbf{PreOrd}
and
\mathbf{Pos}(\mathbf{R})=\mathbf{Pos}
.

Lemma 5.116
.

Let
X
and
Y
be preordered objects in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
and let
f:X\to Y
be a map. Then
f:X\to Y
is monotone if and only if
f:X^{\mathrm{op}}\to Y^{\mathrm{op}}
is monotone.

Proof.

Let
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}
and
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}
be the preorders on
X
and
Y
, respectively. Assume that
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
is a monotone map. Then
f\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f
, hence
({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\circ f^{\dagger}=({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{\dagger}\circ f^{\dagger}\leq f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})^{\mathrm{op}}=f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})
. Using the properties of a map, we obtain

f\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})=f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}\circ f\leq f\circ f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f,

so
f
is indeed a monotone map
X^{\mathrm{op}}\to Y^{\mathrm{op}}
. Now assume that
f:X^{\mathrm{op}}\to Y^{\mathrm{op}}
is a monotone map. Then it follows that
f:X^{\mathrm{op}\mathrm{op}}\to Y^{\mathrm{op}\mathrm{op}}
is a monotone map, and since
X^{\mathrm{op}\mathrm{op}}=X
and
Y^{\mathrm{op}\mathrm{op}}=Y
, the statement follows.
∎

Proposition 5.117
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. Then the assignment
X\mapsto X^{\mathrm{op}}
extends to a functor
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}:\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
that is the identity on morphisms.

Proof.

Let
X
,
Y
and
Z
be preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
, and let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be monotone maps.
Using Lemma
5.116
, we have
\mathrm{id}_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}=\mathrm{id}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{\mathrm{op}}}
, because the underlying object of
X
and
X^{\mathrm{op}}
it the same. The lemma also yields
(f\circ g)^{\mathrm{op}}=f\circ g=f^{\mathrm{op}}\circ g^{\mathrm{op}}
, so
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}
is functorial.
∎

Next, we provide an alternative description of order isomorphisms.

Lemma 5.118
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid and let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
and
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
. Then a map
f:X\to Y
is an order isomorphism if and only if it is a bijection such that
f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f
.

Proof.

Let
f
be an order isomorphism. Then it is a monotone map, and there is a monotone map
g:Y\to X
such that
g\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ g=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
. Then
g=\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ g\leq f^{\dagger}\circ f\circ g=f^{\dagger}\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y}=f_{Y}
, and
g=g\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y}\geq g\circ f\circ f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ f^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}
, so
f^{\dagger}=g
. Thus
f^{\dagger}\circ f=I_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
expressing that
f
is both injective and surjective, hence bijective. Moreover, since
f
and
g
are monotone, we obtain
f\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f
and
g\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\circ g
. From the latter inequality we obtain
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Y}}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f=f\circ g\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f\leq f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ g\circ f=f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}=f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
, whence
f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f
.

Conversely, assume that
f
is a bijection such that
f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f
. It follows immediately that
f
is monotone. Since
f
is a bijection, we have
f^{\dagger}\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
, whence
f^{\dagger}:Y\to X
is also a map. Then
f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}=f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y}=f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f\circ f^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}\circ f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ f^{\dagger}
, so also
f^{\dagger}
is monotone.
∎

Lemma 5.119
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
and
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be preordered objects in a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
. Then
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}):=(X\otimes Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
is a preordered object as well.

Proof.

Since
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
is a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid, the order relation on morphisms respects daggers and the monoidal product. Hence, we have
\mathrm{id}_{X\otimes Y}=\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y}\leq{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}
, so
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}
is reflexive. We also have
({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})=({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\leq{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}
.
∎

Lemma 5.120
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
,
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
,
(W,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W})
and
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
be preordered objects in a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Let
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(W,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W})
and
g:(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
be monotone maps. Then
f\otimes g:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(W,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W})\otimes(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
is a monotone map.

Proof.

Using that the monoidal product in a symmetric monoidal quantaloid preserves the order in both arguments separately, we obtain

(f\otimes g)\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})=(f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(g\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W}\circ f)\otimes({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z}\circ g)=({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})\circ(f\otimes g)
.
∎

Theorem 5.121
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid. The category
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
becomes a symmetric monoidal category as follows:

•

We define the monoidal product by
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}):=(X\otimes Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
on objects, and on monotone maps by the monoidal product of their underlying morphisms in
\mathbf{R}
;

•

The monoidal unit is
(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})
;

•

the associator, unitors and symmetry between preordered objects are the respective associator, unitors and symmetry between the underlying objects of
\mathbf{R}
.

Moreover, the inclusion functor
J:\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
,
X\mapsto(X,\mathrm{id}_{X})
is strict monoidal, and left adjoint to the forgetful functor
U:\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
,
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})\mapsto X
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemmas
5.119
and
5.120
that
\otimes:\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\times\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is a well defined bifunctor.
By Lemma
4.98
,
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
inherits its monoidal structure from
\mathbf{R}
. Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
,
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
and
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
be preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
. We need to show that the associator
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}:(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\to X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)
, the left unitor
\lambda_{X}:I\otimes X\to X
, the right unitor
\rho_{X}:X\otimes I\to X
and the symmetry
\sigma_{X,Y}:X\otimes Y\to Y\otimes X
are order isomorphisms, which in the light of Lemma
5.118
means that we have to show that

\displaystyle\alpha_{X,Y,Z}\circ(({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})

\displaystyle=({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z}))\circ\alpha_{X,Y,Z},

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{I}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})

\displaystyle={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\lambda_{X}

\displaystyle\rho_{X}\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I})

\displaystyle={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\rho_{X},

\displaystyle\sigma_{X,Y}\circ({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})

\displaystyle=({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\circ\sigma_{Y,X},

but this follows directly because
\alpha
,
\lambda
,
\rho
, and
\sigma
are natural isomorphisms in
\mathbf{R}
.

Finally, it also follows from Example
5.109
and Lemma
5.113
that the assignment
X\mapsto(X,\mathrm{id}_{X})
extends to an inclusion functor
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
. For any two object
X
and
Y
of
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, we have
JX\otimes JY=(X,\mathrm{id}_{X})\otimes(Y,\mathrm{id}_{Y})=(X\otimes Y,\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y})=(X\otimes Y,\mathrm{id}_{X\otimes Y})=J(X\otimes Y)
, and
JI=(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})
, from which follows that
J
is strict monoidal. To show that
J
is left adjoint to
U
, let
X
be an object of
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
, we need a candidate unit for the adjunction, so a map
X\to UJX
. Since
UJX=X
, we can choose this map to be the identity
\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Now let
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
be a preordered object of
\mathbf{R}
, and let
f:X\to U(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})=Y
be a map. We need to show that there is a unique monotone map
g:JX\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UJX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ug}

Since
UJX=X
and
U(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})=Y
, the only possible choice would be
g=f
, for which we have to verify that
f
is a monotone map
JX\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
. But this follows directly from Lemma
5.113
.
∎

5.2
Monotone relations

Definition 5.122
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
and
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be preordered objects in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. We say that a morphism
v:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{R}
is a
monotone relation

(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
if it satisfies one of the following two equivalent conditions (hence both):

(1)

({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ v\leq v
and
v\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\leq v
.

(2)

({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ v=v=v\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})
.

Clearly, (2) implies (1). For the other direction, we have
v=\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ v\leq({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ v
, and
v=v\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq v\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})
.

Example 5.123
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
be a preordered object in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
. Then
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}
is a monotone relation
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
as follows from the transitivity of
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}
.

Lemma 5.124
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
,
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
and
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
be preordered objects in a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
and let
r:X\to Y
and
s:Y\to Z
be monotone relations. Then
s\circ r:X\to Z
is a monotone relation.

Proof.

The monotonicity of
r
and
s
implies that
r=r\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}
and
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z}\circ s=s
, hence
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z}\circ s\circ r=s\circ r=s\circ r\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}
.
∎

It follows from the previous lemma that the following categories are well defined.

Definition 5.125
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. Then
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is defined as the category of preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
and monotone relations. The identity monotone relation
\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}
on a preordered object
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
is the monotone relation
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}
. Instead of
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{Rel})
we write
\mathbf{MonRel}
.

Lemma 5.126
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. Then
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a quantaloid: If
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
and
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
are preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
, then the supremum of a collection
(r_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
monotone relations
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
is given by the supremum
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}
of
(r_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
\mathbf{R}
.

Proof.

We need to show that
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}
is a monotone relation. Since
\mathbf{R}
is a quantaloid, we find

{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ r_{\alpha}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}=\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}\right)\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}.\qed

Proposition 5.127
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid with small dagger biproducts. Then
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a quantaloid with small biproducts.

More specifically, let
(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
be a set-indexed family of preordered objects of
\mathbf{R}
. Then
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})=(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
where
X=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
and
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}
. Moreover, if
p_{X_{\beta}}:X\to X_{\beta}
and
i_{X_{\beta}}:X_{\beta}\to X
denote the respective canonical projection and the canonical injection for each
\beta\in A
, then:

•

the canonical projection map
p_{(X_{\beta},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\beta})}:\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})\to(X_{\beta},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\beta})
is given by

{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X_{\beta}}\circ p_{X_{\beta}}=p_{X_{\beta}}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X};

(9)

•

the canonical injection map
i_{(X_{\beta},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\beta})}:(X_{\beta},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\beta})\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})
is given by

i_{X_{\beta}}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X_{\beta}}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\circ i_{X_{\beta}}.

(10)

Proof.

Firstly, by Proposition
2.49
we have
\mathrm{id}_{X}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}\mathrm{id}_{X_{\alpha}}\leq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}
, and
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}=\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}\right)\circ\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}\right)=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}\leq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}
. Thus
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
is a preordered object in
\mathbf{R}
.
By Lemma
2.30
, we have
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\alpha}
, whence (
9
) and (
10
) hold. These two equalities immediately imply that
p_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})}
and
i_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})}
are monotone relations for each
\alpha\in A
.

By Lemma
5.126
,
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a quantaloid.
Using the characterization of biproducts in quantaloids in
Proposition
2.47
, we have
p_{X_{\beta}}\circ i_{X_{\alpha}}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
for each
\alpha,\beta\in A
, and
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}i_{X_{\alpha}}\circ p_{X_{\alpha}}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
. From the first identity it follows that for each
\alpha,\beta\in
that

p_{(X_{\beta},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\beta})}\circ i_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\beta}\circ p_{X_{\beta}}\circ i_{X_{\alpha}}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\alpha}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\beta}\circ\delta_{X_{\alpha},X_{\beta}}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\alpha}=\delta_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha}),(X_{\beta},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\beta})}.

From the second identity, and using that
\mathbf{R}
is a quantaloid, so pre- and postcomposition preserve suprema, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}i_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})}\circ p_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})}

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\circ i_{X_{\alpha}}\circ p_{X_{\alpha}}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}i_{X_{\alpha}}\circ p_{X_{\alpha}}\right)\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}.

Since suprema of parallel morphisms in
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
coincide with the suprema of these morphisms in
\mathbf{R}
, it follows from Proposition
2.47
that
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})
in
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
with projection and injection morphisms
p_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})}
and
i_{(X_{\alpha},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\alpha})}
, respectively.
∎

Lemma 5.128
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. The assignment
X\mapsto X^{\mathrm{op}}
extends to a functor
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}:\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
, which acts on monotone relations
v:X\to Y
by
v^{\mathrm{op}}=v^{\dagger}
. Moreover, this functor
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}
is involutory, hence an isomorphism of categories.

Proof.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
,
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
and
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
be preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
. Let
v:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be a monotone relation. Then
v\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}=v={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ v
, hence
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\circ v^{\dagger}=v^{\dagger}=v^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}
, showing that
v^{\dagger}:(Y,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})
is a monotone relation. We check functoriality. We have
\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}^{\mathrm{op}}=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})}=\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}}}
, and if
w:(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
is another monotone relation, we have
(w\circ v)^{\mathrm{op}}=(w\circ v)^{\dagger}=v^{\dagger}\circ w^{\dagger}=v^{\mathrm{op}}\circ w^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Finally, we have
((X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}=(\mathcal{X},{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}}=(\mathcal{X},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
, and
(v^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}=(v^{\dagger})^{\mathrm{op}}=v^{{\dagger}{\dagger}}=v
, so
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}
is involutory.
∎

Definition 5.129
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid, let
X\in\mathbf{R}
be an object, and let
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be a preordered object in
\mathbf{R}
. For any morphism
r:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{R}
, we define
r_{\diamond}:X\to Y
and
r^{\diamond}:Y\to X
as the morphisms in
\mathbf{R}
given by

\displaystyle r_{\diamond}:=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ r;\qquad r^{\diamond}:=r^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})

Lemma 5.130
.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger quantaloid. There are functors
(-)_{\diamond}:\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
and
(-)^{\diamond}:\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
, which are the identity on objects, and which acts on monotone maps
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
by
f\mapsto f_{\diamond}
and
f\mapsto f^{\diamond}
, respectively (cf. Definition
5.129
). Moreover, for each monotone map
f:X\to Y
, the following identities hold:

f_{\diamond}\circ f^{\diamond}\leq\mathrm{id}_{(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})},\qquad f^{\diamond}\circ f_{\diamond}\geq\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}.

Proof.

We first check that
f_{\diamond}
is a monotone relation if
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
is a monotone map between preordered objects of
\mathbf{R}
. We immediately find

({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f_{\diamond}=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f\leq({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f=f_{\diamond}
. By Lemma
5.116
,
f
is also a monotone map
(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})
, whence
f\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\leq({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f
.
Moreover, we have
hence
f_{\diamond}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\leq({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f\leq({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f=f_{\diamond}
.
Next, we check functoriality. For
\mathrm{id}_{X}:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
, we have
(\mathrm{id}_{X})_{\diamond}=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})
, which is indeed the identity monotone relation
\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}
on
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
. Furthermore, given another preordered object
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
and monotone map
g:(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
, we have

\displaystyle(g\circ f)_{\diamond}=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z})\circ g\circ f=g_{\diamond}\circ f=g_{\diamond}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ f=g_{\diamond}\circ f_{\diamond},

so
(-)_{\diamond}
is indeed a functor.

Next we check that
f^{\diamond}:(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
is a monotone relation if
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
is a monotone map between preordered objects. We immediately find
f^{\diamond}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})=f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\leq f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})=f^{\diamond}
. Moreover,
it follows that
({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\circ f^{\diamond}=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\circ f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})=(f\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\leq({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f)^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})=f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\leq f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})=f^{\diamond}
, where the first inequality follows since
f
is monotone.

We proceed by checking functoriality. For
\mathrm{id}_{X}:(\mathcal{X},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
, we have
(\mathrm{id}_{X})^{\diamond}=\mathrm{id}_{X}^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})
, which is indeed the identity monotone relation
\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}
on
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
. Furthermore, given another preordered object
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
and a monotone map
g:(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
, we have

\displaystyle(g\circ f)^{\diamond}=(g\circ f)^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z})=f^{\dagger}\circ g^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z})=f^{\dagger}\circ g^{\diamond}=f^{\dagger}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\circ g^{\diamond}=f^{\diamond}\circ g^{\diamond},

so
(-)^{\diamond}
is indeed a contravariant functor.

Finally, given a monotone map
f:X\to Y
, two direct calculations yield:

f_{\diamond}\circ f^{\diamond}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f\circ f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\leq{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\leq{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}=\mathrm{id}_{(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})}
, and

f^{\diamond}\circ f_{\diamond}=f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f=f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f\geq f^{\dagger}\circ f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\geq{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}
.
∎

We note that if
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{Rel}
, then any monotone relation
r:X\to Y
that has an upper adjoint
s:Y\to X
in
\mathbf{Rel}
, i.e.,
s\circ r\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
r\circ s\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}
, must be of the form
r=f_{\diamond}
for some monotone map
f:X\to Y
, in which case
s=f^{\diamond}

[
33
, Footnote 3]
. This does not hold in general. For instance, take
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{qRel}
. In Lemma
D.193
, we construct an invertible binary relation
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
in
\mathbf{qRel}
that is not a dagger isomorphism, i.e., the inverse
S
of
R
does not equal
R^{\dagger}
. Since
S
is the inverse of
R
, it is its upper adjoint in
\mathbf{qRel}
. When we equip
\mathcal{X}
with the trivial order, then
R
becomes a monotone relation. If a monotone map
F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
such that
F_{\diamond}=R
exists, then it must be equal to
R
for the order on
\mathcal{X}
is trivial. In order for
F
to be a map,
F^{\dagger}
must be its upper adjoint in
\mathbf{qRel}
, but since upper adjoints are unique, we would obtain
R^{\dagger}=F^{\dagger}=S
, which is a contradiction.

Proposition 5.131
.

For any dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{\diamond}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\diamond}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\mathrm{op}}}

Proof.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
be a preordered object in
\mathbf{R}
. Then

((X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}})^{\diamond}=(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})^{\diamond}=(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})=(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}}=((X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}})_{\diamond}.

Let
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be another preordered object in
\mathbf{R}
and let
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
be a monotone map. Then:

\displaystyle[[f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})]^{\mathrm{op}}]^{\diamond}

\displaystyle=[f:(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})]^{\diamond}

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}:(Y,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})

\displaystyle=[{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y}\circ f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})]^{\mathrm{op}}
