\displaystyle=[[f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})]_{\diamond}]^{\mathrm{op}}.\qed

Recall Lemma
5.119
that states that the monoidal product
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y}):=(X\otimes Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
of preordered objects in a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid
(\mathbf{Q},\otimes,I)
is again a preordered object. We now define the monoidal product of monotone relations between preordered objects.

Lemma 5.132
.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
,
(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
,
(W,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W})
and
(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
be preordered objects in a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid (
\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
. Let
r:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(W,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W})
and
s:(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
. Then
r\otimes s:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})\to(W,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{W})\otimes(Z,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z})
is a monotone relation.

Proof.

By a direct calculation:
({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{W}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z})\circ(r\otimes s)=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{W}\circ r)\otimes({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z}\circ r)=r\otimes s=(r\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(s\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})=(r\otimes s)\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y})
.
∎

Proposition 5.133
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid. Then

\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a symmetric monoidal quantaloid if we equip it with a monoidal product
\otimes
as follows:

•

The monoidal product
\otimes
coincides with the monoidal product on
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
as defined in Theorem
5.121
, i.e.,
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\otimes(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})=(X\otimes Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}\otimes{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
;

•

the monoidal product
r\otimes s
of monotone relations
r
and
s
is given by the monoidal product of
r
and
s
in
\mathbf{R}
;

•

the monoidal unit is given by
(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})
;

•

if
\alpha
,
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\sigma
denote the respective associator, left unitor, right unitor and symmetry of
(\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R}),\otimes,(I,\mathrm{id}_{I}))
, then the associator, left unitor, right unitor and symmetry of
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
are given by
\alpha_{\diamond}
,
\lambda_{\diamond}
,
\rho_{\diamond}
, and
\sigma_{\diamond}
, respectively.

Proof.

Since
\otimes
is a symmetric monoidal product on
\mathbf{R}
, it follows from Lemma
5.132
that it induces a bifunctor on
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
. By Theorem
5.121
, the underlying morphisms in
\mathbf{R}
of the components of the associator, left unitor, right unitor and symmetry of
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
are monotone maps, hence the components of
\alpha_{\diamond}
,
\lambda_{\diamond}
,
\rho_{\diamond}
and
\sigma_{\diamond}
are indeed monotone relations. We verify the naturality of these morphisms. So for
i=1,2
, let
(X_{i},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X_{i}})
,
(Y_{i},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y_{i}})
and
(Z_{i},{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Z_{i}})
be preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
, and let
u:X_{1}\to X_{2}
,
v:Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
and
w:Z_{1}\to Z_{2}
be monotone relations.
Then

\displaystyle(\alpha_{X_{2},Y_{2},Z_{2}})_{\diamond}\circ((u\otimes v)\otimes w)

\displaystyle=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X_{2}}\otimes({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y_{2}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z_{2}}))\circ\alpha_{X_{2},Y_{2},Z_{2}}\circ((u\otimes v)\otimes w)

\displaystyle=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X_{2}}\otimes({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y_{2}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z_{2}}))\circ(u\otimes(v\otimes w))\circ\alpha_{X_{1},Y_{1},Z_{1}}

\displaystyle=(u\otimes(v\otimes w))\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X_{1}}\otimes({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Y_{1}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{Z_{1}}))\circ\alpha_{X_{1},Y_{1},Z_{1}}

\displaystyle=(u\otimes(v\otimes w))\circ(\alpha_{X_{1},Y_{1},Z_{1}})_{\diamond},

where we used naturality of
\alpha
is associator of
\mathbf{R}
in the second equality, and the fact that
u
,
v
and
w
, hence also
u\otimes(v\otimes w)
are monotone relations in the third equality. For the unitors and the symmetry the proof goes completely in an analog way. Then, since
\alpha
,
\lambda
,
\rho
, and
\sigma
satisfy the coherence conditions for symmetric monoidal categories, and
(-)_{\diamond}
is a functor, it follows that
\alpha_{\diamond}
,
\lambda_{\diamond}
,
\rho_{\diamond}
, and
\sigma_{\diamond}
satisfy the same coherence conditions. So
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is indeed a symmetric monoidal category. Moreover,
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a quantaloid where the supremum of parallel monotone relations is calculated in
\mathbf{R}
by Lemma
5.126
. Since also the monoidal product of morphism in
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is the same as the monoidal product of morphism in
\mathbf{R}
, which, by assumption is a symmetric monoidal quantaloid, it follows that the monoidal product on
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
preserves suprema in both arguments separately. Thus
(\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R}),\otimes,(I,\mathrm{id}_{I}))
is a symmetric monoidal quantaloid.
∎

Theorem 5.134
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a dagger compact quantaloid with respective unit and counit morphisms
\eta_{X}:I\to X^{*}\otimes X
and
\epsilon_{X}:X\otimes X^{*}\to I
for each object
X
. Then
(\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R}),\otimes,(I,\mathrm{id}_{I}))
is a compact category quantaloid with respective unit and counit morphism
\eta_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}:(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})\to(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})^{*}\otimes(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
and
\epsilon_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})\otimes(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})^{*}\to(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})
given by
\eta_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}:=({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\eta_{X}
and
\epsilon_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}:=\epsilon_{X}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.133
,
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a symmetric monoidal quantaloid.

Let
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})
be a preordered object of
\mathbf{R}
. Since
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}\leq{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
, and
\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
, we have
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}=\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}\leq{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}\leq{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
, whence
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}
, so preorders are idempotent. We will use this in the remainder of proof without mentioning it. Note that by Lemma
2.11
, we have
({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}^{*})^{\dagger}=({\bm{\preccurlyeq}}^{\dagger})^{*}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*}
. Note furthermore that if
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})\to(Y,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{Y})
is an order isomorphism between preordered objects, functoriality of
(-)_{\diamond}
yields that
f_{\diamond}
is also invertible in
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
, and
(f_{\diamond})^{-1}=(f^{-1})_{\diamond}={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ f
. We simply write
f_{\diamond}^{-1}
instead of
(f_{\diamond})^{-1}
or
(f^{-1})_{\diamond}
.
We have to show that the unit and counit satisfy the defining equations of a compact closed category.

\displaystyle(\lambda_{X})_{\diamond}

\displaystyle\circ(\epsilon_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})})\circ(\alpha_{X,X^{*},X})_{\diamond}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}\otimes\eta_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})})\circ(\rho_{X})_{\diamond}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ((\epsilon_{X}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*}))\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ(({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*})\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes(({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\eta_{X}))\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I})\circ\rho_{X}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ(\epsilon_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ(({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*})\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}^{*}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}))\circ(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\eta_{X})\circ\rho_{X}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ(\epsilon_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ(({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}))\circ(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\eta_{X})\circ\rho_{X}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ(\epsilon_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ((\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}))\circ(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\eta_{X})\circ\rho_{X}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{I}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ(\epsilon_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\eta_{X})\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I})\circ\rho_{X}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ(\epsilon_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\eta_{X})\circ\rho_{X}^{-1}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\succcurlyeq}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}

Here, we used the last two equalities in Lemma
2.11
in the third equality. In the fourth equality, we used that
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}
and hence
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}^{-1}
is natural in
X
,
Y
and
Z
, hence

\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}))=(({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}

and

\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}))=((\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1},

which combines to

(({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}))=((\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\otimes{\bm{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\alpha_{X,X^{*},X}^{-1}\circ({\bm{\succcurlyeq}}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})).

Finally, we used naturality of
\lambda
and
\rho
in the sixth equality, and that
\mathbf{R}
is compact closed in the seventh equality.

In a similar way, we obtain
(\rho_{X^{*}})_{\diamond}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})^{*}}\otimes\epsilon_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})})\circ(\alpha_{X^{*},X,X^{*}})_{\diamond}\circ(\eta_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})^{*}})\circ(\lambda_{X^{*}})_{\diamond}^{-1}=\mathrm{id}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}})^{*}}
, hence
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is indeed compact closed.
∎

The embeddings of
\mathbf{PreOrd}
and
\mathbf{MonRel}

Theorem 5.135
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with all small dagger biproducts.
Then there is a fully faithful strong symmetric monoidal functor
`(-):\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
, which:

•

is defined on objects by
(A,\sqsubseteq)\mapsto(`A,`{\sqsubseteq})
;

•

is defined on morphisms by
f\mapsto`f
;

•

for any preordered sets
(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})
and
(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
, the underlying bijections of the coherence morphisms
\varphi:(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})\to`(1,\mathrm{id}_{1})
and
\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})}:`(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\otimes`(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})\to`((A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\times(B,\sqsubseteq_{B}))
are given by the respective coherence bijections
\varphi:I\to`1
and
\varphi_{A,B}:`A\otimes`B\to`(A\times B)
for the strong monoidal functor
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
of Theorem
3.90
.

Proof.

We will use that
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is fully faithful, preserves the dagger, and the map
\mathbf{Rel}(A,B)\to\mathbf{R}(`A,`B)
,
r\mapsto`r
is an order isomorphism (cf. Theorem
3.90
) for each two sets
A
and
B
.

Let
A
be a set and let
\sqsubseteq
be a preorder on
A
. Since
\sqsubseteq
is reflexive, we have
(\alpha,\alpha)\in(\sqsubseteq)
for each
\alpha\in A
, i.e.,
\mathrm{id}_{A}\subseteq(\sqsubseteq)
. It follows that
\mathrm{id}_{`A}=`(\mathrm{id}_{A})\leq`(\sqsubseteq)
, so
`{\sqsubseteq}
is reflexive.

Since
\sqsubseteq
is transitive, we have for each
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in A
that
(\alpha,\beta)\in(\sqsubseteq)
and
(\beta,\gamma)\in(\sqsubseteq)
implies
(\alpha,\gamma)\in(\sqsubseteq)
. Since
(\sqsubseteq\circ\sqsubseteq)=\{(\alpha,\gamma)\in A\times A:(\alpha,\beta)\in(\sqsubseteq)\text{ and }(\beta,\gamma)\in(\sqsubseteq)\text{ for some }\beta\in A\}
it follows that
(\sqsubseteq\circ\sqsubseteq)\subseteq(\sqsubseteq)
. Since
`(\sqsubseteq)\circ`(\sqsubseteq)=`(\sqsubseteq\circ\sqsubseteq)\leq`(\sqsubseteq)
, it follows that
`{\sqsubseteq}
is transitive.

Next, let
(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})
and
(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
be preordered sets, and let
f:A\to B
be a function.
Regarding
f
as a binary relation, we have

\displaystyle f\circ(\sqsubseteq_{A})

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B:(\alpha,\gamma)\in(\sqsubseteq_{A})\text{ and }(\gamma,\beta)\in f\text{ for some }\gamma\in A\}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B:\alpha\sqsubseteq_{A}\gamma\text{ and }f(\gamma)=\beta\text{ for some }\gamma\in A\},

\displaystyle(\sqsubseteq_{B})\circ f

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B:(\alpha,\gamma)\in f\text{ and }(\gamma,\beta)\in(\sqsubseteq_{B})\text{ for some }\gamma\in B\}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B:f(\alpha)=\gamma\text{ and }\gamma\sqsubseteq_{B}\beta\text{ for some }\gamma\in B\}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B:f(\alpha)\sqsubseteq_{B}\beta\}.

Assume that
f
is monotone, and let
(\alpha,\beta)\in f\circ(\sqsubseteq_{A})
. Then there is some
\gamma\in A
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{A}\gamma
and
f(\gamma)=\beta
. By monotonicity of
f
, we have
f(\alpha)\sqsubseteq_{B}f(\gamma)=\beta
, hence
(\alpha,\beta)\in(\sqsubseteq_{B})\circ f
, Thus monotonicity of
f
implies

f\circ(\sqsubseteq_{A})\subseteq(\sqsubseteq_{B})\circ f

(11)

Conversely, assume (
11
) holds. Let
\alpha,\gamma\in A
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{A}\gamma
. Then
(\alpha,f(\beta))\in f\circ(\sqsubseteq_{A})
, so
(\alpha,f(\gamma))\in(\sqsubseteq_{B}\circ f)
implying
f(\alpha)\sqsubseteq_{B}f(\gamma)
. So
f
is monotone. We conclude that
f
is monotone if and only if (
11
) holds if and only if
`f\circ`(\sqsubseteq_{A})\leq`(\sqsubseteq_{B})\circ`f
if and only if
`f:(`A,`{\sqsubseteq_{A}})\to`(B,`{\sqsubseteq_{B}})
is monotone.

Thus,
`(-):\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is well defined on objects and morphisms. Since its action on morphisms is the action of
`(-):\mathbf{Set}\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
on the underlying functions of the morphisms of
\mathbf{PreOrd}
, it follows that
`(-):\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is functorial. Since
`(-):\mathbf{Set}\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
is faithful by Theorem
4.101
, it follows that
`(-):\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is faithful. To show fullness of the latter functor, let let
F:(`A,`{\sqsubseteq_{A}})\to(`B,`{\sqsubseteq_{B}})
be a monotone map. Then, in particular
F:`A\to`B
is a map, hence by Theorem
4.101
, there is a unique function
f:A\to B
such that
F=`f
. Since we showed that monotonicity of
`f
is equivalent to monotonicity of
f
, it follows that
f
must be monotone. Thus,
`(-):\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is a fully faithful functor.

The monoidal unit of
\mathbf{PreOrd}
is
(1,\mathrm{id}_{1})
. Hence,
`(1,\mathrm{id}_{1})=(`1,`\mathrm{id}_{1})=(`1,\mathrm{id}_{`1})=(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})
, which we call is the monoidal unit of
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
. Hence the coherence map
\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{I}:I\to`1
is a monotone map
(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})\to`(1,\mathrm{id}_{1})
. Let
(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})
and
(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
be preordered sets. We show that the coherence map
\varphi_{A,B}:`A\otimes`B\to`(A\times B)
, which is a bijection by Theorem
4.101
, is an order isomorphism
`(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\otimes`(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})\to`((A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\times(B,\sqsubseteq_{B}))
. We note that
(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\times(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})=(A\times B,\sqsubseteq_{A\times B})
where
(a,b)\sqsubseteq_{A\times B}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
if and only if
a\sqsubseteq_{A}a^{\prime}
and
b\sqsubseteq_{B}b^{\prime}
. So, we need to show that
\varphi_{A,B}=\langle\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\alpha}\otimes p_{\beta})\rangle)_{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B}
is an order isomorphism
(`A\otimes`B,`{\sqsubseteq_{A}}\otimes`{\sqsubseteq_{B}})\to(`(A\times B),`{\sqsubseteq_{A\times B}})
. Here,
p_{\alpha}:`A\to I
is the projection on the
\alpha
-th factor of
`A
, and
p_{\beta}:`B\to I
is the projection on the
\beta
-th factor of
`B
. We also denote the projection of
`(A\times B)
on the
(\alpha,\beta)
-th factor by
p_{(\alpha,\beta}):`(A\times B)\to I
.

Fix
(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B
. Using that
\varphi_{A,B}=\langle\lambda_{I}\circ(p_{\gamma}\otimes p_{\delta})\rangle_{(\gamma,\delta)\in A\times B}
, and by applying Lemma
2.26
and (6) of Proposition
2.22
, one easily calculates

p_{(\alpha,\beta)}\circ`{\sqsubseteq_{A\times B}}\circ\varphi_{A,B}=p_{(\alpha,\beta)}\circ\varphi_{A,B}\circ(`{\sqsubseteq}_{A}\otimes`{\sqsubseteq_{B}})
. We conclude that
`{\sqsubseteq}_{A\times B}\circ\varphi_{A,B}=\varphi_{A,B}\circ(`{\sqsubseteq}_{A}\otimes`{\sqsubseteq}_{B})
, what in combination with the fact that
\varphi_{A,B}
is a bijection yields that it is an order isomorphism (cf. Lemma
5.118
).
∎

Theorem 5.136
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a nondegenerate dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with small dagger biproducts. Then the functor
`(-):\mathbf{MonRel}\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
that sends any preordered set
(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})
to
(`A,`{\sqsubseteq_{A}})
and any monotone relation
v:(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\to(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
to
`v
is:

•

a homomorphism of quantaloids;

•

faithful, and also full if
\mathbf{R}
has precisely two scalars;

•

biproduct-preserving;

•

strong symmetric monoidal with coherence isomorphisms given by
\varphi_{\diamond}
and
(\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})})_{\diamond}
, where
\varphi
and
\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})}
are the coherence isomorphisms for the strong monoidal functor
`(-):\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
of Theorem
5.135
.

Proof.

Let
(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})
and
(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
be preordered sets. We first show that
`(-):\mathbf{MonRel}\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is well defined.
By Theorem
5.135
,
(`A,`{\sqsubseteq_{A}})
is a preordered object of
\mathbf{R}
. Let
v:A\to B
be a monotone relation. Then
v\circ(\sqsupseteq_{A})=v=(\sqsupseteq_{B})\circ v
. Using that
`(-)
preserves daggers (cf. Theorem
3.90
), we obtain that
(`{\sqsubseteq}_{A})^{\dagger}=`(\sqsubseteq_{A}^{\dagger})=`{\sqsupseteq}_{A}
, hence
`v\circ(`{\sqsubseteq}_{A})^{\dagger}=`v\circ`{\sqsupseteq}_{A}=`(v\circ\sqsupseteq_{A})=`v=`(\sqsupseteq_{B}\circ v)=`{\sqsupseteq}_{B}\circ`v=(`{\sqsubseteq}_{B})^{\dagger}\circ`v
, which shows that
`v:`(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\to`(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
is a monotone relation. We also obtain
`\mathrm{id}_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})}=`{\sqsupseteq_{A}}=(`{\sqsubseteq}_{A})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{`(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})}
. If
(C,\sqsubseteq_{C})
is another preordered set, and
w:(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})\to(C,\sqsubseteq_{C})
a monotone relation, then it follows from the functoriality of
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
that
`(w\circ v)=`w\circ`v
, so
`(-):\mathbf{MonRel}\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a functor.
Since by Lemma
5.126
, suprema of parallel morphisms in
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
are calculated in
\mathbf{R}
, and
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is a homomorphism of quantaloids (cf. Theorem
3.90
), it follows that
`(-):\mathbf{MonRel}\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is a homomorphism of quantaloids. It now follows immediately from Proposition
2.50
that
`(-)
preserves biproducts.

To show that
`(-)
is a strong symmetric monoidal functor, we use that
(-)_{\diamond}
is a functor, and the associator and unitors of
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
are obtained by applying
(-)_{\diamond}
to the associator and unitors of
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
(cf. Proposition
5.133
). Then it follows that
(\varphi)_{\diamond}
and
(\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})})_{\diamond}
are isomorphisms in
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
that satisfy the same coherence diagrams as
\varphi
and
\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})}
. So, we only need to show that
(\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})})_{\diamond}
is natural in
(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})
and
(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
. Hence, let
v:(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\to(C,\sqsubseteq_{C})
and
w:(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})\to(D,\sqsubseteq_{D})
be monotone relations between preordered sets. We will use that the coherence isomorphism
\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})}
for the symmetric monoidal functor
`(-):\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
equals the coherence isomorphism
\varphi_{A,B}
for
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
as stated in Theorem
5.135
. Then

\displaystyle(\varphi_{(C,\sqsubseteq_{C}),(D,\sqsubseteq_{D})})_{\diamond}\circ(`v\otimes`w)

\displaystyle=`\sqsupseteq_{C\times D}\circ\varphi_{(C,\sqsubseteq_{C}),(D,\sqsubseteq_{D})}\circ(`v\otimes`w)=`\sqsupseteq_{C\times D}\circ\varphi_{C,D}\circ(`v\otimes`w)

\displaystyle=`\sqsupseteq_{C\times D}\circ`(v\times w)\circ\varphi_{A,B}=`(v\times w)\circ`\sqsupseteq_{A\times B}\circ\varphi_{A,B}

\displaystyle=`(v\times w)\circ`\sqsupseteq_{A\times B}\circ\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})}=`(v\times w)\circ(\varphi_{(A,\sqsubseteq_{A}),(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})})_{\diamond}

where in the third equality, we used that
\varphi_{A,B}
is natural in
A
and
B
as coherence isomorphism for the functor
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
. In the fourth equality, we used that
`(v\times w)
is a monotone relation.

Finally, assume that
\mathbf{R}
has only two scalars. Let
w:`(A,\sqsubseteq_{A})\to`(B,\sqsubseteq_{B})
be a monotone relation between preordered objects in
\mathbf{R}
. Theorem
3.90
assures that
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is full, so there is some
v:A\to B
such that
`v=w
. Moreover, since
w
is a monotone relation, we have

(`{\sqsupseteq_{B}})^{\dagger}\circ w=w=w\circ(`{\sqsupseteq_{A}})^{\dagger}
, which translates to
`(\sqsupseteq_{B}\circ v)=`v=`(v\circ\sqsupseteq_{A})
, and since
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{R}
is faithful, we obtain
(\sqsubseteq_{B})\circ v=v=v\circ(\sqsupseteq_{A})
, so
v
is a monotone relation. This shows that
`(-):\mathbf{MonRel}\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
is full.
∎

Lemma 5.137
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloid with dagger biproducts. Let
2
be the two-point set
\{0,1\}
ordered by
\sqsubseteq
via
0\sqsubseteq 1
. Let
\Omega=`2
with projections
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
on the respective zero-th and first component of
\Omega
, and let
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega}=`(\sqsubseteq)
.
Then the following identities hold:

\displaystyle p_{0}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega}=p_{0},

\displaystyle p_{1}\circ{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega}=\top_{\Omega,I},

\displaystyle p_{1}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}=p_{1},

\displaystyle p_{0}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}=\top_{\Omega,I}.

Proof.

We first prove the statement for
\mathbf{Rel}
, that is, we prove that

\displaystyle q_{0}\circ(\sqsubseteq)=q_{0},

\displaystyle q_{1}\circ(\sqsubseteq)=\top_{2,1},

\displaystyle q_{1}\circ(\sqsupseteq)=q_{1},

\displaystyle q_{0}\circ(\sqsupseteq)=\top_{2,1}.

Here, for
\alpha=0,1
,
q_{\alpha}:2\to 1
denotes the canonical projection on the
\alpha
-th factor,
q_{0}=\{(0,*)\}
and
q_{1}=\{(1,*)\}
if we regard
q_{0},q_{1}
as subsets of
2\times 1
.
Then, for each
\alpha\in 2
, we have
(\alpha,*)\in q_{0}\circ\sqsubseteq
is and only if there is some
\beta\in 2
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq\beta
and
(\beta,*)\in q_{0}
. The latter condition forces that
\beta=0
, which forces
\alpha=0
, i.e.,
q_{0}\circ(\sqsubseteq)=q_{0}
, and in a similar way, we obtain
q_{1}\circ(\sqsupseteq)=q_{1}
. For each
\alpha\in 2
, we have
(\alpha,*)\in q_{1}\circ(\sqsubseteq)
if and only if there is some
\beta\in 2
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq\beta
and
(\beta,*)\in q_{1}
. The latter condition forces
\beta=1
, and since
\alpha\sqsubseteq 1
for each
\alpha\in 2
¡ it follows that
q_{1}\circ(\sqsubseteq)=\top_{2,1}
. In a similar way, we find
q_{0}\circ(\sqsupseteq)=\top_{2,1}
.

For the general case, we use that
`2=\Omega
,
`1=I
and
`(\sqsubseteq)={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega}
. By Theorem
3.90
,
`(-)
preserves daggers and dagger biproducts, hence we have
{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}={\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega}^{\dagger}=(`{\sqsubseteq})^{\dagger}=`(\sqsubseteq^{\dagger})=`(\sqsupseteq)
and
`q_{0}=p_{0}
and
`q_{1}=p_{1}
. Since
\mathbf{R}
is affine, the theorem also assures that
`\top_{2,1}=\top_{\Omega,I}
. The statement now follows from functoriality of
`(-)
.
∎

Proposition 5.138
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger compact quantaloid with small dagger biproducts and dagger kernels such that for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
:

(1)

\top_{X,I}
is a zero-monic effect;

(2)

every zero-monic PER on
X
is a equivalence relation on
X
.

Let
2
be the ordinary set
\{0,1\}
ordered by
\sqsubseteq
defined by
0\sqsubseteq 1
. Let
\Omega=`2
with projection on the second factor denoted by
p_{1}
, and let
{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega}=`(\sqsubseteq)
.
Then for each preorderd objects
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
in
\mathbf{R}
, the map

\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\big((X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}),(\Omega,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega})\big)\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})\big((X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X}),(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})\big),\qquad f\mapsto p_{1}\circ f

is a bijection.

Proof.

Note that
\Omega
coincides the
\Omega
in Corollary
4.106
. By that same corollary, we have a bijection

\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})(X,\Omega)\to\mathbf{R}(X,I),\qquad f\mapsto p_{1}\circ f.

Let
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(\Omega,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega})
be a monotone map. By Lemma
5.116
also
f:(X,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X})\to(\Omega,{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega})
is monotone, i.e.,
f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\leq{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}\circ f
.
Then, using Lemma
5.137
, we find
\mathrm{id}_{I}\circ p_{1}\circ f=p_{1}\circ f=p_{1}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}\circ f\geq p_{1}\circ f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}
, which shows that
p_{1}\circ f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})
is a monotone relation. If
g:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(\Omega,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega})
is another monotone map such that
p_{1}\circ f=p_{1}\circ g
, then it follows from Corollary
4.106
that
f=g
, so the map
f\mapsto p_{1}\circ f
in the statement is injective.
We proceed with showing surjectivity. So let
v:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(I,\mathrm{id}_{I})
be a monotone relation. In particular,
v\in\mathbf{R}(X,I)
, hence by Corollary
4.106
there is a map
f:X\to\Omega
such that
p_{1}\circ f=v
. We only need to show that
f
is monotone. First, we show that
\top_{\Omega,I}\circ f
is a zero-mono. So let
r:Y\to X
be a morphism in
\mathbf{R}
such that
\top_{\Omega,I}\circ f\circ r=0_{Y,I}
. Since
\top_{\Omega,I}
is a zero-mono, we obtain
f\circ r=0_{Y,\Omega}
.
Then
0_{Y,X}=f^{\dagger}\circ 0_{Y,\Omega}=f^{\dagger}\circ f\circ r\geq r
, since
f
is a map, and since
0_{Y,X}=\perp_{Y,X}
by Lemma
2.43
, we obtain
r=0_{Y,X}
. So,
\top_{\Omega,I}\circ f
is a zero-mono
X\to I
, and since by Lemma
4.99

\top_{X,I}
is the unique zero-monic effect
X\to I
, we must have
\top_{X,I}=\top_{\Omega,I}\circ f
.
Then, again using Lemma
5.137
, we obtain

p_{0}\circ f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\leq\top_{X,I}=\top_{\Omega,I}\circ f=p_{0}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}\circ f

and

p_{1}\circ f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}=v\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}=v=p_{1}\circ f=p_{1}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}\circ f.

It now follows from (a) of Proposition
2.49
that
f\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{X}\leq{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}\circ f
, so
f
is monotone.
∎

6
Power objects

Dagger quantaloids can be regarded as categorical generalizations of the category
\mathbf{Rel}
, which allows different examples than allegories - other categorical generalizations of
\mathbf{Rel}
. In the theory of allegories, the notion of power objects is very important, since there is a relation between allegories with power objects and topoi - categorical generalizations of the category
\mathbf{Set}
. The following definition is inspired by the definition of power objects in allegories.

Definition 6.139
.

We say that a dagger quantaloid
\mathbf{R}
has
power objects
if the embedding
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{R}
has a right adjoint.

6.1
Existence of power objects

In this subsection, we explore conditions that assure the existence of power objects in dagger quantaloids. We first state a more general theorem for which neither a quantaloid structure nor daggers are necessary. We note that in the theorem below, the object
\Omega
can be interpreted as an object of truth values, and
\omega
can be interpreted as the dagger of a morphism that represents the element ‘true’ in
\Omega
. The proof of the theorem is heavily inspired by the proof of
[
24
, Theorem 9.2]
.

Theorem 6.140
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a compact-closed category and let
(\mathbf{S},\odot,J)
be a symmetric monoidal closed category with internal hom
[-,-]
and evaluation morphism
\mathrm{Eval}_{A,B}:[A,B]\otimes A\to B
for objects
A,B
of
\mathbf{S}
. Let
E:\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{R}
be a strict monoidal functor that is bijective on objects. Assume that there is an object
\Omega\in\mathbf{S}
and an
\mathbf{R}
-morphism
\omega:E(\Omega)\to I
such that for each object
A\in\mathbf{S}
, we have a bijection

\mathbf{S}(A,\Omega)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{R}(E(A),I),\qquad f\mapsto\omega\circ E(f).

(12)

For each object
X\in\mathbf{R}
, let
P(X):=[E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega]
. Then the assignment
X\mapsto P(X)
extends to a functor
P:\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{S}
that is right adjoint to
E
.
The
X
-component
\ni_{X}
of the co-unit of this adjunction is the unique
\mathbf{R}
-morphism
\ni_{X}:EP(X)\to X
such that

\omega\circ E(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega})=\llcorner\ni_{X}\lrcorner.

(13)

Before we prove the theorem, we need some lemmas. The first one follows directly from the monoidal closure of
\mathbf{S}
:

Lemma 6.141
.

For any
A
in
\mathbf{S}
and each
X
in
\mathbf{R}
, we have a bijection

\mathbf{S}(A,P(X))\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{S}(A\otimes E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega),\qquad f\mapsto\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}\circ(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})}).

We construct the counit of the theorem in the second lemma.

Lemma 6.142
.

For each
X
in
\mathbf{R}
there is a unique morphism
\ni_{X}:EP(X)\to X
in
\mathbf{R}
such that (
13
) holds.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}
is a
\mathbf{S}
-morphism
P(X)\otimes E^{-1}(X^{*})\to\Omega
, it follows from the assumption (
12
) that
\omega\circ E(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega})
is an
\mathbf{R}
-morphism
E(P(X)\otimes E^{-1}(X^{*}))\to I
. Since
E
is strict monoidal, we have that
\omega\circ J(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega})
is an
\mathbf{R}
-morphism
EP(X)\otimes X^{*}\to I
. The existence of
\ni_{X}
such that (
13
) holds follows now from Lemma
2.8
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.140
.

Let
A
be an object of
\mathbf{S}
and let
X
an object of
\mathbf{S}
. We need to show that for each
\mathbf{R}
-morphism
v:E(A)\to X
there is a unique
\mathbf{S}
-morphism
f_{v}:A\to P(X)
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces EP(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f_{v})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ni_{X}}

We define
f_{v}
in steps. Since
v\in\mathbf{R}(E(A),X)
, it follows from Lemma
2.8
that
\llcorner v\lrcorner\in\mathbf{R}(E(A)\otimes X^{*},I)
. Since
E
is strict monoidal and bijective on objects, we have
\llcorner v\lrcorner\in\mathbf{R}(E(A\otimes E^{-1}(X^{*})),I)
. Hence, by the assumption (
12
), there is a unique
\mathbf{S}
-morphism
k_{v}\in\mathbf{S}(A\otimes E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega)
such that

\omega\circ E(k_{v})=\llcorner v\lrcorner.

(14)

Now, by Lemma
6.141
, there is a unique
f_{v}\in\mathbf{S}(A,P(X))
such that

k_{v}=\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}\circ(f_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})}).

(15)

We check that the diagram in the statement commutes.
We have

\displaystyle\llcorner\ni_{X}\circ E(f_{v}))\lrcorner

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{X}\circ((\ni_{X}\circ E(f_{v}))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{X}\circ(\ni_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\circ(E(f_{v})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\llcorner\ni_{X}\lrcorner\circ(E(f_{v})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\omega\circ E(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega})\circ(E(f_{v})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\omega\circ E\big(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}\circ(f_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})})\big)

\displaystyle=\omega\circ E(k_{v})

\displaystyle=\llcorner v\lrcorner,

where we used (
13
) proven in Lemma
6.142
in the fourth equality, functoriality of
E
and the fact that
E
is strict monoidal in the fifth equality (note that
E(\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})})=\mathrm{id}_{EE^{-1}(X^{*})}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
), the definition of
f_{v}
, i.e., equation (
15
), in the penultimate equality, and the definition of
k_{v}
, i.e., equation (
14
) in the last equality. It now follows from Lemma
2.8
that
\ni_{X}\circ E(f_{v})=v
, i.e., the diagram commutes. Next, we check that
f_{v}
is the unique
\mathbf{S}
-morphism for which the diagram commutes. So assume that
g:A\to P(X)
is a
\mathbf{S}
-morphism such that
\ni_{X}\circ E(g)=v
. Then
\ni_{X}\circ E(g)=\ni_{X}\circ E(f_{v})
, hence

\displaystyle\omega\circ E(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})})

\displaystyle=\omega\circ E(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega})\circ(E(g)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\llcorner\ni\lrcorner\circ(E(g)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{X}\circ(\ni_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\circ(E(g)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{X}\circ((\ni_{X}\circ E(g))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{X}\circ((\ni_{X}\circ E(f_{v}))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{X}\circ(\ni_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\circ(E(f_{v})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\llcorner\ni_{X}\lrcorner\circ(E(f_{v})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\omega\circ E(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X),\Omega})\circ(E(f_{v})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle=\omega\circ E(\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}\circ(f_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})}),

where we used functoriality of
E
and the fact that
E
is strict monoidal in the first and last equalities, whereas we used Lemma
6.142
in the second and penultimate equalities. It now follows from the assumption (
12
) that

\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})})=\mathrm{Eval}_{E^{-1}(X^{*}),\Omega}\circ(f_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E^{-1}(X^{*})}).

We can now apply Lemma
6.141
to conclude that
g=f_{v}
.
∎

Corollary 6.143
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger compact quantaloid with small dagger biproducts and dagger kernels such that for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
:

(1)

\top_{X,I}
is a zero-monic effect;

(2)

every zero-monic PER on
X
is an equivalence relation on
X
.

If
\mathbf{S}=\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
is symmetric monoidal closed, then the embedding
E:\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{R}
has a right adjoint
P
.

More precisely, if
\Omega=I\oplus I
, and
\omega:\Omega\to I
be the projection of
\Omega
onto the second factor, and if
[-,-]
and
\mathrm{Eval}
denote the internal hom and the evaluation of
\mathbf{S}
, respectively, then
P
is defined on objects
X
of
\mathbf{S}
by
P(X)=[X^{*},\Omega]
. The
X
-component of the counit
\ni
is the unique morphism
\ni_{X}:P(X)\to X
satisfying
\omega\circ\mathrm{Eval}_{X^{*},\Omega}=\llcorner\ni_{X}\lrcorner
.

Proof.

This follows from combining Corollary
4.106
and Theorem
6.140
, taking
E
to be the inclusion and
\omega=p_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 6.144
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be an affine dagger compact quantaloid with small dagger biproducts and dagger kernels such that for each object
X
of
\mathbf{R}
:

(1)

\top_{X,I}
is a zero-monic effect;

(2)

every zero-monic PER on
X
is an equivalence relation on
X
.

If
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
is symmetric monoidal closed, then the functor
(-)_{\diamond}:\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})
has a right adjoint
D
.

More precisely, let
(\Omega,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega})=`(2,\sqsubseteq)
, where the order
\sqsubseteq
on
2=\{0,1\}
is determined by
0\sqsubseteq 1
. Let
\omega:\Omega\to I
be the projection of
\Omega
onto the second factor. Let
[-,-]
and
\mathrm{Eval}
denote the internal hom and the evaluation of
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})
. Then
D
is defined on objects
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
by
D(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})=[(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{*},(\Omega,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega})]
. The
(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
-component of the counit
\ni
is the unique morphism
\ni_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}:D(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})
satisfying
\omega\circ\mathrm{Eval}_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})^{*},(\Omega,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega})}=\llcorner\ni_{(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})}\lrcorner
.

Proof.

For each monotone map
f:(X,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{X})\to(\Omega,{\bm{\preccurlyeq}}_{\Omega})
, we have
\omega\circ E(f)=p_{1}\circ f_{\diamond}=p_{1}\circ{\bm{\succcurlyeq}}_{\Omega}\circ f=p_{1}\circ f
, where the last equality follows from Lemma
5.137
. Then the statement follows directly from Proposition
5.138
and Theorem
6.140
, where we take
E=(-)_{\diamond}
and
\omega=p_{1}
.
∎

6.2
Examples

We provide some examples of adjunctions obtained via Theorem
6.140
or one of its corollaries. The first example is a direct application of the theorem.

Example 6.145
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial unital commutative quantale. Let
\mathbf{R}:=V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
, which is dagger compact as stated in Theorem
B.169
. Furthermore, we take
\mathbf{S}:=\mathbf{Set}
, which is cartesian closed.
Then the functor
E=(-)_{\circ}:\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{R}
obtained by restricting the functor with the same name in Definition
B.163
to
\mathbf{Set}
has a right adjoint whose action on objects sends every set
X
to its
V
-valued powerset

V^{X}
.

This follows from Theorem
6.140
by taking
\Omega=V
, and by choosing
\omega:V\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow 1
to be the function
V\times 1\to V
,
(v,*)\mapsto v
.
We only need to show that
\mathbf{Set}(X,V)\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}(X,1)
,
f\mapsto\omega\bullet f_{\circ}
is a bijection.
Indeed, for each set
X
, each function
f:X\to V
, and each
x\in X
, we have

(\omega\bullet f_{\circ})(x,*)=\bigvee_{v\in V}\omega(v,*)\cdot f_{\circ}(x,v)=\omega(f(x),*)\cdot f_{\circ}(x,f(x))=f(x)\cdot e=f(x).

As a consequence, if
f,g:X\to V
are distinct functions, then
f(x)\neq g(x)
for some
x\in X
, hence
(\omega\bullet f_{\circ})(x,*)=f(x)\neq g(x)=(\omega\bullet g_{\circ})(x,*)
, showing that
\omega\bullet f_{\circ}\neq\omega\bullet g_{\circ}
, i.e.,
f\mapsto\omega\bullet f_{\circ}
is injective. For surjectivity, let
r:X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow 1
be a
V
-relation, so a function
X\times 1\to V
. Let
f:X\to V
be the function
x\mapsto r(x,*)
. Then for each
(x,*)\in X\times 1
, we have
(\omega\bullet f_{\circ})(x,*)=f(x)=r(x,*)
, so
r=\omega\bullet f_{\circ}
, hence we indeed have a bijection.

As the special case
V=2
of the previous example, we obtain the ordinary power set functor. We can also obtain this functor by applying one of corollaries.

Example 6.146
.

It is well known that
\mathbf{R}:=\mathbf{Rel}
has small dagger biproducts. It is also a dagger kernel category. Since zero-monic PERs in
\mathbf{Rel}
are equivalence relations (cf. Lemma
D.196
), and since it is straightforward to see that the maximal binary relation
\top_{X,1}:X\to 1
is zero-monic, we can apply Corollary
6.143
to conclude that the embedding
E:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Rel}
has a right adjoint
P
, the covariant power set functor.

In an almost similar way, we can derive the existence of a quantum power set functor.

Example 6.147
.

Let
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{qRel}
and
\mathbf{S}=\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})=\mathbf{qSet}
. The former category is dagger compact
[
29
, Theorem 3.6]
, the latter category is symmetric monoidal closed
[
29
, Theorem 9.1]
. By construction,
\mathbf{qRel}
has all small dagger biproducts (see Section
D
). Furthermore,
\mathbf{qRel}
has dagger kernels (cf. Theorem
D.199
), for each object, the top effect is a zero-mono (cf. Proposition
D.194
), and any zero-monic PER is an equivalence relation (cf. Proposition

D.198
). Hence, we can apply Corollary
6.143
to conclude that the embedding
\mathcal{E}:\mathbf{qSet}\to\mathbf{qRel}
has a right adjoint
\mathcal{P}
, which we call the
quantum power set functor
.

Example 6.148
.

If
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{Rel}
, then
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})=\mathbf{PreOrd}
and
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})=\mathbf{MonRel}
. It is well known that
\mathbf{PreOrd}
is cartesian closed. In Example
6.146
, we specified conditions for
\mathbf{Rel}
that allows us to apply Corollary
6.144
, assuring the existence of a right adjoint
D
to the functor
(-)_{\diamond}:\mathbf{PreOrd}\to\mathbf{MonRel}
, which is the lower set functor.

Example 6.149
.

If
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{qRel}
, then
\mathbf{PreOrd}(\mathbf{R})=\mathbf{qPreOrd}
and
\mathbf{MonRel}(\mathbf{R})=\mathbf{qMonRel}
. In
[
24
, Theorem 8.3]
, it was shown that the related category
\mathbf{qPOS}
of quantum posets is symmetric monoidal closed. The proof of this theorem can be simplified to obtain a proof of the symmetric monoidal closure of
\mathbf{qPreOrd}
. In Example
6.147
, we specified conditions for
\mathbf{qRel}
that allows us to apply Corollary
6.144
, assuring that the functor
(-)_{\diamond}:\mathbf{qPreOrd}\to\mathbf{qMonRel}
has a right adjoint
\mathcal{D}
, which we call the
quantum lower set functor
.

6.3
The reconstruction of internal homsets

In
[
29
]
, Kornell showed that
\mathbf{qSet}=\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{qRel})
satisfies properties that strongly resemble the axioms of an elementary topos.

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a dagger compact quantaloid and let
\mathbf{S}=\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{S})
. In the previous section, we explored conditions that assure that the embedding
\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{R}
has a right adjoint, which relied on the assumption that
\mathbf{S}
is symmetric monoidal closed. In this section, assuming some extra mild conditions, we prove the converse, namely that
\mathbf{S}
is symmetric monoidal closed provided that the embedding
\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{R}
has a right adjoint
P
.

We first make the following definition:

Definition 6.150
.

Let
(\mathbf{S},\otimes,I)
be a semicartesian category. For any two objects
X,Y\in\mathbf{S}
we define the canonical projections
p_{X}:X\otimes Y\to X
and
p_{Y}:X\otimes Y\to Y
by
p_{X}:=\rho_{X}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes!_{Y})
and
p_{Y}:=\lambda_{Y}\circ(!_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y})
. Then we call a morphism
f:X\to Y

classical
if there is a morphism
g:X\to X\otimes Y
such that
f=p_{Y}\circ g
and
\mathrm{id}_{X}=p_{X}\circ g
.

We will now state the main theorem of this section. For the remainder of this section, we will assume that the conditions in the theorem hold. Note that by Lemma
4.98

\mathbf{S}
is a monoidal subcategory of
\mathbf{R}

Theorem 6.151
.

Let
(\mathbf{R},\otimes,I)
be a dagger compact quantaloid, and let
\mathbf{S}=\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{R})
. If

(1)

\mathbf{S}
is semicartesian when regarded as a monoidal subcategory of
\mathbf{R}
(cf. Lemma
4.98
);

(2)

The embedding
J:\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{R}
has a right adjoint
P
with unit
\{\cdot\}
and counit
\ni
;

(3)

There exists an object
\Omega
of
\mathbf{S}
and a morphism
\mathrm{true}:I\to\Omega
such that
\mathbf{S}(X,\Omega)\to\mathbf{R}(X,I)
,
f\mapsto\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f
is a bijection;

(4)

\mathbf{S}
has pullbacks;

(5)

For each object
X
and each subobject
m:A\to X
in
\mathbf{S}
there is a unique classical morphism
\chi_{A}:X\to\Omega
such that the diagram below is a pullback square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{true}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{A}}

Then
\mathbf{S}
is symmetric monoidal closed.

Our proof is essentially the proof that power objects in a topos imply the existence of exponential objects, see for instance Section IV.2 of
[
34
]
, which we followed quite closely. We first need some lemmas.

Lemma 6.152
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{S}
. Then there exists a unique classical morphism
\sigma_{X}:P(X)\to\Omega
such that
the following diagram is a pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\cdot\}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{true}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{X}}

Proof.

This follows directly from the fifth property in the statement of Theorem
6.151
.
∎

Lemma 6.153
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{S}
. Then there is a unique morphism
\bar{\ni}_{X}:P(X)\otimes X^{*}\to\Omega
such that
\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ\bar{\ni}_{X}=\llcorner\ni_{X}\lrcorner
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{R}
is dagger compact, we can take the coname
\llcorner\ni_{X}\lrcorner:P(X)\otimes X^{*}\to I
of
\ni_{X}:P(X)\to X
. Then, by the bijection
\mathbf{S}(X,\Omega)\to\mathbf{R}(X,I)
,
f\mapsto\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f
there is a unique morphism
\bar{\ni}_{X}:P(X)\otimes X^{*}\to\Omega
in
\mathbf{S}
such that
\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ\bar{\ni}_{X}=\llcorner\ni_{X}\lrcorner
.
∎

Lemma 6.154
.

Let
f:X\otimes Y^{*}\to\Omega
be a morphism in
\mathbf{S}
. Then there exists a unique morphism
\hat{f}:X\to P(Y)
in
\mathbf{S}
such that
\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\hat{f}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})=f
.

Proof.

By the third assumption in Theorem
6.151
, we have a bijection

\mathbf{S}(X\otimes Y^{*},\Omega)\to\mathbf{R}(X\otimes Y^{*},I),\qquad f\mapsto\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f.

By Lemma
2.8
, we also have a bijection

\mathbf{R}(X,Y)\to\mathbf{R}(X\otimes Y^{*},I),\qquad g\mapsto\llcorner g\lrcorner.

By the second assumption of Theorem
6.151
, we have a bijection

\mathbf{S}(X,P(Y))\to\mathbf{R}(X,Y),\qquad h\mapsto\ni_{Y}\circ h.

So any morphism
f:X\otimes Y^{*}\to\Omega
in
\mathbf{S}
corresponds to a unique morphism
\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f:X\otimes Y^{*}\to I
in
\mathbf{R}
, for which there is a unique morphism
g:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{R}
such that
\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f=\llcorner g\lrcorner
. By the last bijection, there is a unique
\hat{f}:X\to P(Y)
in
\mathbf{S}
such that
\ni_{Y}\circ\hat{f}=g
. Hence,
\hat{f}
is the unique morphism in
\mathbf{S}
such that
\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f=\llcorner\ni_{Y}\circ\hat{f}\lrcorner
.
We have
\llcorner\ni_{Y}\circ\hat{f}\lrcorner=\epsilon_{Y}\circ((\ni_{Y}\circ\hat{f})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}=\epsilon_{Y}\circ(\ni_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(\hat{f}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})=\llcorner\ni_{Y}\lrcorner\circ(\hat{f}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})=\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\hat{f}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})
, where we used Lemma
6.153
in the last equality. Thus we obtain
\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f=\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\hat{f}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})
, hence the statement follows from the bijection
f\mapsto\mathrm{true}^{\dagger}\circ f
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.151
.

Following
[
34
]
, we assume that the associativity isomorphisms are identities to simplify the notation.
Consider objects
X
and
Y
of
\mathbf{S}
. In order to construct an object
Y^{X}
that will be the inner hom of
\mathbf{S}
, we apply Lemma
6.154
to define:

\displaystyle v:=\widehat{\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}\otimes Y}},\qquad u:=\widehat{\sigma_{Y}\circ v},\qquad k:=\widehat{\mathrm{true}\circ!_{I\otimes X}}.

That is,
v:P(X^{*}\otimes Y)\to P(Y)
,
u:P(X^{*}\otimes Y)\to P(X^{*})
, and
k:I\to P(X^{*})
are the respective unique morphisms in
\mathbf{S}
such that

\displaystyle\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(v\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}\otimes Y};

\displaystyle\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}}\circ(u\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{Y}\circ v;

\displaystyle\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}}\circ(k\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{true}\circ!_{I\otimes X}.

We now define
Y^{X}
as the pullback of
u
and
k
, so the following diagram is a pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X^{*}\otimes Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X^{*}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Y^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

In order to construct the evaluation map
e:Y^{X}\otimes X\to Y
, we need to prove that

\mathrm{true}\circ!_{Y^{X}\otimes X}=\sigma_{Y}\circ v\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}).

(16)

Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{X}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X^{*}\otimes Y)\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X^{*})\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Y^{X}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\cdot\}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{I\otimes X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{true}}

Here, the left square commutes, since it is the definition of
Y^{X}
tensored with
X
. The upper middle square commutes by definition of u, the right square commutes by definition of
\sigma_{Y}
and the lower middle diagram commutes by definition of
k
. Since
!_{I\otimes X}\circ(!_{Y^{X}\otimes X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})=!_{Y^{X}\otimes X}
, it follows that (
16
) indeed holds. Thus, we have the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{X}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Y^{X}\otimes X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\cdot\}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{true}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{Y}}

and since the square is a pullback square in
\mathbf{S}
, there must be a unique morphism
e:Y^{X}\otimes X\to Y
such that the diagram commutes.

Next, for another object
Z
of
\mathbf{S}
and a morphism
f:Z\otimes X\to Y
in
\mathbf{S}
, we claim that there is a unique morphism
g:Z\to Y^{X}
in
\mathbf{S}
such that
e\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})=f
. To construct
g
, we first consider the morphism

\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\{\cdot\}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}):Z\otimes X\otimes Y^{*}\to\Omega.

By Lemma
6.154
, there is a unique morphism
h:Z\to P(X^{*}\otimes Y)
in
\mathbf{S}
such that

\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\{\cdot\}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})=\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}\otimes Y}\circ(h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}).

By definition of
v
, we obtain

\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\{\cdot\}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})=\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(v\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}),

whence, using Lemma
6.154
,

\{\cdot\}_{Y}\circ f=v\circ(h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}).

(17)

Now, we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}}\circ(k\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ(!_{Z}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{true}\circ!_{I\otimes X}\circ(!_{Z}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{true}\circ!_{Z\otimes X}=\mathrm{true}\circ!_{Y}\circ f

\displaystyle=\sigma_{Y}\circ\{\cdot\}_{Y}\circ f=\sigma_{Y}\circ v\circ(h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}}\circ(u\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ(h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}),

where the first equality follows from the definition of
k
, the fourth equality from the definition of
\sigma_{Y}
, the fifth equality from (
17
), and the last equality from the definition of
u
. Lemma
6.154
now yields
k\circ!_{Z}=u\circ h
. Note that automatically we have
!_{Y^{X}}\circ g=!_{Z}
, so by definition of
Y^{X}
as the pullback of
u
and
k
, it follows that there is a unique morphism
g:Z\to Y^{X}
in
\mathbf{S}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X^{*}\otimes Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X^{*}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Y^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

We verify that
e\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})=f
.

\displaystyle e\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})

\displaystyle=(\ni_{Y})\circ\{\cdot\}_{Y}\circ e\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})=(\ni_{Y})\circ v\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})

\displaystyle=(\ni_{Y})\circ v\circ(h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})=(\ni_{Y})\circ\{\cdot\}_{Y}\circ f=f,

where in the first and last equalities we used the triangle identities of the adjunction
J\dashv P
, while the second equality follows by definition of
e
, the third equality follows by definition of
g
, and the penultimate equality follows from equality (
17
).
Finally, assume that
g^{\prime}:Z\to Y^{X}
is another morphism in
\mathbf{S}
such that
e\circ(g^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X})=f
. Then:

\displaystyle\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}\otimes Y}\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ v\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\{\cdot\}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(e\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\{\cdot\}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ(\{\cdot\}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(e\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(g^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{Y}\circ v\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(g^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\ni}_{X^{*}\otimes Y}\circ(m\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})\circ(g^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})

where we used the definition of
v
in the first and last equalities, and the definition of
e
in the second and penultimate equalities. Using Lemma
6.154
, we obtain
m\circ g=m\circ g^{\prime}
. Since we have
!_{Y^{X}}\circ g^{\prime}=!_{Z}
, and by definition
g
is the unique morphism in
\mathbf{S}
such that
m\circ g=h
and
!_{Y^{X}}\circ g=!_{Z}
, it follows that
g^{\prime}=g
.
∎

7
Conclusions and future work

We introduced symmetric monoidal quantaloids as a categorical structure that equips quantaloids with a symmetric monoidal structure, and that generalizes the category
\mathbf{Rel}
. Our prime example is the category
\mathbf{qRel}
of quantum sets and binary relations; our main motivation is the internalization of mathematical structures in this category, which corresponds to the quantization of these structures. We showed that symmetric monoidal quantaloids form a framework in which one can internalize functions and partially ordered structures. For dagger symmetric monoidal quantaloids
\mathbf{Q}
, there are still other connections to be investigated such as limits and subobjects in
\mathbf{Maps}(\mathbf{Q})
. It might be that these concepts are best investigated in a
2
-dimensional setting by combining
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{Q})
in a double category. Furthermore, we note that that
\mathbf{qSet}
has properties resembling the axioms of topoi
[
29
]
.
\mathbf{qSet}
is the noncommutative generalization of the prime example of a topos, namely the category
\mathbf{Set}
of sets and functions. This suggests the existence of notions of
quantum allegories
and
quantum topoi
with
\mathbf{qRel}
and
\mathbf{qSet}
as prime examples, respectively. The connection between power objects in
\mathbf{Q}
and the monoidal closure of
\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{Q})
in the last section also points towards a notion that generalizes power allegories. We hope that this work eventually contributes to finding these notions and generalizations.

Appendix A
Suplattices

In this article, we consider quantaloids, which we recall are
\mathbf{Sup}
-enriched categories, where
\mathbf{Sup}
denotes the category of complete lattices and supremum-preserving maps. We summarize the categorical properties of
\mathbf{Sup}
. For proofs, we refer to
[
10
, Section 2.1]
.

Firstly, given any two complete lattices
X
and
Y
, the (external) homset
\mathbf{Sup}(X,Y)
is a complete lattice when ordered pointwise. Hence, also
X\otimes Y:=\mathrm{Sup}(X,Y^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}

is a complete lattice. When equipped with
\otimes
,
\mathbf{Sup}
becomes symmetric monoidal closed category whose internal hom is given by the external hom. The monoidal unit
I
of the monoidal structure is given by the two point lattice
\{0,1\}
ordered by
0<1
.
As a consequence,
\mathbf{Sup}
is enriched over itself
[
4
, Proposition 6.2.6]
. Given a collection
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of complete lattices, their set-theoretic product
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
is a complete lattice when ordered coordinate-wise. The canonical projections
p_{\beta}:\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}\to X_{\beta}
preserve all suprema, hence
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
is the product of
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
. Since
\mathbf{Sup}
is a quantaloid, it follows from Proposition
2.47
it has all small biproducts. Explicitly, the canonical injection
i_{\beta}:X_{\beta}\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}
is given by
x\mapsto(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
, where

x_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}x,&\alpha=\beta,\\
\perp,&\alpha\neq\beta.\end{cases}

Since
\mathbf{Sup}
is symmetric monoidal closed and has small biproducts, it follows from Proposition
2.34
that
\mathbf{Sup}
is an infinitely distributive symmetric monoidal category. Using Theorem
3.61
, we conclude:

Theorem A.155
.

\mathbf{Sup}
is a symmetric monoidal closed quantaloid with all small biproducts.

We note that
\mathbf{Sup}
is not a dagger category. Every morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{Sup}
has an upper Galois adjoint
g:Y\to X
, but this adjoint generally preserves infima instead of suprema, hence in general it is not a morphism of
\mathbf{Sup}
.

Appendix B
Quantale-valued relations

In this section, we explore the properties of the category
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
of quantale-valued binary relations. We refer to
[
18
]
for background information.

B.1
Quantales

Quantales are partially ordered structures that can be regarded as quantum generalizations of locales. They are also instrumental in fuzzy mathematics, where one considers sets where the membership relations does not take binary values, but values in a quantale
V
. This can be described in the setting of the category of sets and binary relations with values in
V
, which forms a dagger compact quantaloid for certain classes of quantales.

Definition B.156
.

A
quantale

V
is a complete lattice equipped with an associative binary relation
\cdot:V\times V\to V
such that

\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}x_{\alpha}\right)\cdot y=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}x_{\alpha}\cdot y,\qquad y\cdot\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}x_{\alpha}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}y\cdot x_{\alpha}

for each set-indexed family
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of elements in
V
and each
y\in V
. We denote the least and greatest element of
V
by
\perp
and
\top
, respectively. We call
V
:

•

nontrivial
if
\top\neq\perp
;

•

unital
if
V
has an element
e
such that
e\cdot x=x=x\cdot e
for each
x\in V
;

•

affine
or
integral
if
V
is unital and
e
is the largest element of
V
;

•

commutative
if
x\cdot y=y\cdot x
for each
x,y\in V
;

•

idempotent
if
x\cdot x=x
for each
x\in V
.

The proofs of the following two lemmas are straightforward.

Lemma B.157
.

Let
V
be a quantale. Then
{\perp}\cdot x=\perp=x\cdot{\perp}
for each
x\in V
.

Lemma B.158
.

Let
V
be a unital quantale. Then
V
is nontrivial if and only if
e\neq\perp
.

Frames are special cases of quantales as follows from the following well-known result.

Lemma B.159
.

Let
V
be a unital quantale. Then it is a frame if it is affine and idempotent, in which case it is commutative in particular.

B.2
Quantale-valued relations

Definition B.160
.

Let
V
be a unital quantale. Let
X
and
Y
be sets. Then a function
r:X\times Y\to V
is called a
V
-
valued relation
or simply a
V
-
relation
from
X
to
Y
, in which case we write
r:X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y
. Sets and
V
-valued relations form a category
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
if we define the composition
s\bullet r
of
V
-valued relations
r:X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y
and
s:Y\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Z
by

(s\bullet r)(x,z):=\bigvee_{y\in Y}r(x,y)\cdot s(y,z),

and the identity morphism on a set
X
as the
V
-relation
e_{X}:X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow X
defined by

e_{X}(x,x^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}e,&x=x^{\prime},\\
\perp,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
becomes a quantaloid if we order parallel
V
-valued relations
r,s:X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y
by
r\leq s
if and only if
r(x,y)\leq s(x,y)
for each
x\in X
and
y\in Y
. The supremum
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}
of any set-indexed family
(r_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of parallel
V
-valued relations
X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y
is calculated via

\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}\right)(x,y)=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}r_{\alpha}(x,y)

for each
x\in X
and each
y\in Y
.

If, in addition,
V
is commutative, then
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is a dagger quantaloid where for any
V
-relation
r:X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y
we define
r^{\dagger}:Y\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow X
as the function
Y\times X\to V
given by
(y,x)\mapsto r(x,y)
.

Proposition B.161
.

Let
V
be an affine commutative quantale. If
V
is not a frame, then
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is not an allegory

Proof.

Assume that
V
is not a frame, but that
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is an allegory. Since
V
is not a frame, it follows from Lemma
B.159
that
V
cannot be idempotent, so there must be some
v\in V
such that
v\cdot v\neq v
. Since
V
is affine, we have
v\leq e
, hence
v\cdot v\leq v\cdot e=v
, hence, we must have
v\nleq v\cdot v
. Now, we cannot have
v\leq v\cdot v\cdot v
, because otherwise
v\leq v\cdot v\cdot v\leq v\cdot v\cdot e=v\cdot v
. Thus
v\nleq v\cdot v\cdot v
.

Now, since
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is an allegory by assumption, we must have
r\leq r\bullet r^{\dagger}\bullet r
for any
V
-relation
r

[
19
, Lemma A.3.2.1]
.
As a consequence, by taking
r:1\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow 1
given by
r(*,*)=v
for some
v\in V
, we must have that
v\leq v\cdot v\cdot v
, which gives a contradiction.
∎

B.3
Dagger biproducts

The proof of the following proposition is straightforward when using the alternative characterization of biproducts in quantaloids as presented in Proposition
2.47
.

Proposition B.162
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial commutative unital quantale. Then
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
has all small dagger biproducts. To be precise, the biproduct of a set-indexed family
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of sets is the disjoint union
X:=\biguplus_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
, so
X=\{(\alpha,x):\alpha\in A,x\in X_{\alpha}\}
. For each
\alpha\in A
, the canonical injection
i_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow X
is the
V
-relation given by

i_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\begin{cases}e,&x^{\prime}=(\alpha,x),\\
\perp,&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
x\in X_{\alpha}
and each
x^{\prime}\in X
. The canonical projection
p_{\alpha}:X\to X_{\alpha}
is given by
p_{\alpha}=i_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
.

B.4
Monoidal structure

In order to define a monoidal structure on
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
, we need the following functor.

Definition B.163
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial unital quantale, i.e.,
e\neq\perp
. Then we have an embedding
(-)_{\circ}:\mathbf{Rel}\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
that is the identity on objects and which sends every binary relation
r:X\to Y
to
r_{\circ}:X\times Y\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow V
given by

r_{\circ}(x,y)=\begin{cases}e,&(x,y)\in r,\\
\perp,&(x,y)\notin r.\end{cases}

Lemma B.164
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial unital quantale. Then

(-)_{\circ}:\mathbf{Rel}\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is a faithful homomorphism of dagger quantaloids.

After defining the monoidal structure on
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
, we can also define the functor
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
as defined in Section
3.4
, which we will prove below to be natural dagger isomorphic to
(-)_{\circ}
.

\mathbf{Rel}
is a symmetric monoidal category with the usual product
\times
of sets as a monoidal product. If
V
is commutative, then we can show that
\times
can also be extended to a functor
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}\times V
-
\mathbf{Rel}\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
.

Lemma B.165
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial commutative unital quantale. Then
\times
becomes a bifunctor on
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
if for
V
-relations
r:X_{1}\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y_{1}
and
s:X_{2}\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y_{2}
we define
r\times s:X_{1}\times X_{2}\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow Y_{1}\times Y_{2}
as the function
(X_{1}\times X_{2})\times(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})\to V
given by

(r\times s)\big((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})\big):=r(x_{1},y_{1})\cdot s(x_{2},y_{2}).

Proposition B.166
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial commutative unital quantale. Then
(V
-
\mathbf{Rel},\times,1,\alpha_{\circ},\lambda_{\circ},\rho_{\circ},\sigma_{\circ})
becomes a symmetric monoidal category, where
\alpha
,
\lambda
,
\rho
,
\sigma
denote the associator, left unitor, right unitor and symmetry of
\mathbf{Rel}
.

Lemma B.167
.

Let
V
be a commutative unital quantale. Then:

•

V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is nondegenerate if and only if
V
is nontrivial;

•

V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is affine if and only if
V
is nontrivial and affine.

Proof.

Any element of
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}(1,1)
is a function
1\times 1\to V
, and since
1\times 1\cong 1
, we obtain a bijection between
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}(1,1)
and
V
, from which the first statement follows. This bijection is actually an order isomorphism as follows from the fact that the order on
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}(1,1)
is the pointwise order. Hence,
Then
e_{1}:1\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow 1
is the function
(*,*)\mapsto e
, which is clearly the greatest element of
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}(1,1)
if and only if
e=\top
, i.e., if
V
is affine.
∎

We can now define the functor
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
as in Section
3.4
, namely
`X=\bigoplus_{x\in X}1
for each set
X
, and for any binary relation
r:X\to Y
, we define
`r:`X\to`Y
by

(`r)_{x,y}=\begin{cases}e_{1},&(x,y)\in r,\\
0_{1},&(x,y)\notin r.\end{cases}

Proposition B.168
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial unital commutative quantale. Then the functors
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
and
(-)_{\circ}:\mathbf{Rel}\to V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
are natural dagger isomorphic, i.e., there is a natural transformation between both functors, and any component of this natural transformation is a dagger isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
X
be a set. Then
X_{\circ}=X
¡ whereas Proposition
B.162
yields
`X=\bigoplus_{x\in X}1=\biguplus_{x\in X}\{*\}=\{(x,*):x\in X\}
, with for each
x\in X
the canonical injection
i_{x}:1\to`X
given by

i_{x}\big(*,(x^{\prime},*)\big)=\begin{cases}e,&x=x^{\prime},\\
\perp,&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Since
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
has dagger biproducts, for each
x\in X
, the canonical projection
p_{x}:`X\to 1
is given by
i_{x}^{\dagger}
.
Let
g_{X}:X\to`X
be the bijection
x\mapsto(x,*)
. Since dagger isomorphisms in
\mathbf{Rel}
are bijections, and
(-)_{\circ}
is a homomorphism of dagger quantaloids by Lemma
B.164
, it follows that
\kappa_{X}:=(g_{X})_{\circ}:X_{\circ}\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow(`X)_{\circ}
is a dagger isomorphism. Because
(-)_{\circ}
is the identity on objects, it follows that
\kappa_{X}
is actually a
V
-relation
X\relbar\joinrel\mapstochar\joinrel\rightarrow`X
. Note that for each
(x^{\prime},*)
in
`X
we have

\kappa_{X}\big(x,(x^{\prime},*)\big)=\begin{cases}e,&x=x^{\prime},\\
\perp,&x\neq x^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Naturality of
\kappa
is straightforward.
∎

Theorem B.169
.

Let
V
be a nontrivial unital commutative quantale. Then
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
is dagger compact. More precisely, any set
X
is its own dual
X^{*}
in
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
, and if
\eta
and
\epsilon
denote the respective unit and counit of the dagger compact structure of
\mathbf{Rel}
, then
\eta_{\circ}
and
\epsilon_{\circ}
are the respective unit and the counit of the dagger compact structure of
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the fact that
\mathbf{Rel}
is dagger compact, and all the natural transformations in the symmetric monoidal structure of
V
-
\mathbf{Rel}
as well as in the proposed dagger compact structure are obtained by applying
(-)_{\circ}
to the analog natural transformations of
\mathbf{Rel}
.
∎

Appendix C
Quantum relations between von Neumann algebras

An example of a dagger symmetric monoidal category is provided by the category
\mathbf{WRel}
of von Neumann algebras and Weaver’s quantum relations. These relations were originally introduced in
[
40
]
.

C.1
Preliminaries on Hilbert spaces

C.1.1
Parseval’s identity

Given a set
A
, we denote its set of finite subsets by
\mathrm{Fin}(A)
, which becomes a directed set when ordered by inclusion. Given a (possibly uncountable) family
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of elements in a normed space
X
, we say that the sum
\sum_{\alpha\in A}x_{\alpha}
exists if the limit of the net
(\sum_{\alpha\in F}x_{\alpha})_{F\in\mathrm{Fin}(A)}
exists, in which case we define
\sum_{\alpha\in A}x_{\alpha}:=\lim_{F\in\mathrm{Fin}(A)}\sum_{\alpha\in F}x_{\alpha}
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Then
h,k\in H
are called
orthogonal
if
\langle h,k\rangle=0
. The expression of the norm of the sum in the following lemma is also called
Parseval’s identity
.

Lemma C.170
.

[
20
, Proposition 2.2.5]

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and let
S\subseteq H
be a set of mutually orthogonal elements of
H
.
Then
s:=\sum S
exists in
H
if and only if
\sum_{h\in S}\|h\|^{2}<\infty
, in which case
\|s\|^{2}=\sum_{h\in S}\|h\|^{2}
.

C.1.2
Subspaces

Given Hilbert spaces
H
and
K
, we denote by
B(H,K)
the Banach space of bounded (=norm-continuous) operators
H\to K
. A closed subspace of
B(H,K)
is called an
operator space
.
B(H):=B(H,H)
is an algebra under composition, and any norm-closed subalgebra of
B(H)
is called an
operator algebra
. Denoting inner products on Hilbert spaces by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, we denote the adjoint of a bounded operator
x:H\to K
by
x^{\dagger}
. That is,
x^{\dagger}:K\to H
is the unique bounded operator such that
\langle k,xh\rangle=\langle x^{\dagger}k,h\rangle
for each
h\in H
and each
k\in K
. The map
B(H)\to B(H)
,
x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
is an involution. Since in the operator algebras literature one typically writes
x^{*}
instead of
x^{\dagger}
, one refers to algebras with an involution as
*
-algebras
. An involution-preserving homomorphism between (unital)
*
-algebras is called a (unital)
*
-homomorphism
. A subalgebra of a unital algebra is called a
unital
subalgebra if it contains the identity element of the ambient algebra. The proof of the following lemma is elementary, hence we omit it.

Lemma C.171
.

Let
H
and
K
be Hilbert spaces, and let
j:K\to H
be a linear isometry. Then

(a)

j^{\dagger}j=\mathrm{id}_{K}
;

(b)

p:=jj^{\dagger}
is the projection on
H
with range
jK\subseteq H
;

(c)

the map
\varphi\colon B(K)\to pB(H)p
,
x\mapsto jxj^{\dagger}
is a
*
-isomorphism with inverse
\psi\colon pB(H)p\to B(K)
,
a\mapsto j^{\dagger}aj
.

C.1.3
Sums

We define the
\ell^{2}
-sum of a set-indexed family
(H_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of Hilbert spaces as the Hilbert space

\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}=\left\{(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\in\prod_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}:\sum_{\alpha\in A}\|h_{\alpha}\|^{2}<\infty\right\}

equipped with the inner product defined for each
h=(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
and
k=(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}
by

\langle h,k\rangle:=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle h_{\alpha},k_{\alpha}\rangle.

In particular, we have
\|h\|^{2}=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\|h_{\alpha}\|^{2}.

For each
\beta\in A
, we denote the canonical projection
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}\to H_{\beta}
,
(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\mapsto h_{\beta}
by
q_{\beta}
, which is bounded, i.e.,
q_{\beta}\in B\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\right)
. We denote the canonical injection
H_{\beta}\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}
,
h\mapsto(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
where

h_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}h,&\alpha=\beta,\\
0,&\alpha\neq\beta\end{cases}

by
j_{\beta}
.
Then
j_{\beta}=q_{\beta}^{\dagger}
, since for each
h\in H_{\beta}
and each
(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}
, writing
(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}:=j_{\beta}h
, we have

\langle j_{\beta}h,(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\rangle=\langle(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A},(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle h_{\alpha},k_{\alpha}\rangle=\langle h_{\beta},k_{\beta}\rangle=\langle h_{\beta},q_{\beta}((k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A})\rangle=\langle q^{\dagger}_{\beta}h_{\beta},(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\rangle.

We also note that
j_{\beta}
is an isometry: if
h\in H_{\beta}
, and writing
j_{\beta}h=(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
as above then
\|j_{\beta}h\|^{2}=\langle j_{\beta}h,j_{\beta}h\rangle=\langle(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A},(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle h_{\alpha},h_{\alpha}\rangle=\langle h_{\beta},h_{\beta}\rangle=\langle h,h\rangle=\|h\|^{2}
.

Given a Hilbert space
K
, and bounded maps
x_{\alpha}\in B(K,H_{\alpha})
for each
\alpha\in A
, the map
k\mapsto(x_{\alpha}k)_{\alpha\in A}
defines a linear operator
x:K\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}
. However,
x
is not necessarily bounded. A sufficient condition for
x
being bounded is that
\sum_{\alpha\in A}\|x_{\alpha}\|^{2}<\infty
.

Tensor products

Given two Hilbert spaces
H
and
K
, we denote their algebraic tensor product by
H\odot K
. We equip this tensor product with an inner product defined on elementary tensors
h\otimes k
,
h^{\prime}\otimes k^{\prime}
in
H\odot K
by

\langle h\otimes k,h^{\prime}\otimes k^{\prime}\rangle:=\langle h,h^{\prime}\rangle\langle k,k^{\prime}\rangle,

and extend this inner product by linearity on whole of
H\odot K
. It follows that
\|h\otimes k\|=\|h\|\|k\|
for each elementary tensor
h\otimes k
in
H\odot K
.
We now define the
tensor product

H\otimes K
of
H
and
K
as the completion of
H\odot K
with respect to the norm induced by the inner product on the latter space.

C.1.4
Trace class operators

Let
H
be a Hilbert space, and let
x
be a bounded operator on
H
. We define its
trace

\mathrm{Tr}(x)
by
\mathrm{Tr}(x):=\sum_{e\in E}\langle e,xe\rangle
, where
E
is any orthonormal basis for
H
. The trace of
x
is possibly infinite, but is in all cases independent of the choice of basis for
H

[
36
, Corollary 3.4.4]
.

We say that a bounded operator
x:H\to H
is
positive
if
\langle h,xh\rangle\geq 0
for each
h\in H
. If
x
is positive, then there is a unique positive operator
z\in B(H)
such that
x=z^{2}
, which is called the
square root
of
x

[
20
, Theorem 4.2.6]
. We write
z=\sqrt{x}
.

Now, let
K
be a second Hilbert space, and let
y:K\to H
be bounded. Since
\langle k,y^{\dagger}yk\rangle=\langle yk,yk\rangle\geq 0
for each
k\in K
, it follows that
y^{\dagger}y\in B(K)
is a positive operator, hence its square root exists. We write
|y|:=\sqrt{y^{\dagger}y}
, which is called the
absolute value
of
y
. Moreover, there is a unitary operator
u:\overline{\mathrm{Ran\ }{|y|}}\to\overline{\mathrm{Ran\ }{y}}
such that
y=u|y|
(cf.
[
39
, pp. 489-490]
). We say that
u
and
|y|
form the
polar decomposition
of
y
. Any bounded operator
y:K\to H
such that
\mathrm{Tr}(|y|)<\infty
is called a
trace class
operator. The set of all trace class operators
K\to H
is denoted by
T(K,H)
, which becomes a Banach space with norm
\|y\|_{1}:=\mathrm{Tr}(|y|
.

The following theorem summarizes the properties of trace class operators and the trace that are relevant to us:

Theorem C.172
.

[
42
, Theorems 7.6, 7.8 & 7.11]

Let
H
,
K
and
L
be Hilbert spaces and let
y:K\to H
be a bounded operator. Then
y\in T(K,H)
if and only if
|y|\in T(K)
if and only if
|y^{\dagger}|\in T(H)
in which case we have:

•

xy\in T(K,L)
and
\mathrm{Tr}(xy)=\mathrm{Tr}(yx)
for each
x\in B(H,L)
;

•

yz\in T(L,H)
and
\mathrm{Tr}(yz)=\mathrm{Tr}(zy)
for each
z\in B(L,K)
.

C.1.5
The weak*-topology on
B(H,K)

Let
H
and
K
be Hilbert spaces. Given
y\in T(K,H)
, it follows from Theorem
C.172
that
xy\in T(K)
for each
x\in B(H,K)
. Since the trace of trace class operators is finite, it follows that the map
B(H,K)\to\mathbb{C}
,
x\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(xy)
defines a functional on
B(H,K)
. In fact, the map
B(H,K)\to(T(K,H))^{*}
,
y\mapsto\varphi_{y}
is an isometric isomorphism, where
\varphi_{y}(x)=\mathrm{Tr}(xy)
, cf.
[
3
, 1.4.5]
or
[
13
, Section 1.2]
. As a consequence, we can describe the weak*-topology on
B(H,K)
in terms of trace class operators
K\to H
. It follows that a net
(x_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
in
B(H,K)
weak*-converges to
x\in B(H,K)
if and only if
\mathrm{Tr}((x-x_{\lambda})y)\to 0
for each
y\in T(K,H)
. We denote the weak*-closure of a subset
S\subseteq B(H,K)
by
\overline{S}
.

In
[
40
]
, Weaver defines the weak*-topology on
B(H,K)
in an alternative way, namely, by identifying
B(H,K)
isometrically with the
(H,K)
-corner of
B(H\oplus K)
via the isometry
\psi\colon B(H,K)\to B(H\oplus K)
,
x\mapsto j_{K}xq_{H}
, where
j_{H}\colon H\to H\oplus K
,
h\mapsto(h,0)
and
j_{K}\colon K\to H\oplus K
,
k\mapsto(0,k)
denote the canonical embeddings with associated canonical projections
q_{H}:=j_{H}^{\dagger}
and
q_{K}:=j_{K}^{\dagger}
. Using Theorem
C.172
, it is straightforward to verify that a subset
S\subseteq B(H,K)
is weak*-closed in
B(H,K)
if and only if
\psi[S]
is weak*-closed in
B(H\oplus K)
.

Proposition C.173
.

Let
H,K,L
be Hilbert spaces, and let
a\in B(K,L)
and
b\in B(L,H)
. Then the maps

\displaystyle B(H,K)

\displaystyle\to B(H,L),\quad x\mapsto ax,

\displaystyle B(H,K)

\displaystyle\to B(L,K),\quad x\mapsto xb

\displaystyle B(H,K)

\displaystyle\to B(K,H),\quad x\mapsto x^{\dagger}

are weak*-continuous.

Proof.

Let
