(x_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a net in
B(H,K)
with weak*-limit
x
. Thus, for each
y\in T(K,H)
, we have
\mathrm{Tr}((x-x_{\lambda})y)\to 0.

Let
a\in B(K,L)
. Then for each
z\in T(L,H)
, Theorem
C.172
assures that
y:=za\in T(K,H)
, hence by Theorem
C.172
, we find

\mathrm{Tr}((ax-ax_{\lambda})z)=\mathrm{Tr}(a(x-x_{\lambda})z)=\mathrm{Tr}((x-x_{\lambda})za)=\mathrm{Tr}((x-x_{\lambda})y)\to 0.

Since
z\in T(L,H)
was chosen arbitrarily, we find that
ax
is the weak*-limit of
(ax_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. Thus
x\mapsto ax
preserves weak*-limits of nets, whence
x\mapsto ax
is weak*-continuous.
In a similar way, we find that
x\mapsto xb
is weak*-continuous.

Finally, for any trace class operator
a:H\to K
, we have that
\mathrm{Tr}(a^{\dagger})=\sum_{e\in E}\langle e,a^{\dagger}e\rangle=\sum_{e\in E}\langle ae,e\rangle=\sum_{e\in E}\overline{\langle e,ae\rangle}=\overline{\mathrm{Tr}(a)}
. Let
z\in T(H,K)
. By Theorem
C.172
, we have
y:=z^{\dagger}\in T(K,H)
, hence

\mathrm{Tr}((x^{\dagger}-x_{\lambda}^{\dagger})z)=\overline{\mathrm{Tr}((x-x_{\lambda})y)}\to 0,

hence
(x_{\lambda}^{\dagger})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
weak*-converges to
x^{\dagger}
. Hence
x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
preserves weak*-limits of nets, which shows that
x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
is weak*-continuous.
∎

C.2
Definitions

We briefly recall the definition of von Neumann algebras, for which we first need the notion of the
commutant

S^{\prime}
of a subset
S\subseteq B(H)
for a fixed Hilbert space
H
, which is defined as

S^{\prime}:=\{y\in B(H):xy=yx\text{ for each }x\in S\}.

Now, a
von Neumann algebra

M
on a Hilbert space
H
is a
*
-subalgebra of
B(H)
that equals its bicommutant, i.e., we have
M^{\prime\prime}=M
. Equivalently, a von Neumann algebra
M
on
N
is a weak*-closed unital
*
-subalgebra of
B(H)
. Let
N
be another von Neumann algebra on a Hilbert space
K
. Then a
normal unital
*
-homomorphism

\varphi:M\to N
between von Neumann algebras is
*
-homomorphism that is continuous with respect to the weak*-topologies on
M
and
N
, i.e., the topologies inherited from the weak*-topologies on
B(H)
and
B(K)
, respectively. Any
*
-isomorphism between von Neumann algebras is unital and is automatically normal
[
2
, III.2.2.1]
. We denote the category of von Neumann algebras and normal unital
*
-homomorphisms by
\mathbf{WStar}
.

Definition C.174
.

Given Hilbert spaces
H,K,L
, and subspaces
V\subseteq B(H,K)
and
W\subseteq B(K,L)
we denote by
WV\subseteq B(H,L)
the span of all
wv
with
v\in V
and
w\in W
. We denote by
W\cdot V
the weak*-closure of
WV
in
B(H,L)
.

Lemma C.175
.

Let
H,K,L
be Hilbert spaces and
V\subseteq B(H,K)
and
W\subseteq B(K,L)
be subspaces. Then
\overline{W}\cdot\overline{V}=\overline{WV}
, where
\overline{(-)}
denotes the weak*-closure operator.

Proof.

Clearly, we have
W\subseteq\overline{W}
and
V\subseteq\overline{V}
, hence
WV\subseteq\overline{W}\phantom{.}\overline{V}\subseteq\overline{W}\cdot\overline{V}
. For the converse inclusion, let
a\in\overline{W}
and
b\in\overline{V}
. Hence, there are nets
(a_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
in
W
and
(b_{\kappa})_{\kappa\in K}
in
V
that weak*-converge to
b
and
a
, respectively. Note that
a_{\lambda}b_{\kappa}\in WV
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
and each
\kappa\in K
. Fix
\kappa\in K
. Then it follows from Proposition
C.173
that
(a_{\lambda}b_{\kappa})
weak*-converges to
ab_{\kappa}
, hence
ab_{\kappa}\in\overline{WV}
. Again using Proposition
C.173
, it now follows that
\lim_{\kappa\in K}ab_{\kappa}=ab
, hence
ab\in\overline{WV}
. It follows that
\overline{W}\phantom{.}\overline{V}\subseteq\overline{WV}
. Since
\overline{WV}
is weak*-closed, it follows that it contains the weak*-closure
\overline{W}\cdot\overline{V}
of
\overline{W}\phantom{.}\overline{V}
.
∎

In the following, we denote the algebraic tensor product of vector spaces by
\odot
. The following lemma is a generalization of
[
41
, Proposition T.4.3]
. In this reference, one takes
H_{1}=H_{2}
and
K_{1}=K_{2}
. However, the proof is completely the same for distinct
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, and distinct
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
.

Lemma C.176
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2},K_{1}
, and
K_{2}
be Hilbert spaces, and let
V\subseteq B(H_{1},H_{2})
and
W\subseteq B(K_{1},K_{2})
be subspaces. Then we can embed
V\odot W
into
B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{2})
by identifying
v\otimes w
in
V\otimes W
with the operator
H_{1}\otimes K_{1}\to H_{2}\otimes K_{2}
determined by
h\otimes k\mapsto vh\otimes wk
.

In the following, we will always regard
V\odot W
as a subspace of
B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{2})
as in the lemma above.

Definition C.177
.

Given Hilbert spaces
H_{1},H_{2},K_{1},K_{2}
and weak*-closed subspaces
V\subseteq B(H_{1},H_{2})
and
W\subseteq B(K_{1},K_{2})
, we denote the weak*-closure of
V\odot W
in
B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{2})
by
V\bar{\otimes}W
.

In the previous definition, note that if
H_{1}=H_{2}
and
K_{1}=K_{2}
, and
V
and
W
are von Neumann algebras on
H_{1}
and
K_{1}
, respectively, then
V\bar{\otimes}W
is the usual spatial tensor product of
V
and
W
.

Lemma C.178
.

Let
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
,
K_{1}
, and
K_{2}
be Hilbert spaces,
a\in B(H_{1},H_{2})
and
b\in B(K_{1},K_{2})
. Then:

•

The map
B(K_{1},K_{2})\to B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{2})
,
x\mapsto a\otimes x
is weak*-continuous;

•

The map
B(H_{1},H_{2})\to B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{2})
,
x\mapsto x\otimes b
is weak*-continuous.

Proof.

We prove only the weak*-continuity of the second map; the first is proven in a similar way.
By Lemma
C.171
, we have
q_{H_{i}}j_{H_{i}}=j_{H_{i}}^{\dagger}j_{H_{i}}=\mathrm{id}_{H_{i}}
for each
i=1,2
, the map
B(H_{1},H_{2})\to B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{2})
,
a\mapsto a\otimes b
is precisely the composition of the following maps:

\displaystyle B(H_{1},H_{2})

\displaystyle\to B(H_{1}\oplus H_{2}),\quad x\mapsto j_{H_{2}}xq_{H_{1}}

\displaystyle B(H_{1}\oplus H_{2})

\displaystyle\to B((H_{1}\oplus H_{2})\otimes K_{1}),\quad x\mapsto x\otimes\mathrm{id}_{K_{1}}

\displaystyle B((H_{1}\oplus H_{2})\otimes K_{1})

\displaystyle\to B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{1}),\quad x\mapsto(q_{H_{2}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{K_{1}})x(j_{H_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{K_{1}})

\displaystyle B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{1})

\displaystyle\to B(H_{1}\otimes K_{1},H_{2}\otimes K_{2}),\quad x\mapsto(\mathrm{id}_{H_{2}}\otimes b)x.

The second of these four maps is weak*-continuous by
[
2
, Proposition I.8.6.4]
. The weak*-continuity of the other maps follows from
Proposition
C.173
. Hence,
x\mapsto x\otimes b
is weak*-continuous for it is a composition of weak*-continuous maps.
∎

Lemma C.179
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2},K_{1}
, and
K_{2}
be Hilbert spaces, and let
V\subseteq B(H_{1},H_{2})
and
W\subseteq B(K_{1},K_{2})
be subspaces. Then
\overline{V}\bar{\otimes}\overline{W}=\overline{V\odot W}
.

Proof.

Clearly, we have
V\odot W\subseteq\overline{V}\odot\overline{W}
. Since the weak*-closure of the right-hand side is
\overline{V}\bar{\otimes}\overline{W}
, it follows that
\overline{V\odot W}\subseteq\overline{V}\bar{\otimes}\overline{W}
. Conversely, we claim that
\overline{V}\odot\overline{W}\subseteq\overline{V\odot W}
. Indeed, if
a\in\overline{V}
and
b\in\overline{W}
, then there are nets
(a_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
in
V
and
(b_{\kappa})_{\kappa\in K}
in
W
with weak*-limits
a
and
b
, respectively. For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
\kappa\in K
, we have
a_{\lambda}\otimes b_{\kappa}\in V\odot W\subseteq\overline{V\odot W}
. Hence, for each
\kappa\in K
, it follows from Lemma
C.178
that
a\otimes b_{\kappa}=\lim_{\lambda}a_{\lambda}\otimes b_{\kappa}\in\overline{V\odot W}
. Again using the lemma, it follows that
a\otimes b=\lim_{\kappa}a\otimes b_{\kappa}\in\overline{V\odot W}
. Since any elementary tensor
a\otimes b
for
a\in\overline{V}
and
b\in\overline{W}
is contained in
\overline{V\odot W}
, it follows that
\overline{V}\odot\overline{W}\subseteq\overline{V\odot W}
. We conclude that
\overline{V}\bar{\otimes}\overline{V}=\overline{\overline{V}\odot\overline{W}}\subseteq\overline{V\odot W}
.
∎

Lemma C.180
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2},H_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}
be Hilbert spaces, and let
V_{1}\subseteq B(H_{1},H_{2})
,
V_{2}\subseteq B(H_{2},H_{3})
,
W_{1}\subseteq B(K_{1},K_{2})
and
W_{2}\subseteq B(K_{2},K_{3})
be weak*-closed subspaces.
Then

(V_{2}\bar{\otimes}W_{2})\cdot(V_{1}\bar{\otimes}W_{1})=(V_{2}\cdot V_{1})\bar{\otimes}(W_{2}\cdot W_{1}).

Proof.

By functoriality of the algebraic tensor product, we have
(v_{2}\otimes w_{2})(v_{1}\otimes w_{1})=(v_{2}v_{1})\otimes(w_{2}w_{1})
for each
v_{i}\in V_{i}
,
w_{i}\in W_{i}
and
i=1,2
. It follows that
(V_{2}\odot W_{2})(V_{1}\odot W_{1})=(V_{2}V_{1})\odot(W_{2}W_{1})
.

Then by definition of
\bar{\otimes}
and
\cdot
, and using Lemmas
C.175
and
C.179
, we obtain

\displaystyle(V_{2}\bar{\otimes}W_{2})\cdot(V_{1}\bar{\otimes}W_{1})

\displaystyle=\overline{V_{2}\odot W_{2}}\cdot\overline{V_{1}\odot W_{1}}=\overline{(V_{2}\odot W_{2})(V_{1}\odot V_{2})}

\displaystyle=\overline{(V_{2}V_{1})\odot(W_{2}W_{1})}=\overline{V_{2}V_{1}}\bar{\otimes}\overline{W_{2}W_{1}}=(V_{2}\cdot V_{1})\bar{\otimes}(W_{2}\cdot W_{1}).\qed

Definition C.181
.

A
quantum relation

V
from a von Neumann algebra
M\subseteq B(H)
to a von Neumann algebra
N\subseteq B(K)
is a weak*-closed subspace of
B(H,K)
such that
N^{\prime}\cdot V\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq V
.

We note that Weaver mainly discusses quantum endorelations on
M
, and remarks that there is an identification between quantum relations
M\to N
as in the above definition, and quantum endorelations
V
on
M\oplus N\subseteq B(H\oplus K)
such that
V=p_{K}Vp_{H}
, where for
L=H,K
we define
p_{L}:=j_{L}j_{L}^{\dagger}
with
j_{L}:L\to H\oplus K
the embedding. Moreover, Weaver shows that quantum relations on
M
are independent of the choice of the Hilbert space
H
on which we represent
M

[
40
, Theorem 2.7]
.

Let
V
be a quantum relation between
M\subseteq B(H)
and
N\subseteq B(K)
, and
W
be a quantum relation between
N
and
R\subseteq B(L)
. We define composition of
V
and
W
to be
W\cdot V
. Furthermore,
M^{\prime}\subseteq B(H)
is a quantum relation on
M
, which acts as the the identity on
M
. We will often write
I_{M}:=M^{\prime}
.

Definition C.182
.

We denote the category of von Neumann algebras and quantum relations by
\mathbf{WRel}
.

Proposition C.183
.

[
27
, Proposition 1.6, Theorem 4.6]
Given a normal unital
*
-homomorphism
\varphi:N\to M
between von Neumann algebras
N\subseteq B(K)
and
M\subseteq B(H)
, the set

E_{\varphi}:=\{v\in B(H,K):xv=v\varphi(x)\text{ for all }x\in M\}

defines a quantum relation
M\to N
that is an internal map in
\mathbf{WRel}
. This induces a faithful functor
E\colon\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{WRel}
that is the identity on objects and acts on morphisms by
\varphi\mapsto E_{\varphi}
, and that corestricts to an equivalence of categories
\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{WRel})
.

C.3
Properties of quantum relations

Based on
[
40
, Proposition 2.3]
, but reformulated in terms of quantum relations
M\to N
instead of quantum relations
M\to M
, we have

Theorem C.184
.

\mathbf{WRel}
has the following properties:

(a)

\mathbf{WRel}
is a dagger category: given a quantum relation
V:M\to N
between von Neumann algebras
M\subseteq B(H)
and
N\subseteq B(K)
, we define
V^{\dagger}:N\to M
to be the space
\{v^{\dagger}:v\in V\}
, which again is a quantum relation.

(b)

\mathbf{WRel}
is a symmetric monoidal category when equipped with
\bar{\otimes}
; the associator, unitors and symmetry of
(\mathbf{WRel},\bar{\otimes},\mathbb{C})
are obtained by applying the functor
E:\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{WRel}
in Proposition
C.183
to the associator, unitors and symmetry of
(\mathbf{WStar},\bar{\otimes},\mathbb{C})
;

(c)

\mathbf{WRel}
is a dagger quantaloid: its homsets are complete lattices when ordered by inclusion: the infimum
\bigwedge_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
of a set-indexed family
(V_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of quantum relations
M\to N
is given by
\bigcap_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
, and the supremum
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
of the same family is given by the weak*-closure of the span of
\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let
V:M\to N
be a quantum relation. The map
B(H,K)\to B(K,H)
,
v\mapsto v^{\dagger}
is weak*-continuous by Proposition
C.173
, and
B(K,H)\to B(H,K)
,
w\mapsto w^{\dagger}
is clearly its inverse, hence it is an homeomorphism with respect to the weak*-topology. As a consequence,
V^{\dagger}
is a weak*-closed subspace of
B(K,H)
. Furthermore, since von Neumann algebras and their commutants are
{\dagger}
-closed, and the commutant of a von Neumann algebra is a von Neumann algebra, we have

M^{\prime}\cdot V^{\dagger}\cdot N^{\prime}=(M^{\prime})^{\dagger}\cdot V\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\dagger}=(N^{\prime}\cdot V\cdot M^{\prime})^{\dagger}\subseteq V^{\dagger}
, where the inclusion follows because
V
is a quantum relation. So
V^{\dagger}
is a quantum relation. Let
W:N\to R
be another quantum relation. Let
v\in V
and
w\in W
. Then
v^{\dagger}\in V^{\dagger}
and
w\in W^{\dagger}
, hence
(wv)^{\dagger}=w^{\dagger}v^{\dagger}\in W^{\dagger}\cdot V^{\dagger}
. It follows that
\{x^{\dagger}:x\in WV\}\subseteq V^{\dagger}\cdot W^{\dagger}
. By the weak*-continuity of
x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
(cf. Proposition
C.173
), it now follows that
(W\cdot V)^{\dagger}\subseteq V^{\dagger}\cdot W^{\dagger}
. Conversely, for
x\in V^{\dagger}
and
y\in W^{\dagger}
, we have
x=v^{\dagger}
and
y=w^{\dagger}
for some
v\in V
and some
w\in W
. Then
xy=v^{\dagger}w^{\dagger}=(wv)^{\dagger}\in(W\cdot V)^{\dagger}
. Hence,
V^{\dagger}W^{\dagger}\subseteq V^{\dagger}\cdot W^{\dagger}
, which implies
V^{\dagger}\cdot W^{\dagger}\subseteq(W\cdot V)^{\dagger}
. We conclude that
(W\cdot V)^{\dagger}=V^{\dagger}\cdot W^{\dagger}
. Since
I^{\dagger}_{M}=(M^{\prime})^{\dagger}=M^{\prime}=I_{M}
, it follows that
(-)^{\dagger}
is a contravariant functor on
\mathbf{WRel}
, which clearly is the identity on objects.
Furthermore, we clearly have
V^{{\dagger}{\dagger}}=V
, whence
(-)^{\dagger}
is a dagger on
\mathbf{WRel}
.

For (b), let
V\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
and
W\colon N_{1}\to N_{2}
be quantum relations. We first note that for any two von Neumann algebras
M
and
N
, we have

(M\bar{\otimes}N)^{\prime}=(M^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}N^{\prime})
, see for instance
[
2
, Theorem III.4.5.8]
.
It now follows from Lemma
C.180
that

\displaystyle(M_{2}\bar{\otimes}N_{2})^{\prime}\cdot(V\bar{\otimes}W)\cdot(M_{1}\bar{\otimes}N_{1})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(M_{2}^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}N_{2}^{\prime})\cdot(V\bar{\otimes}W)\cdot(M_{1}^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}N_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(M_{2}^{\prime}\cdot V\cdot M_{1}^{\prime})\bar{\otimes}(N_{2}^{\prime}\cdot W\cdot N_{1}^{\prime})\subseteq V\bar{\otimes}W,

so
V\bar{\otimes}W
is a quantum relation. Functoriality of
\bar{\otimes}
follows from Lemma
C.180
and from
(I_{M}\bar{\otimes}I_{N})=M^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}N^{\prime}=(M\bar{\otimes}N)^{\prime}=I_{M\bar{\otimes}N}
for any two von Neumann algebras
M
and
N
. Clearly,
\mathbf{WRel}
becomes a symmetric monoidal category with the associator, unitors and symmetry obtained by applying the functor
E
to the associator, unitors and symmetry of
\mathbf{WStar}
.

Finally, we prove (c). Clearly
\bigcap_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
is weak*-closed as an intersection of weak*-closed subsets, and we have
N^{\prime}\cdot\left(\bigcap_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq N^{\prime}\cdot V_{\beta}\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq V_{\beta}
for each
\beta\in A
, whence
N^{\prime}\cdot\left(\bigcap_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq\bigcap_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
, so
\bigcap_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
is a quantum relation.

\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
is weak*-closed by definition. Let
x\in N^{\prime}\mathrm{span}\left(\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)M^{\prime}
. Then
x
is of the form
x=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}v_{i}m_{i}
for
n_{i}\in N^{\prime}
,
v_{i}\in\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}
and
m_{i}\in M^{\prime}
, and since
N^{\prime}\cdot V_{\alpha}\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq V_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in A
, it follows that
n_{i}v_{i}m_{i}
and hence also
x
is an element of
\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
. Thus,
N^{\prime}\mathrm{span}\left(\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)M^{\prime}\subseteq\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
. It now follows from Lemma
C.175
that
N^{\prime}\cdot\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
, so
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
is a quantum relation.
Let
V:M\to N
be a quantum relation such that
V_{\alpha}\subseteq V
for each
\alpha\in A
. Clearly
\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\subseteq V
, hence also
\mathrm{span}\left(\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)\subseteq V
, whence
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\subseteq V
for
V
is weak*-closed. So
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
is the supremum of
(V_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
\mathbf{WRel}(M,N)
. Now, let
W:N\to R
and
U:S\to M
be quantum relations. Then
W\cdot V_{\beta}\subseteq W\cdot\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\beta}\cdot U\subseteq\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}\right)\cdot U
for each
\beta\in A
, whence
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}(W\cdot V_{\alpha})\subseteq W\cdot\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
and
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}(V_{\alpha}\cdot U)\subseteq\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)\cdot U
. The converse inclusions follow from the observation that
W\mathrm{span}\left(\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)\subseteq\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}(W\cdot V_{\alpha})
and
\mathrm{span}\left(\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}\right)U\subseteq\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}(V_{\alpha}\cdot U)
and Lemma
C.175
. Finally, clearly
(-)^{\dagger}
is an order isomorphism, hence
\mathbf{WRel}
is a dagger quantaloid.
∎

Products in
\mathbf{WStar}
, so coproducts in
\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}
are described by the following construction (see also
[
35
, p.30]
,
[
9
, Proposition 43.5]
, and
[
28
, Proposition 5.1]
).

Proposition C.185
.

Let
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
be a set-indexed family where
M_{\alpha}
is a von Neumann algebra on a Hilbert space
H_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in A
. Then the product of
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
\mathbf{WStar}
is given by the
\ell^{\infty}
-sum of
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
, which is defined as the von Neumann algebra

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}:=\left\{(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\in\prod_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}:\sup_{\alpha\in A}\|x_{\alpha}\|<\infty\right\}

on
H:=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}
. The action of
M
on
H
is defined by
xh=(x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
for each
x=(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
M
and
h=(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
H
.
The commutant of
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
in
B(H)
is given by

\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\right)^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}^{\prime},

where the commutant of each summand
M_{\alpha}
in the right-hand side is calculated in
B(H_{\alpha})
.

For
\beta\in A
, we denote the canonical projection
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\to M_{\beta}
,
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\mapsto x_{\beta}
by
\pi_{\beta}
.

The sum of von Neumann algebras allows us to introduce an important class of von Neumann algebras that is relevant for quantum sets.

Definition C.186
.

Any von Neumann algebra
*
-isomorphic to a (possibly infinite)
\ell^{\infty}
-sum of matrix algebras is called
hereditarily atomic
.

Proposition C.187
.

\mathbf{WRel}
has small dagger biproducts, which are coproducts created by the embedding
E:\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{WRel}
.

More explicitly, let
A
be an index set, and for each
\alpha\in A
, let
M_{\alpha}
be a von Neumann algebra on a Hilbert space
H_{\alpha}
. Then the dagger biproduct
M
of the set-index family
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}

is given by
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
. For each
\beta\in A
, the canonical injection
J_{\beta}:M_{\beta}\to M
is given by

E_{\pi_{\beta}}=\{v\in B(H_{\beta},H):xv=vx_{\beta}\text{ for all }x=(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\in M\},

where
\pi_{\beta}:M\to M_{\beta}
is the canonical projection, and
H=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}
. Moreover, we have
J_{\beta}=j_{\beta}M_{\beta}^{\prime}
, where
j_{\beta}:H_{\beta}\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}H_{\alpha}
is the canonical injection and
M_{\beta}^{\prime}
is the commutant of
M_{\beta}
in
B(H_{\beta})
.

Proof.

Applying Proposition
C.183
to the canonical projection
\pi_{\beta}:M\to M_{\beta}
yields the expression for
J_{\beta}
in the statement, and shows that
J_{\beta}\in\mathrm{Maps}(\mathbf{WRel})
.
We show that
J_{\beta}=j_{\beta}M_{\beta}
.
Let
x=(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
M
. Let
h=(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
H
and
k\in H_{\beta}
. Note that
(j_{\beta}k)_{\alpha}=0
for
\alpha\neq\beta
, and
(j_{\beta}k)_{\beta}=k
. Hence,
(xj_{\beta}k)_{\alpha}=0
if
\alpha\neq\beta
, and
(xj_{\beta}k)_{\beta}=x_{\beta}k
. Then

\langle h,xj_{\beta}k\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle h_{\alpha},(xj_{\beta}k)_{\alpha}\rangle=\langle h_{\beta},x_{\beta}k\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in A}h_{\alpha},j_{\beta}x_{\beta}k\rangle=\langle h,j_{\beta}x_{\beta}k\rangle,

which shows that
xj_{\beta}=j_{\beta}x_{\beta}
, hence
j_{\beta}\in J_{\beta}
. Now, if
y\in M_{\beta}^{\prime}
, then we find for each
x=(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
M
that
xj_{\beta}y=j_{\beta}x_{\beta}y=j_{\beta}yx_{\beta}
, so also
j_{\beta}y\in J_{\beta}
. Thus
j_{\beta}M^{\prime}\subseteq J_{\beta}
.

For the converse inclusion, given an arbitrary
v\in B(H_{\beta},H)
, we define
v_{\alpha}\in B(H_{\beta},H_{\alpha})
for each
\alpha\in A
by
v_{\alpha}:=q_{\alpha}v
. Then
\|v_{\alpha}\|\leq\|q_{\alpha}\|\|v\|=\|v\|
, so
\sup_{\alpha\in A}\|v_{\alpha}\|\leq\|v\|<\infty
. It follows that
vh=(v_{\alpha}h)_{\alpha\in A}
for each
h\in H
.

Now let
v\in J_{\beta}
. Let
y\in M_{\beta}
and let
x:=(\delta_{\alpha\beta}y)_{\alpha\in A}
. Clearly, we have
x\in M
, hence it follows from
v\in J_{\beta}
that
xv=vx_{\beta}
. Then for
\alpha\neq\beta
, we have that
0=q_{\alpha}xv=q_{\alpha}vx_{\beta}=q_{\alpha}vy=v_{\alpha}y
, so if we choose
y=\mathrm{id}_{M_{\beta}}
, we obtain that
v_{\alpha}=0
for
\alpha\neq\beta
. On the other hand, for arbitrary
y\in M_{\beta}
and for each
h\in H_{\beta}
, we have

v_{\beta}yh=q_{\beta}vyh=q_{\beta}vx_{\beta}h=q_{\beta}xvh=x_{\beta}(vh)_{\beta}=y(vh)_{\beta}=yv_{\beta}h,

where the last equality follows because we previously found that
vh=(v_{\alpha}h)_{\alpha\in A}
. Thus
v_{\beta}y=yv_{\beta}
, which shows that
v_{\beta}\in M_{\beta}^{\prime}
.
It follows for each
h\in H_{\beta}
and each
k=(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}\in H
that

\langle k,vh\rangle=\langle(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A},(v_{\alpha}h)_{\alpha\in A}\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle k_{\alpha},v_{\alpha}h\rangle=\langle k_{\beta},v_{\beta}h\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle k_{\alpha},(j_{\beta}v_{\beta}h)_{\alpha})\rangle=\langle k,j_{\beta}v_{\beta}h\rangle,

which shows that
vh=j_{\beta}v_{\beta}h
for each
h\in H
, whence
v=j_{\beta}v_{\beta}
, so
v\in j_{\beta}M^{\prime}_{\beta}
. We conclude that indeed
J_{\beta}=j_{\beta}M_{\beta}^{\prime}
.
It now follows immediately that for each
\alpha,\beta\in A
, we have

J_{\beta}^{\dagger}\cdot J_{\alpha}=(M_{\beta}^{\prime})^{\dagger}j_{\beta}^{\dagger}\cdot j_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}^{\prime}=M_{\beta}^{\prime}\cdot(q_{\beta}j_{\alpha})M_{\alpha}^{\prime}=M_{\beta}^{\prime}\cdot\delta_{\alpha,\beta}M_{\alpha}^{\prime}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}M_{\alpha}^{\prime}=\delta_{M_{\alpha},M_{\beta}}.

Let
\beta\in A
. We claim that
J_{\beta}\cdot J_{\beta}^{\dagger}\subseteq M^{\prime}
.
Let
y\in J_{\beta}\cdot J_{\beta}^{\dagger}
. Then we need to show that
yx=xy
for each
x\in M
. So fix
x=(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
M
.
We note that that

J_{\beta}\cdot J_{\beta}^{\dagger}=j_{\beta}M_{\beta}^{\prime}\cdot(M_{\beta}^{\prime})^{\dagger}j_{\beta}^{\dagger}=j_{\beta}M_{\beta}^{\prime}\cdot M_{\beta}^{\prime}\cdot q_{\beta}=j_{\beta}M_{\beta}^{\prime}q_{\beta}
, hence there is some
z\in M_{\beta}^{\prime}
such that
y=j_{\beta}zq_{\beta}
. Furthermore, since
J_{\beta}=j_{\beta}M_{\beta}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{id}_{H_{\beta}}\in M_{\beta}^{\prime}
, it follows that
j_{\beta}\in J_{\beta}
. Since
J_{\beta}=E_{\pi_{\beta}}
, we have
j_{\beta}\in E_{\pi_{\beta}}
, hence
xj_{\beta}=j_{\beta}x
.
Moreover, for each
h=(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
, we have by
q_{\beta}xh=x_{\beta}h_{\beta}=x_{\beta}q_{\beta}h
by definition of the action of
M
on
H
, so
q_{\beta}x=x_{\beta}q_{\beta}
. Collecting our results, we obtain

xy=xj_{\beta}zq_{\beta}=j_{\beta}x_{\beta}zq_{\beta}=j_{\beta}zx_{\beta}q_{\beta}=j_{\beta}zq_{\beta}x=yx.

So indeed
y\in M^{\prime}
, which shows that
J_{\beta}\cdot J_{\beta}^{\dagger}\subseteq M^{\prime}
. Thus
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\subseteq M^{\prime}
.

In order to show the converse inclusion, let
x=(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
M^{\prime}
. By Proposition
C.185
we have
x_{\alpha}\in M_{\alpha}^{\prime}
for each
\alpha\in A
. Recall that
\|x\|=\sup_{\alpha\in A}\|x_{\alpha}\|
. Moreover, for
h=(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
, we have
(xh)_{\alpha}=x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in A

by definition of the action of
M^{\prime}
on
H
. Hence,

\|xh\|^{2}=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\|x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}\|^{2}.

(18)

Since
j_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}^{\prime}q_{\alpha}=J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}
, we have
j_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}q_{\alpha}\in J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
for each
\alpha\in A
. If for each finite subset
F
of
A
we define
s_{F}:=\sum_{\alpha\in F}j_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}q_{\alpha},
then it follows that
s_{F}\in\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
. Moreover, for
h=(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
in
H
, we have
s_{F}h=\sum_{\alpha\in F}j_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}q_{\alpha}h=\sum_{\alpha\in F}j_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}
, hence

(s_{F}h)_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha},&\alpha\in F\\
0,&\alpha\notin F\end{cases}

It follows that
\|s_{F}h\|^{2}=\sum_{\alpha\in F}\|x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}\|^{2}
.
Let
\epsilon>0
. Then it follows from equation (
18
) that there is some finite
F\subseteq A
such that

\sum_{\alpha\in X\setminus G}\|x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}\|^{2}=\left|\|xh\|^{2}-\sum_{\alpha\in G}\|x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}\|^{2}\right|<\epsilon^{2}.

(19)

for each finite
G\subseteq A
with
F\subseteq G
.
Since

((x-s_{G})h)_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha},&\alpha\notin G,\\
0,&\alpha\in G,\end{cases}

we have

\|(x-s_{G})h\|^{2}=\sum_{\alpha\notin G}\|x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}\|^{2}<\epsilon^{2}
for each finite
G\subseteq A
with
F\subseteq G
. Hence, the net
(s_{F}h)_{F\in\mathrm{Fin}(A)}
converges to
xh
in
H
for each
h\in H
, whence
(s_{F})_{F\in\mathrm{Fin}(A)}
is a net in
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
converging to
x
in the strong operator topology on
B(H)
.
Now for each
h\in H
and each finite
F\subseteq A
, we have

\|s_{F}h\|^{2}=\sum_{\alpha\in F}\|x_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}\|^{2}\leq\sum_{\alpha\in F}\|x_{\alpha}\|^{2}\|h_{\alpha}\|^{2}\leq(\sup_{\alpha\in A}\|x_{\alpha}\|)^{2}\sum_{\alpha\in A}\|h_{\alpha}\|^{2}=\|x\|^{2}\|h\|^{2},

so
\|s_{F}h\|\leq\|x\|\|h\|
, which implies
\|s_{F}\|\leq\|x\|
. Thus,
(s_{F})_{F\in\mathrm{Fin}(A)}
is a net that converges to
x
in the strong operator topology on
B(H)
, hence by
[
2
, I.3.1.4]
, it converges to
x
in the weak operator topology on
B(H)
. Since
(s_{F})_{F\in\mathrm{Fin}(A)}
is a net that is norm-bounded by the norm of its limit
x
, it follows that it converges to
x
in the
\sigma
-weak operator topology on
B(H)
by
[
2
, I.3.1.4]
. The
\sigma
-weak operator topology is a synonym for the weak*-topology on
B(H)
, so the net
(s_{F})_{F\in\mathrm{Fin}(A)}
in
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
weak*-converges to
x
. Thus
x\in\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
. We conclude that
I_{M}=M^{\prime}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}J_{\alpha}\cdot J_{\alpha}^{\dagger}
.
Since
\mathbf{WRel}
is a dagger quantaloid by Theorem
C.184
, it follows from Proposition
2.47
that it has all small dagger biproducts.
∎

Next, we show that
\mathbf{WRel}
has dagger kernels, for which we need some lemmas. Firstly, given Hilbert spaces
H
and
K
and a unitary map
u:H\to K
, we say that two von Neumann algebras
M\subseteq B(H)
and
N\subseteq B(K)
are
spatially isomorphic
if
uMu^{\dagger}=N
, in which case the
*
-isomorphism
B(H)\to B(K)
,
a\mapsto uau^{\dagger}
restricts and corestricts to a
*
-isomorphism
M\to N
. The proof of the following lemma is elementary, hence we omit it.

Lemma C.188
.

Let
M\subseteq B(H)
and
N\subseteq B(K)
be von Neumann algebras that are spatially isomorphic via a unitary map
u:H\to K
. Then
N^{\prime}=uM^{\prime}u^{\dagger}
.

Lemma C.189
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and let
K\subseteq H
be a subspace. Let
j:K\to H
be the embedding with corresponding projection
p:=jj^{\dagger}
. Let
M\subseteq B(H)
be a von Neumann algebra containing
p
. Then
j^{\dagger}Mj
is a von Neumann algebra on
B(K)
with
(j^{\dagger}Mj)^{\prime}=j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}j
. Moreover, if
M
is hereditarily atomic, so is
j^{\dagger}Mj
.

Proof.

By (a) and (b) of Lemma
C.171
, we have
j^{\dagger}p=j^{\dagger}
and
pj=j
. It follows that
jK=pjK\subseteq pH
, and
pH=jj^{\dagger}H\subseteq jK
, so
jK=pH
. Hence,
j:K\to pH
is a unitary with inverse
j^{\dagger}:pH\to K
. Now,
pMp
and
pM^{\prime}p
are von Neumann algebras on
B(pH)
with
(pMp)^{\prime}=pM^{\prime}p
, which can be found in
[
38
, Proposition II.3.10]
or alternatively
[
21
, Corollary 5.5.7]
. By (c) of Lemma
C.171
, we have a
*
-isomorphism
\psi:pB(H)p\to B(K)
,
a\mapsto j^{\dagger}aj
, which restricts to an
*
-isomorphism
pMp\to\psi[pMp]=j^{\dagger}pMpj=j^{\dagger}Mj
, so
pMp
and
j^{\dagger}Mj
are spatially isomorphic via the unitary
j^{\dagger}:pH\to K
. By Lemma
C.188
, we have
(j^{\dagger}Mj)^{\prime}=(j^{\dagger}pMpj)^{\prime}=j^{\dagger}(pMp)^{\prime}j=j^{\dagger}pM^{\prime}pj=j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}j
.

Now assume that
M
is hereditarily atomic, so we can write
M=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}B(H_{\alpha})
for some finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces
H_{\alpha}
. Then
p=(p_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
for some projection
p_{\alpha}\in B(H_{\alpha})
. Then
p_{\alpha}B(H_{\alpha})p_{\alpha}
is finite-dimensional, hence an
\ell^{\infty}
-sum of matrix algebras by the Artin-Wedderburn Theorem (cf.
[
38
, Theorem I.11.2]
). Therefore,
j^{\dagger}Mj\cong pMp=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}p_{\alpha}B(H_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is an
\ell^{\infty}
sum of matrix algebras, hence hereditarily atomic.
∎

Lemma C.190
.

Let
M\subseteq B(H)
and
N\subseteq B(K)
be von Neumann algebras. Let
V:M\to N
be a quantum relation. Let
L=\bigcap_{v\in V}\mathrm{ker}(v)
. Let
p\in B(H)
be the projection with range
L
. Then
p\in M
.

Proof.

Since
V
is a quantum relation, we have
N^{\prime}\cdot V\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq V
. Since
1_{K}\in N^{\prime}
, this implies
V\cdot M^{\prime}\subseteq V
. Let
x\in M^{\prime}
and
h\in L
. Then for each
v\in V
, we have
vx\in V
, so
vxh=0
. Therefore, we have
xh\in\mathrm{ker}(v)
for each
v\in V
, whence
xh\in L
. We conclude that
xL\subseteq L
, so
L
is an invariant subspace for
x
, which implies that
pxp=xp

[
8
, Proposition 3.7]
. Thus we have
pxp=xp
for each
x\in M^{\prime}
, which is a self-adjoint algebra, hence
px^{\dagger}p=x^{\dagger}p
for each
x\in M^{\prime}
, whence
px=(x^{\dagger}p)^{\dagger}=(px^{\dagger}p)^{\dagger}=pxp
for each
x\in M^{\prime}
. We conclude that
xp=px
for each
x\in M^{\prime}
, which shows that
p
is an element of
M^{\prime\prime}=M
.
∎

Proposition C.191
.

\mathbf{WRel}
has dagger kernels. Moreover, if
M
is a hereditarily atomic von Neumann algebra and
V:M\to N
a quantum relation with dagger kernel
E:R\to M
, then
R
is hereditarily atomic.

Proof.

Let
M\subseteq B(H)
and
N\subseteq B(K)
, and let
V:M\to N
be a quantum relation. Let
L=\bigcap_{v\in V}\mathrm{ker}(v)
.
Let
j:L\to H
be the embedding, and let
p=jj^{\dagger}
, the projection in
B(H)
associated to
L
. By Lemma
C.190
we have
p\in M
. We define
R\subseteq B(L)
as
j^{\dagger}Mj
, which is indeed a von Neumann algebra on
L
by Lemma
C.189
, which also assures that
R^{\prime}=j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}j
. Note that the same lemma assures that
R
is hereditarily atomic if
M
is hereditarily atomic. We define
E\subseteq B(L,H)
by
E=\overline{M^{\prime}j}
, the weak*-closure of
M^{\prime}j=\{xj:x\in M^{\prime}\}
. Using Lemma
C.175
, we have

M^{\prime}\cdot E\cdot R^{\prime}=\overline{M^{\prime}M^{\prime}jj^{\dagger}M^{\prime}j}=\overline{M^{\prime}pM^{\prime}j}=\overline{M^{\prime}M^{\prime}pj}=\overline{M^{\prime}j}=E,

where we used that
p\in M
commutes with any element in
M^{\prime}
and
pj=j
. Thus
E
is a quantum relation. By Proposition
C.173
, the map
(-)^{\dagger}
is weak*-continuous, and clearly, it is its own inverse, hence
E^{\dagger}=(\overline{M^{\prime}j})^{\dagger}=\overline{(M^{\prime}j)^{\dagger}}=\overline{j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}}
, because
M^{\prime}
is selfadjoint.

We have
E^{\dagger}\cdot E=\overline{j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}M^{\prime}j}=\overline{j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}j}=j^{\dagger}Mj=R^{\prime}
, so
E
is a dagger monomorphism. Furthermore,
V\cdot E=\overline{VM^{\prime}j}=0
, because for each
v\in V
and
x\in M^{\prime}
, we have
vx\in V
, hence
vxj=0
by construction of
L
. So
V\cdot E=0
.

Now let
G
be another Hilbert space, and
S\subseteq B(G)
a von Neumann algebra, and
W:S\to M
a quantum relation such that
V\cdot W=0
. Then
vw=0
for each
v\in V
and each
w\in W
, so
w(g)\in\mathrm{ker}(v)
for each
v\in V
, for each
w\in W
and each
g\in G
, so
w(g)\in L
for each
w\in W
and each
g\in G
, hence
pw(g)=w(g)
for each
w\in W
and each
g\in G
, therefore
pw=w
for each
w\in W
, hence
pW=W
.

Let
T=\overline{j^{\dagger}W}
. Again using Lemma
C.175
, we find

R^{\prime}\cdot T\cdot S^{\prime}=\overline{j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}jj^{\dagger}WS^{\prime}}=\overline{j^{\dagger}M^{\prime}pWS^{\prime}}=\overline{j^{\dagger}pM^{\prime}WS^{\prime}}\subseteq\overline{j^{\dagger}pW}=\overline{j^{\dagger}W}=T,

so
T
is a quantum relation.
Furthermore, we have

E\cdot T=\overline{M^{\prime}jj^{\dagger}W}=\overline{M^{\prime}pW}=\overline{M^{\prime}W}=M^{\prime}\cdot W=I_{M}\cdot W=W.

Let
U:S\to R
be another quantum relation such that
E\cdot U=W
.
Then

U=E^{\dagger}\cdot E\cdot U=E^{\dagger}\cdot W=E^{\dagger}\cdot E\cdot T=T.

Thus,
E
is the equalizer of
V
and
0
.
∎

Theorem C.192
.

\mathbf{WRel}
is a symmetric monoidal dagger quantaloid with all small dagger biproducts and dagger kernels.

Proof.

By Theorem
C.184
,
\mathbf{WRel}
is a dagger quantaloid. By Proposition
C.191
it has dagger kernels. By Proposition
C.187
it has dagger biproducts, which are coproducts created by the embedding
E:\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{WRel}
. Since
\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}
is symmetric monoidal closed
[
28
, Theorem 9.5]
, its monoidal product
\bar{\otimes}
(which on objects coincides with the monoidal product on
\mathbf{WRel}
) preserves coproducts. Thus
\bar{\otimes}
distributes over small coproducts in
\mathbf{WStar}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and since the biproducts in
\mathbf{WRel}
are coproducts created by the embedding
E
, it follows that
\bar{\otimes}
distributes over biproducts in
\mathbf{WRel}
, so the latter category is infinitely distributive symmetric monoidal. It now follows that
\mathbf{WRel}
is a symmetric monoidal dagger quantaloid by Theorem
3.61
.
∎

Appendix D
Binary relations between quantum sets

In the introduction, we defined
\mathbf{qRel}
as the category of hereditarily atomic von Neumann algebras and quantum relations. Here, we give an alternative definition of
\mathbf{qRel}
in terms of quantum sets, which are essentially sets of nonzero finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. The equality between both definitions was proven by Kornell in Appendix A of
[
30
]
. Furthermore, we refer to
[
29
]
, where quantum sets were introduced.

D.1
Definitions

We define
\mathbf{qRel}
as the biproduct completion
\mathrm{Matr}(\mathbf{FdOS})
of a dagger compact quantaloid
\mathbf{FdOS}
defined below. It follows from Theorem
3.76
that
\mathbf{qRel}
is a dagger compact quantaloid that has all small dagger biproducts by construction.

We define the category
\mathbf{FdOS}
as the category whose objects are non-zero finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces; any morphism
V:X\to Y
between objects
X
and
Y
of
\mathbf{FdOS}
is a subspace
V\subseteq B(X,Y)
. Since
X
and
Y
are finite-dimensional, so is
B(X,Y)
, whence such
V
is closed, hence an operator space. Given another object
Z\in\mathbf{FdOS}
and an operator space
W:Y\to Z
, the composition
W\cdot V:X\to Z
of
V
with
W
is defined as the operator space spanned by
\{wv:w\in W,v\in V\}
.

\mathbf{FdOS}
becomes a quantaloid if we order its homsets by inclusion. The identity operator space
I_{X}
on the object
X
is given by
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}_{X}
, where
\mathrm{id}_{X}:X\to X
is the identity operator on
X
. The supremum
\bigvee_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
of a set-indexed family
(V_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of parallel operator spaces
X\to Y
is given by the span of
\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}V_{\alpha}
.

\mathbf{FdOS}
is a dagger category if for each morphism
V:X\to Y
we define
V^{\dagger}:Y\to X
by
V^{\dagger}=\{v^{\dagger}:v\in V\}
, where
v^{\dagger}:Y\to X
denotes the adjoint of the operator
v:X\to Y
.

\mathbf{FdOS}
is a dagger compact category. The monoidal product of
X\otimes Y
of objects in
\mathbf{FdOS}
is the usual tensor product of Hilbert spaces. The monoidal unit is
\mathbb{C}
. Given morphisms
V:X_{1}\to Y_{1}
and
W:X_{2}\to Y_{2}
, we define
V\otimes W:X_{1}\otimes X_{2}\to Y_{1}\otimes Y_{2}
as the span of
\{v\otimes w:v\in V,w\in W\}
. The associator
(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\to X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)
in
\mathbf{FdOS}
is given by
\mathbb{C}\alpha_{X,Y,Z}
, where
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}:(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\to X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)
is the associator in the category
\mathbf{FdHilb}
of finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and linear operators. The unitors, the symmetry, the unit and the counit of
\mathbf{FdOS}
are defined similarly, where the dual
X^{*}
of an object
X
in
\mathbf{FdOS}
is the usual Banach space dual of
X
.

The objects of
\mathbf{qRel}
are called
quantum sets
, and are typically denoted by scripted letters
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{Z}
. Thus, a quantum set
\mathcal{X}
is by construction an indexed set
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of nonzero finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. The quantum set for which
A=\emptyset
is the
empty
quantum set, and is denoted by
\emptyset
. Given a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
X
and a quantum set
\mathcal{X}
, we typically write
X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}
to indicate that there is some
\alpha\in A
such that
X=X_{\alpha}
, in which case we call
X
an
atom
of
\mathcal{X}
. We pronounce
X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}
as ‘
X
is an atom of
\mathcal{X}
’. In some sense, the atoms of a quantum set behave like elements of ordinary sets, but philosophically, only one-dimensional atoms correspond to actual elements of the quantum set, whereas atoms of dimension
n>1
can be regarded as subsets of the quantum set consisting of
n^{2}
elements that are inextricably clumped together.
We denote the set of atoms of a quantum set
\mathcal{X}
by
\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{X})
. Given a set
M
of non-zero finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, we can define a quantum set
\mathcal{Q}M
such that
\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{Q}M)=M
, namely
\mathcal{Q}M
is the indexed set obtained by indexing
M
by itself via the identity function, i.e.,
\mathcal{Q}M=(X)_{X\in M}
. Clearly, if
\mathcal{X}=(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
and
\mathcal{Y}=(Y_{\beta})_{\beta\in B}
, the existence of a bijection
f:A\to B
such that
Y_{f(\alpha)}\cong X_{\alpha}
as Hilbert spaces for each
\alpha\in A
implies that
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are dagger isomorphic in
\mathbf{qRel}
. In particular, any quantum set
\mathcal{X}
is dagger isomorphic to
\mathcal{Q}\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{X})
, hence
\mathcal{X}
is determined by its atoms, which justify to represent a quantum set
\mathcal{X}
as
\mathcal{X}=(X)_{X\in\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{X})}
. With this notation, we have
\mathcal{X}^{*}=(X^{*})_{X\in\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{X})}
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{X}^{*}=\mathcal{Q}\{X^{*}:X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}\}
.

A morphism
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
\mathbf{qRel}
is called a
binary relation
, even though
R
does not correspond to a subset of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
as in the case of binary relations between ordinary sets. Nevertheless, the definition of a binary relation between quantum sets is an extension of the notion of a binary relation between ordinary sets as follows from the existence of the embedding
`(-):\mathbf{Rel}\to\mathbf{qRel}
(cf. Definition
3.85
). Writing
\mathcal{X}=(X)_{X\in\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{X})}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{Y}
,
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is a function that to each
X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}
and each
Y\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}
assigns a subspace
R(X,Y)
of
B(X,Y)
. Then
R^{\dagger}:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
is given by
R^{\dagger}(Y,X)=\{r^{\dagger}:r\in R(X,Y)\}
.

The monoidal product on
\mathbf{qRel}
is typically denoted by
\times
, as it is the quantum generalization of the cartesian product of sets. Then on objects, we have
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Q}\{X\otimes Y:X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X},Y\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}\}
, whereas on morphisms
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{W}
and
S:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
, we have
(R\times S)(X\otimes Y,W\otimes Z)=\mathrm{span}\{r\otimes s:r\in R(X,W),s\in S(Y,Z)\}
for each
X\otimes Y\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
and
W\otimes Z\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}
.

Furthermore, the monoidal unit of
\mathbf{qRel}
is the quantum set
\mathbf{1}:=\mathcal{Q}\{\mathbb{C}\}
. A scalar
R:\mathbf{1}\to\mathbf{1}
is determined by the subspace
R(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})\subseteq B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})
, and since
B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})
is one-dimensional,
R(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})
can only be either one dimensional or zero dimensional. Hence there are precisely two scalars.

Even though
\mathbf{qRel}
shares many similarities with
\mathbf{Rel}
, the following two results reflect some differences.
In particular,
\mathbf{Rel}
is the prime example of an
allegory

[
11
]
[
19
, A.3.2]
. An allegory is in particular an order-enriched dagger category, hence we can formulate the concept of an internal map in an allegory as a morphism
f:X\to Y
for which
f^{\dagger}\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ f^{\dagger}\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}
. In fact, it is sufficient to define internal maps in allegories to be morphisms
f:X\to Y
that have an
upper adjoint
, i.e., there is some
g:Y\to X
such that
g\circ f\geq\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ g\leq\mathrm{id}_{Y}

[
19
, Lemma A.3.2.3]
. In particular, this means that any invertible morphism in an allegory is a dagger isomorphism. We use this fact to show that
\mathbf{qRel}
is not an allegory.

Lemma D.193
.

There is an invertible relation
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
in
\mathbf{qRel}
that is not a dagger isomorphism, hence
\mathbf{qRel}
is not an allegory.

Proof.

Let
H
be any finite-dimensional Hilbert space and let
a:H\to H
be an invertible linear map that is not a unitary map. For instance, let
H=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and let
a=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. Hence,
a^{\dagger}\neq a^{-1}
. Let
V,W\subseteq B(H)
be given by
V=\mathbb{C}a
and
W=\mathbb{C}a^{-1}
. Then
V\cdot W=\mathbb{C}1_{H}
and
W\cdot V=\mathbb{C}1_{H}
, so
W
is the upper adjoint of
V
in
\mathbf{FdOS}(H,H)
, but
V^{\dagger}=\mathbb{C}a^{\dagger}\neq W
. Now, let
\mathcal{X}
to be the quantum set with single atom
H
, let
R,S:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
be defined by
R(H,H)=V
and
S(H,H)=W
. Then it follows
S
is the inverse of
R
, but
S\neq R^{\dagger}
.
∎

D.2
Zero-monos

Proposition D.194
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a quantum set. Then
R:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbf{1}
is a zero-mono if and only if
R=\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}
.

Proof.

We first show that
\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}
is a zero-mono. Let
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a nonzero relation. This means that there is some
X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}
and some
Y\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}
for which there is a nonzero
r\in R(X,Y)
, which implies that
r(x)\neq 0
for some
x\in X
. Write
y=r(x)
. Let
\hat{y}=\langle y,-\rangle:Y\to\mathbb{C}
, which is an element of
B(Y,\mathbb{C})
. Then
\hat{y}(r(x))=\langle y,y\rangle\neq 0
, since
y\neq 0
. Thus
\hat{y}r\neq 0
, hence
0\neq B(Y,\mathbb{C})\cdot R(X,Y)\leq\bigvee_{Y^{\prime}\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}}B(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{C})\cdot R(X,Y^{\prime})=\bigvee_{Y^{\prime}\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}}\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{C})\cdot R(X,Y^{\prime})=(\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}\circ R)(X,\mathbb{C})
, which shows that
\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}\circ R\neq 0_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{1}}
. By contraposition, we now obtain that
\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}\circ R=0_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{1}}
implies
R=0_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}
, so
\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}
is a zero-mono.

For the converse, assume that
S:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbf{1}
is not equal to
\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}
. This means that there is some
Y\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}
such that
S(Y,\mathbb{C})
is a proper subspace of
\top_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbf{1}}(Y,\mathbb{C})=B(Y,\mathbb{C})
. Hence, there is some nonzero functional
\varphi:Y\to\mathbb{C}
that is orthogonal to all functionals in
S(Y,\mathbb{C})
. The Riesz representation theorem states that the map
y\mapsto\langle y,-\rangle
is an antilinear bijective isometry
Y\to B(Y,\mathbb{C})
. Hence, for any functional
\psi:Y\to\mathbb{C}
, the Riesz representation theorem assures the existence of a unique
y_{\psi}\in Y
such that
\psi=\langle y_{\psi},-\rangle
.
Write
y=y_{\varphi}
. Note that
y\neq 0
, for
\varphi
is nonzero. Let
\psi\in S(Y,\mathbb{C})
. Since
\psi\perp\varphi
, it follows from the Riesz representation theorem that
0=\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle y_{\psi},y\rangle=\psi(y)
.As a consequence, we have
\psi(y)=\langle y_{\psi},y_{\varphi}\rangle=\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle=0
.
Let
\check{y}:\mathbb{C}\to Y
be function
\lambda\mapsto\lambda y
. Then for each
\psi\in S(Y,\mathbb{C})
and each
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, we have
\psi(\check{y}(\lambda)=\psi(\lambda y)=\lambda\psi(y)=0
, hence
\psi\hat{y}=0
for each
\psi\in S(Y,\mathbb{C})
. It follows that
S(Y,\mathbb{C})\cdot\mathbb{C}\hat{y}=0
.

Let
R:\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{Y}
be given by

R(\mathbb{C},Y^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\check{y},&Y^{\prime}=Y,\\
0,&Y^{\prime}\neq Y,\end{cases}

for each
Y^{\prime}\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}
. Clearly,
R
is nonzero by construction. Moreover,

(S\circ R)(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})=\bigvee_{Y^{\prime}\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}}S(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{C})\cdot R(\mathbb{C},Y^{\prime})=S(Y,\mathbb{C})\cdot R(\mathbb{C},Y)=S(Y,\mathbb{C})\cdot\mathbb{C}\check{y}=0.

Thus,
S\circ R=0_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}}
, but
R\neq 0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{Y}}
, showing that
S
is not a zero-mono.
∎

Proposition D.195
.

A relation
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
\mathbf{qRel}
is a zero-mono if and only if
R\circ F=0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{Y}}
implies
F=0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{X}}
for each
F:\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

If
R
is a zero-mono, then it follows by definition that
R\circ F=0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{Y}}
implies
F=0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{X}}
for each
F:\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{X}
.

For the converse, assume that
R\circ F=0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{Y}}
implies
F=0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{X}}
for each
F:\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{X}
. Let
S:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}
be a nonzero relation. In order to show that
R
is a zero-mono, we have to show that
R\circ S\neq 0_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{Y}}
. Since
S
is nonzero, there is some
Z\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Z}
and some
X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}
such that
S(Z,X)
is nonzero, which, in turn, implies the existence of some
s\in S(Z,X)
and some
z\in Z
such that
s(z)\neq 0
.
Let
x=s(z)
, and let
\check{x}:\mathbb{C}\to X
be the function
\lambda\mapsto\lambda x
. Let
F:\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{X}
be given by

F(\mathbb{C},X^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\check{x},&X^{\prime}=X,\\
0,&X^{\prime}\neq X.\end{cases}

Then
F
is nonzero, so also
R\circ F
must be nonzero by assumption in contraposition. Hence, there is some
Y\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{Y}
such that
(R\circ F)(\mathbb{C},Y)\neq 0
. That is,

0\neq(R\circ F)(\mathbb{C},Y)=\bigvee_{X^{\prime}\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}}R(X^{\prime},Y)\cdot F(\mathbb{C},X^{\prime})=R(X,Y)\cdot F(\mathbb{C},X)=R(X,Y)\check{x}.

Thus, there is some
r\in R(X,Y)
such that
r\circ\check{x}\neq 0
, implying that
\lambda r(x)=r(\lambda x)=(r\circ\check{x})(\lambda)\neq 0
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. Thus, we must have
r(x)\neq 0
for some
r\in R(X,Y)
.
Now,
(R\circ S)(Z,Y)=\bigvee_{X^{\prime}\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}}R(X^{\prime},Y)\cdot S(Z,X^{\prime})\geq R(X,Y)\cdot S(Z,X)
, which contains
r\circ s
, which must be nonzero since
(r\circ s)(z)=r(x)\neq 0
. So, indeed
R\circ S\neq 0_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{Y}}
.∎

D.3
Zero-monic PERs

Partial equivalence relations (PERs) in
\mathbf{Rel}
have the following property:

Lemma D.196
.

Let
p:X\to X
be a zero-monic PER in
\mathbf{Rel}
. Then
p
is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

We only need to show that
p
is reflexive, so let
x\in X
, and let
\check{x}:1\to X
be the relation specified by the subset
\check{x}=\{(*,x)\}
of
1\times X
. Since
\check{x}
is nonzero,
p\circ\check{x}
must be nonzero, so there is some
y\in X
such that
(*,y)\in p\circ\check{x}
. This, in turn, implies the existence of some
z\in X
such that
(*,z)\in\check{x}
and
(z,y)\in p
. Since we have
\check{x}=\{(*,x)\}
, we must have
z=x
, whence
(x,y)\in p
for some
y
. Now,
p^{\dagger}=p
, whence
(y,x)\in p
. Since both
(x,y)\in p
and
(y,x)\in p
, it follows that
(x,x)\in p\circ p\leq p
. So
(x,x)\in p
for each
x\in X
, which means that
\mathrm{id}_{X}\leq p
.
∎

In
\mathbf{qRel}
, PERs satisfy the same property. We first need a lemma.

Lemma D.197
.

Let
H
be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and let
A\subseteq B(H)
be a C*-subalgebra that acts on
H
in a nondegenerate way, i.e., for each nonzero
h\in H
, there is some
a\in A
such that
ah\neq 0
. Then
1_{H}\in A
.

Proof.

Since
A
is a C*-subalgebra of a finite-dimensional C*-algebra, it must be finite-dimensional itself, hence it should contain a unit element
e

[
38
, Lemma 11.1]
. We will show that
e=1_{H}
.

Since
A
acts in a nondegenerate way on
H
, we have that the span of
\{ah:a\in A,h\in H\}
equals
H

[
38
, Proposition 9.2]
. Hence, for each
h\in H
, there are
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A
and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\in H
such that
h=a_{1}h_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}h_{n}
. Then
eh=e(a_{1}h_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}h_{n})=(ea_{1})h_{1}+\ldots+(ea_{n})h_{n}=a_{1}h_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}h_{n}=h
. Thus
e
is indeed the identity
1_{H}
on
H
.
∎

Proposition D.198
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a quantum set and let
P:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
be a
partial equivalence relation (PER)
, i.e., a symmetric and transitive relation, so
P^{\dagger}=P
and
P\circ P\leq P
. If
P
is a zero-mono in
\mathbf{qRel}
, then
P\geq I_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

Since
I_{\mathcal{X}}(X,X^{\prime})=0
for distinct atoms
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}
, we only need to show that
I_{\mathcal{X}}(X,X)\leq P(X,X)
for each
X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}
. That is,
\mathbb{C}1_{X}\leq P(X,X)
, which is equivalent to
1_{X}\in P(X,X)
. Let
X\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}
. Then
P(X,X)^{\dagger}=P^{\dagger}(X,X)=P(X,X)
, so
P(X,X)
is a self-adjoint subspace of
B(X)
. Furthermore, let
a,b\in P(X,X)
. Then
ab\in P(X,X)\cdot P(X,X)\leq\bigvee_{Y\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}}P(Y,X)\cdot P(X,Y)=(P\circ P)(X,X)\leq P(X,X)
, so
P(X,X)
is a self-adjoint subalgebra of
B(X)
.

Let
x\in X
be nonzero, and let
\check{x}:\mathbb{C}\to X
be the map
\lambda\mapsto\lambda x
. Define
R:\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{X}
by

R(\mathbb{C},Y)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\check{x},&Y=X,\\
0,&Y\neq 0.\end{cases}

Then
R
is nonzero, hence since
P
is a zero-mono, we must have
P\circ R\neq 0_{\mathbf{1},\mathcal{X}}
. Using Lemma
2.57
, we obtain
0_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}}=R^{\dagger}\circ P^{\dagger}\circ P\circ R=R^{\dagger}\circ P\circ P\circ R=R^{\dagger}\circ P\circ R
. Thus,

0\neq(R^{\dagger}\circ P\circ R)(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})=\bigvee_{Y,Z\text{\raisebox{1.2pt}{\,$\propto$\,}}\mathcal{X}}R^{\dagger}(Z,\mathbb{C})\cdot P(Y,Z)\cdot R(\mathbb{C},Y)=R^{\dagger}(X,\mathbb{C})\cdot P(X,X)\cdot R(X,\mathbb{C}).

In particular, we must have
P(X,X)\check{x}=P(X,X)\cdot R(X,\mathbb{C})\neq 0
, i.e., there is some
a\in P(X,X)
such that
a\check{x}\neq 0
, which means that
\lambda ax=a(\lambda x)=(a\circ\check{x})(\lambda)\neq 0
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, which is only possible if
ax\neq 0
.
Hence, we have shown that
P(X,X)
is a C*-subalgebra of
B(X)
that acts in a nondegenerate way on
X
, hence
1_{X}\in P(X,X)
by Lemma
D.197
. We conclude that
I_{\mathcal{X}}\leq P
.
∎

D.4
Dagger kernels

Theorem D.199
.

\mathbf{qRel}
has dagger kernels.

Proof.

We will use the fact that
\mathbf{qRel}
can be identified with a full dagger subcategory of
\mathbf{WRel}

[
31
, Propositions A.2.1 & A.2.2]
whose objects are precisely the hereditarily atomic von Neumann algebras. Then the statement follows immediately from Proposition
C.191
, which states that
\mathbf{WRel}
has dagger kernels, and that a von Neumann algebra
R
is hereditarily atomic if it is the kernel of a quantum relation
V:M\to N
with
M
hereditarily atomic.
∎

We briefly sketch how dagger kernels can be constructed in
\mathbf{qRel}
without reference to
\mathbf{WRel}
.
Firstly, let
R:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a relation between quantum sets. We define
P_{R}(X):=\bigcap\{\ker r:r\in R(X,Y),Y\in\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{Y})\}
for each
X\in\mathrm{At}(X)
. In the language of
[
29
, Appendix B]
,
P_{R}
is a
predicate
on
\mathcal{X}
, i.e., a function that assigns a subspace of each atom of
\mathcal{X}
. For each
X\in\mathrm{At}(X)
, let
e_{X}:P_{R}(X)\to X
be the inclusion. Now, we define
\mathcal{K}_{R}:=\mathcal{Q}\{P_{R}(X):X\in\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{X})\}
. Then
E:\mathcal{K}_{R}\to\mathcal{X}
defined by
E(P_{R}(X),X^{\prime})=\mathbb{C}\delta_{X,X^{\prime}}e_{X}
is a dagger mono that is the dagger kernel of
R
.
